# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q01

## title
Cell membrane of the prokaryotic cells differs from eukaryotic cells in:

## question
Cell membrane of the prokaryotic cells differs from eukaryotic cells in:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Composed of lipopolysaccharide

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Lipopolysaccharide is a component of the Gram-negative outer membrane, not of the cytoplasmic membrane that every bacterium (Gram-positive and Gram-negative alike) shares -- this option picks the student who has mixed up the two different membranes a bacterium can have.

## answer_b
Made of glycoprotein

## explanation_b
Incorrect. The bacterial cytoplasmic membrane is a phospholipid bilayer with embedded proteins, not a glycoprotein structure; this distractor invents a composition that is not the tested distinguishing feature.

## answer_c
Made purely of phospholipids

## explanation_c
Incorrect. "Purely" phospholipids is too strong: the membrane also carries embedded membrane proteins, and more importantly this option fails to name the actual tested difference from a eukaryotic membrane, which is sterol content.

## answer_d
Lacks sterol

## explanation_d
Correct. Unlike a eukaryotic cell membrane, the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane contains no sterol such as cholesterol, with the single exception of Mycoplasma, whose membrane does contain cholesterol scavenged from the host. This absence of sterol is the classic distinguishing feature examiners use to separate a prokaryotic membrane from a eukaryotic one. The point to remember is the exception as much as the rule: seeing "Mycoplasma" in a later question should immediately flag that the usual sterol-free rule does not apply to it.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q02

## title
Concerning prokaryotic bacterial cells, which of the following is correct?

## question
Concerning prokaryotic bacterial cells, which of the following is correct?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
It has a prominent nucleolus

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A nucleolus is a eukaryotic nuclear feature; a prokaryotic bacterial cell has no true nucleus and therefore no nucleolus at all -- this distractor picks the student who has not yet separated prokaryotic from eukaryotic cell structure.

## answer_b
70S ribosomes are responsible for protein synthesis

## explanation_b
Correct. Prokaryotic bacterial cells use 70S ribosomes -- built from a 30S and a 50S subunit -- as the site of protein synthesis, in contrast to the 80S ribosomes of the eukaryotic cytoplasm. This size difference is not just a labelling detail: it is the molecular basis for the selective toxicity of aminoglycoside, macrolide and tetracycline antibiotics, which can attack the bacterial ribosome while sparing the human host's own 80S ribosomes. Holding onto the number (70S, not 80S) is what lets a student correctly answer any later question about antibiotic mechanisms acting on protein synthesis.

## answer_c
Its DNA is coiled around histone proteins

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Histone-coiled DNA is a eukaryotic feature; bacterial (prokaryotic) DNA is not organised around histones in the same way, so this option again describes a eukaryotic cell, not a prokaryotic one.

## answer_d
It has a prominent rough endoplasmic reticulum

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Rough endoplasmic reticulum is a eukaryotic membranous organelle; a prokaryotic bacterial cell has no internal membrane-bound organelles of this kind at all.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q03

## title
Mycoplasma differs from the rest of prokaryotic cells in:

## question
Mycoplasma differs from the rest of prokaryotic cells in:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Using mitochondria as a site for cellular respiration

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Mycoplasma is still a prokaryote and has no mitochondria at all; this option describes a eukaryotic feature and would make Mycoplasma sound more complex than it actually is.

## answer_b
Utilising 80S ribosomes for protein synthesis

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Mycoplasma still uses 70S ribosomes like every other bacterium; only its membrane composition is unusual, not its ribosome.

## answer_c
Having cholesterol in its cell membrane

## explanation_c
Correct. Mycoplasma is the one bacterial genus whose cytoplasmic membrane contains cholesterol, a sterol scavenged from the host cell. This is functionally important: because Mycoplasma is genetically incapable of making a peptidoglycan cell wall, the sterol-stiffened membrane is what gives the cell enough mechanical stability to survive without one. In every other respect -- no mitochondria, still 70S ribosomes -- Mycoplasma behaves exactly like an ordinary prokaryote, which is what makes the sterol-containing membrane the one genuinely distinguishing feature to remember.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This is only right if every individual option is right, and two of the three (mitochondrial respiration, 80S ribosomes) are eukaryotic features that do not apply to Mycoplasma; picking "all of the above" here rewards not checking each option on its own.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q04

## title
Concerning Gram-negative bacteria cell wall, all of the following is true except:

## question
Concerning Gram-negative bacteria cell wall, all of the following is true except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
It has a thin peptidoglycan layer

## explanation_a
This is true, not the exception: the Gram-negative wall does have a thin layer of peptidoglycan, thinner than the thick peptidoglycan of a Gram-positive wall, so it does not answer an "except" question.

## answer_b
Has an outer LPS (lipopolysaccharide) membrane

## explanation_b
This is true, not the exception: the outer membrane carrying lipopolysaccharide is a defining feature of the Gram-negative envelope, absent from Gram-positive bacteria.

## answer_c
Contains teichoic acid

## explanation_c
Correct -- this is the false statement, and therefore the answer to an "except" question. Teichoic acid (and its membrane-anchored form, lipoteichoic acid) is a Gram-positive wall component; it is not part of the Gram-negative cell wall at all. A student who forgets that teichoic acid is Gram-positive-specific will wrongly treat it as a universal wall component and pick the wrong option on any Gram-positive-versus-negative comparison question. Keeping the two envelopes' component lists strictly separate -- teichoic/lipoteichoic acid on the Gram-positive side, outer membrane/LPS/periplasmic space on the Gram-negative side -- is exactly what this question is testing.

## answer_d
Has proteins in the periplasmic space

## explanation_d
This is true, not the exception: the periplasmic space, present between the cytoplasmic membrane and the outer membrane only in Gram-negative bacteria, does hold transport and hydrolytic proteins.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q05

## title
Mycoplasma differs from L-forms in:

## question
Mycoplasma differs from L-forms in:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
It has a thicker cell wall

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Mycoplasma has no cell wall at all, thick or thin, so this option is simply false rather than a subtle mistake about degree.

## answer_b
It permanently lacks a cell wall

## explanation_b
Correct. Mycoplasma is genetically incapable of synthesising peptidoglycan and so permanently lacks a cell wall as part of its normal genotype. This is the key contrast with an L-form, which is a cell-wall-deficient variant of an otherwise wall-forming species produced only when a chemical reagent, an antibiotic such as a beta-lactam, or the enzyme lysozyme removes the wall from outside. Because an L-form's underlying species still carries the genes for a wall, its wall-deficient state is, in principle, an induced and reversible one, unlike Mycoplasma's permanent condition.

## answer_c
It doesn't have a cell wall because of a chemical reagent or lysozyme

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This describes exactly how an L-form loses its wall, not how Mycoplasma does -- it reverses the comparison the question is asking for.

## answer_d
It has teichoic acid in its cell wall

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Mycoplasma has no cell wall, so it cannot have any wall component, teichoic acid included; this distractor also incorrectly implies teichoic acid is somehow Mycoplasma-specific.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q06

## title
Which of the following structures is considered essential to the bacteria?

## question
Which of the following structures is considered essential to the bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Ordinary pili

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Ordinary pili are useful for adhesion but are not classed as an essential structure in the way this question intends -- many bacteria lack pili altogether and still survive.

## answer_b
Sex pili

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Sex pili are used for conjugation and are present only in some bacteria carrying a fertility factor; they are not essential to bacterial survival in general.

## answer_c
Mesosomes

## explanation_c
Correct. Mesosomes -- invaginations of the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane -- are described here as an essential structure and the main site of cytochrome (respiratory-chain) enzymes. Because a mesosome is simply folded cytoplasmic membrane, it contains no sterol, and because it is a prokaryote-specific structure it is not present in eukaryotic cells such as fungi, which instead have mitochondria for that respiratory role. This is the local exam's own classical teaching point; it is recorded as tested content, not as an independently verified statement about modern microbiology's view of mesosomes.

## answer_d
Slime layer

## explanation_d
Incorrect. The slime layer is a loosely organised, often optional secretion outside the cell wall in some species; it is not the structure classically described as essential here.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q07

## title
The structure which is responsible for the motility of the bacteria consists of a protein:

## question
The structure which is responsible for the motility of the bacteria consists of a protein:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Pilin

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Pilin is the subunit protein of a pilus, which mediates adhesion (ordinary pili) or DNA transfer (sex pili), not motility -- this is the classic distractor for this question, catching a student who confuses the two very similar-sounding appendages.

## answer_b
Hamartin

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Hamartin is a human tumour-suppressor protein product (of the TSC1 gene), entirely unrelated to bacterial structure; it is included here as an implausible-sounding distractor with no bacteriological role at all.

## answer_c
Flagellin

## explanation_c
Correct. The bacterial flagellum, the structure responsible for motility, is a helical filament built from repeating subunits of the protein flagellin, rotated by a basal-body motor to drive the cell through liquid. Flagellin is distinct from pilin, the pilus subunit, even though the names and the general "bacterial surface filament" idea can blur together under exam pressure. Keeping "flagellin -- motility" and "pilin -- adhesion/gene transfer" as a fixed pair is what prevents that mix-up.

## answer_d
Hemagglutinin

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Hemagglutinin is a surface protein that mediates attachment (well known from influenza virus, and also present on some bacteria), not motility; it is a plausible-sounding "attachment protein" distractor rather than the true motility protein.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q08

## title
Which of the following is a function of the capsule?

## question
Which of the following is a function of the capsule?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Vaccine preparation

## explanation_a
This is true, but it is only one of the capsule's three roles here; picking it alone under-answers a question that is asking about all of its listed functions together.

## answer_b
Identification of bacteria

## explanation_b
This is true, but again only one role: capsular antigenic specificity is used to identify and serotype organisms, but the question wants every function that is genuinely correct, not just one.

## answer_c
Major virulence factor

## explanation_c
This is true, but incomplete on its own: the capsule resists phagocytosis and so is a major virulence factor, but it is not the capsule's only listed function in this question.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Correct. The bacterial capsule genuinely serves all three roles at once: it is a major virulence factor because it resists phagocytosis, its antigenic specificity is used to identify and serotype bacteria, and that same antigenic specificity is exploited to make capsular-polysaccharide vaccines such as those against pneumococcus, meningococcus and Haemophilus influenzae type b. A student who picks only one of A, B or C has correctly identified a true fact but has not recognised that a capsule can do more than one job simultaneously -- which is exactly what an "all of the above" option is built to test.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q09

## title
The rigidity of the bacterial cell is attributed to which of the following components?

## question
The rigidity of the bacterial cell is attributed to which of the following components?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Lipoprotein

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Lipoprotein (such as Braun's lipoprotein in Gram-negative bacteria) anchors the outer membrane to the peptidoglycan layer; it is a connector, not the source of the wall's rigidity itself.

## answer_b
Galactomannan

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Galactomannan is a polysaccharide associated with the fungal cell wall (notably Aspergillus), not with bacterial cell-wall rigidity; this distractor imports a fungal term into a bacteriology question.

## answer_c
Polysaccharide capsule

## explanation_c
Incorrect. The capsule is a separate, often loosely organised outer layer whose role is virulence, identification and antigenicity, not structural rigidity; a bacterium's rigidity comes from the wall beneath the capsule, not the capsule itself.

## answer_d
Peptidoglycan

## explanation_d
Correct. Peptidoglycan (murein) is a mesh-like polymer of alternating N-acetylglucosamine and N-acetylmuramic acid chains, cross-linked by short peptides, and it is this cross-linked meshwork that gives the bacterial cell wall its structural rigidity and shape. That rigidity is also what lets the wall resist osmotic lysis when the cell is in a hypotonic environment. Peptidoglycan is present, in different thicknesses, in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative walls, which is why it is the rigidity answer regardless of Gram stain.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q10

## title
Which of the following is present in Gram-positive, but not Gram-negative bacteria?

## question
Which of the following is present in Gram-positive, but not Gram-negative bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Outer membrane

## explanation_a
Incorrect and reversed: the outer membrane is a Gram-negative-specific feature, absent from the Gram-positive envelope, which has no second membrane at all.

## answer_b
Lipopolysaccharide

## explanation_b
Incorrect and reversed: lipopolysaccharide is carried on the Gram-negative outer membrane; Gram-positive bacteria have no LPS.

## answer_c
Periplasmic space

## explanation_c
Incorrect and reversed: the periplasmic space sits between the two membranes of a Gram-negative cell; a Gram-positive cell has no outer membrane and therefore no equivalent periplasmic space.

## answer_d
Teichoic acid

## explanation_d
Correct. Teichoic acid is threaded through the thick peptidoglycan wall of Gram-positive bacteria and is genuinely absent from the Gram-negative wall. This is the reciprocal fact to the previous three options: outer membrane, LPS and periplasmic space are all Gram-negative-only, while teichoic acid (and its membrane-anchored relative, lipoteichoic acid) is Gram-positive-only. Learning both halves of this contrast as one paired fact, rather than memorising each envelope's parts separately, is what prevents the reversal error the three distractors are built to catch.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q11

## title
Which of the following about the outer membrane layer isn't true?

## question
Which of the following about the outer membrane layer isn't true?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Its lipid A component is toxic

## explanation_a
This is true, not the exception: lipid A, the lipid portion of lipopolysaccharide, is the toxic component of the Gram-negative outer membrane, also called endotoxin.

## answer_b
Porins in the outer membrane allow passage of amino acids

## explanation_b
This is true, not the exception, in the narrower technical sense: porin channels allow passage of small hydrophilic solutes generally, and this option is graded as a true statement about porins here (the false statement the question is built around is option C, not this one).

## answer_c
Responsible for osmotic protection

## explanation_c
Correct -- this is the false statement, and therefore the answer to an "isn't true" question. Osmotic protection of the bacterial cell is generally attributed to the rigid peptidoglycan layer, not primarily to the outer membrane, whose main roles are barrier function (via LPS and porins) rather than resisting osmotic lysis. A student who assumes every envelope layer contributes equally to every protective function will miss that this specific role belongs to the wall, not the outer membrane. Keeping each envelope layer's specific job separate -- rigidity and osmotic protection to peptidoglycan, permeability barrier and endotoxin to the outer membrane -- is what this question is testing.

## answer_d
Present only on Gram-negative bacteria

## explanation_d
This is true, not the exception: the outer membrane is a defining feature of the Gram-negative envelope and has no equivalent in Gram-positive bacteria.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q12

## title
Which of the following cell wall deficient bacteria are able to replicate?

## question
Which of the following cell wall deficient bacteria are able to replicate?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
L-forms

## explanation_a
This is true, but incomplete: L-forms genuinely can replicate despite lacking a cell wall, but the question is asking for every organism on the list that can, not just one.

## answer_b
Protoplast

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A protoplast is an artificially, experimentally wall-stripped cell, typically osmotically fragile, and is not treated as a stable, self-replicating clinical form the way a naturally occurring L-form or Mycoplasma is.

## answer_c
Mycoplasma

## explanation_c
This is true, but incomplete: Mycoplasma genuinely can replicate despite permanently lacking a cell wall, but again the question wants every correct organism, not just one.

## answer_d
A and C (L-forms and Mycoplasma)

## explanation_d
Correct. Both L-forms (induced cell-wall-deficient variants of otherwise wall-forming bacteria) and Mycoplasma (a genus that never forms a cell wall) are capable of ongoing replication despite the absence of peptidoglycan. A protoplast, by contrast, is an artificially wall-stripped cell that is not treated here as a stable, replicating clinical form, which is exactly why it is excluded from the correct answer. The distinction to hold is: naturally occurring, replication-competent wall-deficient forms (L-forms, Mycoplasma) versus an experimentally stripped, fragile protoplast.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q13

## title
Which of the following statements is true regarding the cytoplasmic membrane?

## question
Which of the following statements is true regarding the cytoplasmic membrane?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Present outside the cell wall

## explanation_a
Incorrect. The cytoplasmic membrane is the innermost boundary of the bacterial cell, lying inside the cell wall, not outside it; this option reverses the actual anatomical arrangement.

## answer_b
Lacks sterol in Mycoplasma

## explanation_b
Incorrect and reversed: Mycoplasma is the one bacterium whose membrane does contain sterol (cholesterol); every other bacterium's membrane lacks it.

## answer_c
Osmotically resistant

## explanation_c
Incorrect, as a general statement about the cytoplasmic membrane on its own: the membrane's resistance to osmotic stress in an intact cell is generally attributed to the surrounding rigid cell wall, not to the cytoplasmic membrane acting independently.

## answer_d
Plays a role in toxin excretion

## explanation_d
Correct. The bacterial cytoplasmic membrane plays a role in toxin excretion: many bacterial exotoxins are exported across it on their way out of the cell. This function sits alongside the membrane's more obvious roles in transport and energy generation, but toxin excretion is the true statement among the four options. Remembering that the membrane is functionally active in secretion, not merely a passive inner boundary, is the point being tested here.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q14

## title
Which of the followings isn't true about mesosomes?

## question
Which of the followings isn't true about mesosomes?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Invaginations from the cytoplasmic membrane

## explanation_a
This is true, not the exception: a mesosome is, by definition, an invagination of the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane.

## answer_b
Doesn't contain sterol

## explanation_b
This is true, not the exception: because a mesosome is derived from the cytoplasmic membrane, which itself lacks sterol in essentially all bacteria, the mesosome likewise contains no sterol.

## answer_c
Are the main site of cytochrome enzymes and respiration

## explanation_c
This is true, not the exception: mesosomes are classically described as the main site of cytochrome (respiratory-chain) enzymes in the bacterial cell.

## answer_d
Present in fungi

## explanation_d
Correct -- this is the false statement, and therefore the answer to an "isn't true" question. Mesosomes are a prokaryote-specific structure; a eukaryotic fungal cell has mitochondria to carry out the respiratory role that a mesosome performs in bacteria, not mesosomes of its own. A student who blurs the line between the two kingdoms studied in the same microbiology course, and assumes any respiratory structure could appear in either, will wrongly accept this option as true.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q15

## title
What is the main function of ordinary pili?

## question
What is the main function of ordinary pili?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Motility

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Motility is the flagellum's job, driven by the rotating protein flagellin; a pilus is a rigid, non-rotating appendage and does not propel the cell.

## answer_b
Adhesion

## explanation_b
Correct. The main function of ordinary (common) pili is adhesion of the bacterium to host cells or surfaces. This is distinct from the role of sex pili, a longer, separate type of pilus used specifically for conjugation -- the transfer of DNA between bacteria -- not for adhesion. Keeping "ordinary pili adhere, sex pili transfer DNA" as one fixed pairing is what prevents mixing up the two pilus types under exam pressure.

## answer_c
Gene transfer

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Gene transfer by conjugation is the function of sex pili specifically, not ordinary pili; this distractor picks the student who has conflated the two pilus types.

## answer_d
Protection from phagocytosis

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Protection from phagocytosis is a role of the capsule, not of pili; this distractor imports a different structure's function into a question about pili.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q16

## title
Most of the pathogenic bacteria are:

## question
Most of the pathogenic bacteria are:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Heterotrophic, obligate aerobic, capnophilic, mesophilic and halophilic

## explanation_a
Incorrect. This combines several requirements that do not describe the typical pathogen: most pathogens are facultatively, not obligately, aerobic, and true capnophilia and halophilia are the exception, not the general rule.

## answer_b
Autotrophic, facultatively anaerobic, psychrophilic and require 0.03% CO2 concentration

## explanation_b
Incorrect. No human pathogen is autotrophic -- autotrophs use CO2 as their carbon source and do not cause disease -- and psychrophilic (cold-loving) is also wrong for organisms adapted to human body temperature.

## answer_c
Heterotrophic, facultatively anaerobic, mesophilic and capnophilic

## explanation_c
Incorrect, on the last term only: this option is right about heterotrophic, facultatively anaerobic and mesophilic, but wrong to call most pathogens capnophilic (needing an enriched CO2 atmosphere) rather than simply tolerant of ordinary atmospheric CO2.

## answer_d
Heterotrophic, facultatively anaerobic, mesophilic and require 0.03% CO2 concentration

## explanation_d
Correct. The typical growth profile of pathogenic bacteria combines being heterotrophic (using organic carbon sources), facultatively anaerobic (able to grow with or without oxygen), mesophilic (optimal growth around body temperature) and undemanding for CO2, needing only about the 0.03 percent concentration present in ordinary air rather than an enriched atmosphere. This profile is what makes most pathogens easy to culture on standard media in a standard incubator, unlike true capnophiles such as Neisseria or Brucella, which specifically need extra CO2. The 0.03% figure is the detail this question is really testing, since it is what separates "most pathogens" from the smaller group of genuine capnophiles.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q17

## title
Exacting bacteria are:

## question
Exacting bacteria are:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Heterotrophic, requiring a non-organic (inorganic) source of nitrogen

## explanation_a
Incorrect. An exacting organism needs an organic nitrogen source, not an inorganic one; this option gets the nitrogen requirement backwards.

## answer_b
Autotrophic bacteria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Exacting bacteria are heterotrophic, not autotrophic -- autotrophs use CO2 as their carbon source and have no special organic-nitrogen requirement at all.

## answer_c
Heterotrophic bacteria which get their carbon from CO2

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This is self-contradictory: an organism that fixes its carbon from CO2 is autotrophic, not heterotrophic; a genuinely heterotrophic organism needs a preformed organic carbon source instead.

## answer_d
Heterotrophic bacteria which require organic sources for both carbon and nitrogen

## explanation_d
Correct. Exacting (fastidious) bacteria are heterotrophic organisms with the additional, more demanding requirement of an organic source for both carbon and nitrogen, rather than being able to use an inorganic nitrogen source or fix carbon from CO2. This combined organic-carbon-and-organic-nitrogen requirement is exactly why exacting bacteria typically need enriched laboratory media to grow, unlike less demanding heterotrophs. The word to hold onto is "both": getting only one of the two elements right (as in options A, B and C) is not enough to describe an exacting organism.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q18

## title
Bacteria which can stand high NaCl concentrations are called:

## question
Bacteria which can stand high NaCl concentrations are called:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Halophilic

## explanation_a
Correct. Bacteria that tolerate high NaCl (salt) concentrations are called halophilic. This is one of a family of environmental-tolerance terms -- alongside capnophilic (CO2-requiring), thermophilic (heat-preferring) and microaerophilic (low-oxygen-preferring) -- and each names a different environmental axis. Learning halophilic specifically as "salt", rather than as a generic "unusual bacterium" label, is what prevents it being confused with the other three terms.

## answer_b
Capnophilic

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Capnophilic describes a requirement for increased CO2, an entirely different environmental axis from salt tolerance.

## answer_c
Thermophilic

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Thermophilic describes a preference for high growth temperature, not salt tolerance.

## answer_d
Microaerophilic

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Microaerophilic describes a preference for only small amounts of oxygen, an oxygen-tolerance term unrelated to salt concentration.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q19

## title
Bacteria which use aerobic respiration require the enzyme:

## question
Bacteria which use aerobic respiration require the enzyme:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Alkaline phosphatase

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Alkaline phosphatase is an enzyme involved in phosphate-ester hydrolysis and has no role in detoxifying the reactive oxygen species generated by aerobic respiration; it is an unrelated enzyme included as a plausible-sounding distractor.

## answer_b
Superoxide dismutase

## explanation_b
This is true, but incomplete on its own: superoxide dismutase does detoxify reactive oxygen species, but catalase can serve the same purpose, which is why the correct answer is "b or c" rather than b alone.

## answer_c
Catalase

## explanation_c
This is true, but incomplete on its own: catalase does detoxify reactive oxygen species (specifically hydrogen peroxide), but superoxide dismutase can also serve this role, which is why the correct answer is "b or c" rather than c alone.

## answer_d
b or c

## explanation_d
Correct. Bacteria that use aerobic respiration generate toxic reactive oxygen species -- superoxide and hydrogen peroxide -- and require catalase and/or superoxide dismutase to detoxify them, so either enzyme (or both) satisfies the requirement. Recognising that a question can accept two different, non-identical enzymes as jointly correct is itself part of what is being tested, alongside the underlying fact that aerobic metabolism cannot proceed safely without at least one ROS-degrading enzyme present. This is the same fact that explains, in the next question, why obligate anaerobes -- lacking both enzymes -- cannot tolerate oxygen at all.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q20

## title
All of the following types of bacteria have a ROS-degradation enzyme except:

## question
All of the following types of bacteria have a ROS-degradation enzyme except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Obligate aerobes

## explanation_a
This is true, not the exception: obligate aerobes require oxygen for respiration and, unsurprisingly, retain catalase and/or superoxide dismutase to cope with the reactive oxygen species that respiration produces.

## answer_b
Obligate anaerobes

## explanation_b
Correct -- this is the exception, and therefore the answer. Obligate anaerobes are, by definition, the one oxygen-tolerance group that lacks ROS-degrading enzymes such as catalase and superoxide dismutase, which is exactly why oxygen exposure is toxic to them rather than merely unnecessary. This is the mechanistic explanation behind the clinical fact that obligate anaerobes must be cultured away from atmospheric oxygen. Every other group on this list -- aerobes, facultative anaerobes, microaerophiles -- retains at least one of these enzymes because each of them is exposed to at least some oxygen during normal growth.

## answer_c
Facultative anaerobes

## explanation_c
This is true, not the exception: facultative anaerobes can grow in the presence of oxygen, so they retain at least one ROS-degrading enzyme to cope with it when it is present.

## answer_d
Microaerophiles

## explanation_d
This is true, not the exception: microaerophiles grow best with small amounts of oxygen, so they too retain at least one ROS-degrading enzyme, just tuned to a lower oxygen tolerance than an obligate aerobe.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q21

## title
Which of the following bacteria can't be isolated on culture media?

## question
Which of the following bacteria can't be isolated on culture media?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Mycobacterium tuberculosis

## explanation_a
Incorrect. M. tuberculosis is notoriously slow-growing, but it is culturable on specialised media (such as Lowenstein-Jensen); slow growth is not the same as being non-cultivable, which is the trap this option sets.

## answer_b
Treponema pallidum

## explanation_b
Correct. Treponema pallidum, the spirochaete that causes syphilis, cannot be isolated or grown on artificial culture media in the clinical laboratory. This is the one organism on this list that is genuinely non-cultivable, which is why its diagnosis relies on dark-field microscopy and serology instead of culture-based identification. The lesson is not "unusual organisms can't be cultured" (M. tuberculosis is unusual but culturable) but specifically that T. pallidum is the exception.

## answer_c
Bacillus anthracis

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Bacillus anthracis grows readily on ordinary culture media; it is not the non-cultivable organism this question is testing for.

## answer_d
Corynebacterium diphtheriae

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Corynebacterium diphtheriae is culturable on selective media (such as tellurite media); it is not the exception the question is looking for.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q22

## title
Both lag and stationary phases are represented by horizontal lines in the growth curve; however, the lag phase is different in:

## question
Both lag and stationary phases are represented by horizontal lines in the growth curve; however, the lag phase is different in:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
No cell division occurs

## explanation_a
Correct. Although both the lag phase and the stationary phase appear as horizontal lines on the bacterial growth curve, they differ mechanistically: during the lag phase, the population is adapting to its new environment and no net cell division occurs at all, whereas during the stationary phase cells are actively dividing at a rate exactly balanced by an equal rate of death. A horizontal line on the curve therefore does not always mean "nothing is happening" -- it can hide balanced activity (stationary phase) or genuinely mean no activity (lag phase), and telling the two apart is exactly what this question is testing. This is also why the lag phase is sometimes called the adaptation phase, a label the stationary phase does not share.

## answer_b
The number of dying cells is equal to the number of generated cells

## explanation_b
Incorrect. This describes the stationary phase, not the lag phase -- it is the definition of the very state the question is asking the lag phase to be distinguished from.

## answer_c
The number of dying cells exceeds the number of formed cells

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This describes the decline (death) phase, a different phase again from both lag and stationary, and not the tested contrast.

## answer_d
The rate of bacterial division is higher

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A higher division rate describes the exponential (log) phase, not the lag phase, which by definition has no net division at all.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q23

## title
Which of the following is true about autotrophic bacteria?

## question
Which of the following is true about autotrophic bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Utilise complex organic substances

## explanation_a
Incorrect and reversed: using complex organic substances is the definition of a heterotroph, not an autotroph, which instead builds its own organic molecules from simple inorganic starting material.

## answer_b
Require CO2 as a source of carbon

## explanation_b
Correct. Autotrophic bacteria synthesise organic molecules using carbon dioxide as their carbon source, rather than requiring preformed organic carbon compounds. This is the defining feature that separates autotrophs from heterotrophs, and it is also why autotrophs are not medically relevant: every parasitic, pathogenic and normal-flora organism a medical student needs to know is heterotrophic, not autotrophic. Recognising "uses CO2 as carbon source" as the autotroph's signature is what lets a student instantly reject an autotroph as a candidate answer in any "which organism causes disease" question.

## answer_c
Require an organic source of nitrogen

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Requiring an organic nitrogen source is a feature of exacting (fastidious) heterotrophic bacteria, not of autotrophs, which are defined by their carbon source, not their nitrogen source.

## answer_d
Cause disease in humans

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Autotrophic bacteria are not human pathogens; every disease-causing bacterium a medical curriculum covers is heterotrophic.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q24

## title
What are the requirements of exacting bacteria?

## question
What are the requirements of exacting bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Inorganic carbon and organic nitrogen

## explanation_a
Incorrect. This gets the carbon requirement backwards -- an exacting organism needs an organic carbon source, not an inorganic one.

## answer_b
Organic carbon and inorganic nitrogen

## explanation_b
Incorrect. This gets the nitrogen requirement backwards -- an exacting organism needs an organic nitrogen source, not an inorganic one.

## answer_c
Organic carbon and organic nitrogen

## explanation_c
Correct. Exacting (fastidious) bacteria are heterotrophs that require an organic source for both carbon and nitrogen, which is precisely why they typically need enriched laboratory media to grow, unlike less demanding organisms that can use an inorganic nitrogen source. Getting only one of the two elements' organic requirement right, as in options A and B, is not enough to satisfy the definition. This is the same fact tested from a different angle in the earlier question about what exacting bacteria "are" -- here the question asks for the requirements directly.

## answer_d
Inorganic carbon and inorganic nitrogen

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This describes neither an exacting heterotroph nor even an ordinary heterotroph; it is the wrong combination on both counts.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q25

## title
Maximal sensitivity to antibiotics occurs in which of the following phases?

## question
Maximal sensitivity to antibiotics occurs in which of the following phases?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Exponential phase

## explanation_a
Correct. Antibiotics that target actively growing or dividing cells, such as beta-lactams acting on cell-wall synthesis, are most effective when bacteria are in the exponential (log) phase, when division and metabolic activity are maximal. This is the mechanistic reason antibiotic timing matters clinically: a population that has not yet reached exponential growth, or one that has plateaued, is comparatively less vulnerable to the same drug. The phrase to hold is "actively dividing cells are the most exposed cells" -- it is division-dependent drug targets, not simply population size, that this question is testing.

## answer_b
Stationary phase

## explanation_b
Incorrect. The stationary phase has a large population but a division rate balanced by an equal death rate, not the maximal active division that makes cells most vulnerable to division-dependent antibiotics.

## answer_c
Lag phase

## explanation_c
Incorrect. During the lag phase there is no cell division at all, so division-dependent antibiotic targets are least, not most, active here.

## answer_d
Decline phase

## explanation_d
Incorrect. The decline (death) phase is characterised by cells dying faster than they are formed, not by maximal active growth, so it is not the phase of greatest antibiotic sensitivity.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q26

## title
Heterotrophic bacteria belong to which of the following?

## question
Heterotrophic bacteria belong to which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Parasitic

## explanation_a
This is true, but incomplete: parasitic organisms are indeed heterotrophic, but the question wants every group that is, not just one.

## answer_b
Pathogenic

## explanation_b
This is true, but incomplete: pathogenic organisms are indeed heterotrophic, but again the question wants every correct group, not just one.

## answer_c
Flora

## explanation_c
This is true, but incomplete: normal flora (commensal) organisms are indeed heterotrophic, but the question again wants the complete set.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Correct. Heterotrophic bacteria require preformed organic carbon and include every parasitic, pathogenic and normal-flora organism relevant to medicine -- in other words, every medically important bacterium is heterotrophic, whichever of these three roles it plays in the body. This is the direct complement to the autotroph fact tested elsewhere in this bank: autotrophs use CO2 as their carbon source and are never pathogens, while heterotrophs cover the entire clinically relevant spectrum from harmless flora to frank pathogens. A student who picks only one of A, B or C has a true fact but has missed that the three categories are not mutually exclusive alternatives here -- they are three examples of the same underlying nutritional class.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q27

## title
Facultative anaerobes can grow in which of the following conditions?

## question
Facultative anaerobes can grow in which of the following conditions?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Complete absence of O2 only

## explanation_a
Incorrect. This describes an obligate anaerobe, which cannot tolerate oxygen at all -- a facultative anaerobe is defined precisely by not being restricted to this one condition.

## answer_b
Presence or absence of O2

## explanation_b
Correct. Facultative anaerobes are bacteria that can grow in the presence or absence of oxygen, switching their metabolism accordingly -- fermentative or anaerobic respiration without oxygen, aerobic respiration when it is available. This metabolic flexibility is broader than that of a microaerophile, which specifically needs a small amount of oxygen and does not thrive at either extreme. Recognising "either condition" as the defining feature is what separates a facultative anaerobe from every other oxygen-tolerance class in this bank.

## answer_c
Small amount of O2 only

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This describes a microaerophile, a narrower and different oxygen-tolerance class from a facultative anaerobe, which tolerates the full range from none to normal atmospheric oxygen.

## answer_d
High amount of CO2

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This names a CO2 requirement, not an oxygen-tolerance condition, and is not the axis a facultative anaerobe is defined by.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q28

## title
What are the bacteria which grow best in the presence of traces of O2?

## question
What are the bacteria which grow best in the presence of traces of O2?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Obligate aerobes

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Obligate aerobes require oxygen and generally grow best at full atmospheric concentration, not merely at trace levels.

## answer_b
Obligate anaerobes

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Obligate anaerobes are killed or fail to grow in the presence of oxygen at all, including trace amounts, so this is the opposite of the tested answer.

## answer_c
Microaerophilics

## explanation_c
Correct. Microaerophiles are the bacteria that grow best with only small (trace) amounts of oxygen, rather than the full atmospheric concentration that suits an obligate aerobe or the complete absence that an obligate anaerobe requires. This narrower tolerance window is what distinguishes a microaerophile from a facultative anaerobe, which tolerates the whole range from no oxygen to normal atmospheric oxygen rather than needing a specific trace amount. Holding "microaerophile = a small, specific amount, not a wide range" is the key discriminator this question is testing.

## answer_d
Facultative anaerobes

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Facultative anaerobes can grow across the whole range from no oxygen to normal atmospheric oxygen; they are not specifically defined by growing best only at trace levels, which is the microaerophile's narrower niche.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q29

## title
What is the main event characterising phase (A) in the corresponding figure of the bacterial growth curve?

## question
What is the main event characterising phase (A) in the corresponding figure of the bacterial growth curve?

## vignette
The accompanying figure shows the standard bacterial growth curve, with phase A as the earliest, flat portion of the curve before the population begins its steep rise.

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Adaptation of the bacteria to the new environment

## explanation_a
Correct. Phase A on the standard bacterial growth curve is the lag phase, and the main event that characterises it is adaptation of the bacteria to the new environment -- synthesising the enzymes and cofactors the new medium requires, without yet dividing. No net cell division occurs during this adaptation window, which is what makes the lag phase a genuine plateau rather than a slow-growth period. Recognising the lag phase from a figure, not only from a verbal description, is exactly what this question adds to the earlier lag-versus-stationary question in this bank.

## answer_b
Germination of spore-forming bacteria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Spore germination is not the defining event of the lag phase for bacteria in general; it is a separate, spore-specific process relevant only to spore-forming genera, and it is not what phase A on a generic growth curve represents.

## answer_c
Increased susceptibility to antibiotics

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Increased antibiotic susceptibility characterises the later exponential phase, when division and metabolic activity are maximal, not the lag phase, where there is no division at all.

## answer_d
Depletion of nutrients

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Nutrient depletion is what drives the transition into the stationary and decline phases, much later in the curve, not the event that defines the earliest lag phase.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q30

## title
Which of the following components characterises the prokaryotic cell and differentiates it from the eukaryotic cell?

## question
Which of the following components characterises the prokaryotic cell and differentiates it from the eukaryotic cell?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
80S ribosomes

## explanation_a
Incorrect. 80S ribosomes are the eukaryotic feature; a prokaryotic cell has 70S ribosomes instead, so this option describes the wrong cell type entirely.

## answer_b
Nuclear membrane

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A nuclear membrane is a defining eukaryotic feature; a prokaryotic cell has no true nucleus and therefore no nuclear membrane, so this again describes the eukaryotic, not the prokaryotic, cell.

## answer_c
Mesosomes

## explanation_c
Correct. Mesosomes -- invaginations of the bacterial cytoplasmic membrane -- are presented in this source as the component that characterises the prokaryotic cell and differentiates it from the eukaryotic cell, being the main site of cytochrome (respiratory-chain) enzymes and absent from eukaryotic cells such as fungi. This is the third time this exact fact is tested in the bank (after questions on mesosomes' essential status and on what is not true about them), reflecting how heavily the local exam weights this one structure. The pattern to hold across all three questions is the same: mesosome = bacterial-only, membrane-derived, respiratory-enzyme-bearing structure.

## answer_d
Histones

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Histones package DNA in the eukaryotic nucleus; prokaryotic DNA is not organised around histones in the same way, so this again names a eukaryotic, not a prokaryotic, feature.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q31

## title
Which of the following components is found in Gram-positive but not Gram-negative bacteria?

## question
Which of the following components is found in Gram-positive but not Gram-negative bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Lipoteichoic acid

## explanation_a
Correct. Lipoteichoic acid, the membrane-anchored relative of wall-bound teichoic acid, is threaded through the thick peptidoglycan wall of Gram-positive bacteria and is genuinely absent from Gram-negative bacteria. This is the same Gram-positive-versus-negative contrast tested earlier in this bank with plain teichoic acid, now asked with its membrane-anchored form instead -- both are correctly grouped under "Gram-positive-only wall components" and should be answered the same way. Neither plasmids, pili nor peptidoglycan distinguishes the two groups the way teichoic and lipoteichoic acid do.

## answer_b
Plasmids

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Plasmids are extrachromosomal DNA elements found in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria; they are not a Gram-stain-specific structural feature at all.

## answer_c
Pili

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Pili occur in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria (though more classically described in Gram-negative organisms); they are not the feature that is specifically Gram-positive-only.

## answer_d
Peptidoglycan

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Peptidoglycan is present in both Gram-positive and Gram-negative cell walls, just in different thicknesses; its mere presence does not distinguish the two groups, which is exactly the trap this option sets.

# Item

## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH123-Q32

## title
Length of the lag period is determined by?

## question
Length of the lag period is determined by?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Type of organism

## explanation_a
This is true, but incomplete: the organism's own physiology genuinely affects lag length, but the question wants every named lag-length determinant, not just one.

## answer_b
Type of media

## explanation_b
This is true, but incomplete: the medium an organism is inoculated into genuinely affects lag length (for example, moving from a rich to a poor medium lengthens it), but again this is only one of two named factors.

## answer_c
Temperature of incubation

## explanation_c
Incorrect: temperature affects growth rate throughout the curve, but it is not one of the two factors that determine lag-period length specifically.

## answer_d
A and B (type of organism and type of media)

## explanation_d
Correct. The length of the lag period is determined by the type of organism being inoculated together with the type of media (and prior conditions) it is inoculated into -- for example, an organism moved from a rich medium to a poor one, or transferred from a different growth phase, shows a longer lag. Naming only one of the two factors, as in options A or B alone, under-answers the question; naming temperature, as in option C, answers a different question about growth rate rather than lag duration specifically. Holding "organism plus medium, not temperature" as the paired answer is what this question is testing.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q01

## title
All of the following are factors contributing in colonization of bacteria except:

## question
All of the following are factors contributing in colonization of bacteria except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Glycocalyx

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true colonization factor). The glycocalyx is a loose polysaccharide meshwork extending from the bacterial surface that promotes adherence to host tissue and to other bacteria, making it one of the genuine colonization factors this question asks you to exclude the odd one out from. Because it directly mediates attachment, it belongs with capsule and fimbriae, not with sex pili. This option is a true statement about colonization, so it is not the exception.

## answer_b
Polysaccharide capsule

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true colonization factor). The polysaccharide capsule promotes colonization both by aiding adherence and by resisting early host clearance, placing it firmly among the recognized colonization factors. Its role here is adhesive/protective rather than related to gene transfer. Since the question asks for the one factor that does NOT contribute to colonization, and the capsule does contribute, this is not the answer.

## answer_c
Ordinary fimbria

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true colonization factor). Ordinary fimbriae (common pili) are classic adhesion organelles that let bacteria bind to specific host-cell receptors, a textbook colonization mechanism. They function purely in attachment, unlike the sex pilus described in option D. Because fimbrial adhesion is exactly what "contributes to colonization" means, this option is excluded as the correct answer.

## answer_d
Sex pili

## explanation_d
Correct. Sex pili are structures used for bacterial conjugation -- transferring DNA between bacteria -- not for attaching a bacterium to host tissue, so they play no role in colonization of a host. Glycocalyx, capsule and ordinary fimbria all promote adherence and are legitimate colonization factors, which is why sex pili stand out as the exception the question is looking for. Remembering that sex pili belong to the genetics/gene-transfer toolkit, not the adhesion toolkit, is the key discriminator here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q02

## title
Invasins are proteins that help in bacterial invasion by:

## question
Invasins are proteins that help in bacterial invasion by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Inducing apoptosis of epithelial barrier cells

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Invasins do not act by killing epithelial cells through apoptosis; that destructive mechanism is not how this class of virulence factor operates. Inducing cell death would remove the very cell the bacterium is trying to enter, which works against the invasin strategy of hijacking a live cell's own uptake machinery. This option describes a plausible-sounding but factually wrong mechanism.

## answer_b
Breaking down extracellular matrix of connective tissue

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Breaking down the extracellular matrix of connective tissue is the role of spreading-factor enzymes such as collagenase and hyaluronidase, not of invasins. Invasins act at the level of an individual host cell's receptors, not on the surrounding tissue matrix. Confusing these two distinct virulence-factor categories is the trap this distractor sets.

## answer_c
Preventing phagocytosis by immunity

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Preventing phagocytosis is the job of antiphagocytic factors like capsule, protein A and protein M, which is a completely different strategy from what invasins do. Invasins actually promote a form of cellular uptake (into non-phagocytic cells), the opposite conceptual direction from blocking phagocytic uptake. Keeping "prevent uptake" (capsule/protein A) separate from "cause uptake" (invasins) is essential to answering this correctly.

## answer_d
Interacting with specific receptors on the host cells, causing intracellular uptake

## explanation_d
Correct. Invasins are bacterial surface proteins that bind specific receptors on host-cell membranes, and this receptor engagement triggers the host cell itself to internalise the bacterium by intracellular uptake -- effectively hijacking the cell's own endocytic machinery. This lets bacteria enter cells that are not professional phagocytes, expanding the range of tissue a pathogen can invade. The defining feature to remember is "specific receptor interaction causing intracellular uptake," which separates invasins from matrix-degrading enzymes and antiphagocytic factors.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q03

## title
Which of the following is correct regarding immune evasion by bacteria?

## question
Which of the following is correct regarding immune evasion by bacteria?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Protein A on bacterial cell wall inactivates opsonizing antibodies, preventing opsonization & phagocytosis

## explanation_a
Correct. Staphylococcal protein A binds the Fc portion of opsonizing antibodies (rather than the antigen-binding Fab region), which inactivates their ability to flag the bacterium for phagocytes -- this blocks both opsonization and the phagocytosis that normally follows it. Because the antibody is bound "upside down," neutrophils and macrophages cannot recognize the coated bacterium as a target. This Fc-binding mechanism is the specific, testable detail that makes protein A an antiphagocytic factor.

## answer_b
Capsule helps in immune system evasion by preventing intracellular killing

## explanation_b
Incorrect. The bacterial capsule promotes immune evasion mainly by preventing phagocytic uptake in the first place (it masks underlying surface antigens and resists complement/antibody binding), not by allowing the bacterium to survive after already being engulfed. "Preventing intracellular killing" describes a post-phagocytosis survival mechanism (seen in some intracellular pathogens), which is a different strategy from the capsule's pre-phagocytosis blocking role. Mixing up "prevents engulfment" with "survives after engulfment" is the trap in this option.

## answer_c
IgA protease helps immune system resistance by killing plasma cells

## explanation_c
Incorrect. IgA protease works by cleaving secretory IgA, the antibody that normally protects mucosal surfaces, not by killing plasma cells (the antibody-producing cells themselves). By destroying the antibody rather than its producer, the bacterium can still colonize a mucosal surface even in the presence of an intact immune system. Confusing "cleaves the antibody" with "kills the antibody-producing cell" is the key error this distractor is testing.

## answer_d
Protein M prevents opsonization by IgG

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Streptococcal protein M binds host factor H, which accelerates degradation of C3b and thereby blocks complement-mediated opsonization, rather than blocking opsonization by IgG antibody. The mechanism is specifically about interfering with the complement cascade, not with antibody-mediated opsonization. This is a subtle but important mechanistic distinction the question is testing between protein A (antibody-based) and protein M (complement-based) evasion.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q04

## title
All of the following prevents phagocytosis except:

## question
All of the following prevents phagocytosis except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Protein M

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true antiphagocytic factor). Streptococcal protein M is antiphagocytic: it binds factor H and accelerates C3b degradation, blunting complement-mediated opsonization and the phagocytosis that depends on it. Because it genuinely blocks phagocytosis, it belongs with protein A and capsule, not with the odd one out. This option is therefore not the exception the question asks for.

## answer_b
Protein A

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true antiphagocytic factor). Protein A binds the Fc portion of antibodies, inactivating opsonization and preventing the phagocytosis that would otherwise follow. Like protein M and capsule, it works specifically by interfering with the opsonization step that phagocytes rely on. Since it does prevent phagocytosis, it is excluded as the answer.

## answer_c
Capsule

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true antiphagocytic factor). The polysaccharide capsule is one of the most classic antiphagocytic virulence factors, physically masking surface antigens and complement deposition sites so phagocytes cannot efficiently engulf the bacterium. It shares the same functional category (blocking phagocytosis) as protein A and protein M. Being a genuine antiphagocytic factor rules it out as the exception.

## answer_d
Lecthinase

## explanation_d
Correct. Lecithinase (a phospholipase) is a tissue-damaging enzyme that hydrolyses membrane phospholipids and destroys host cells directly; it does not act by blocking a phagocyte's ability to recognize or engulf the bacterium. Because its mechanism is direct membrane/tissue damage rather than antiphagocytic interference, it is the one option here that does not prevent phagocytosis. The key distinction to hold onto is that not every damaging bacterial factor is antiphagocytic -- some (like lecithinase) attack tissue by an entirely separate route.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q05

## title
Ability of causing a disease by bacteria is determined by:

## question
Ability of causing a disease by bacteria is determined by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Reaction to Gram stain by bacteria

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Gram-stain reaction (positive vs negative) is a laboratory identification characteristic, not a determinant of whether disease actually occurs; both Gram-positive and Gram-negative organisms range from harmless commensals to serious pathogens. This option confuses a diagnostic classification tool with a mechanism that decides pathogenicity. It is not part of the correct pairing tested here.

## answer_b
Virulence of bacteria

## explanation_b
Incorrect as a standalone answer (true but incomplete). Virulence -- the organism's intrinsic capacity to cause damage -- is genuinely one of the two determinants, so this option is not wrong in substance, but it is incomplete on its own because it omits the host side of the equation. The question specifically asks for what determines disease, which is a joint product of two factors. Because a more complete option (B & C) is available, virulence alone is not the best answer.

## answer_c
Host immunity

## explanation_c
Incorrect as a standalone answer (true but incomplete). Host immunity is the other genuine determinant, but again it is only half of the picture; a highly susceptible host can still avoid disease if exposed only to a low-virulence organism. Selecting immunity alone ignores that the organism's own virulence also has to be factored in. This option is superseded by the composite choice.

## answer_d
B & C

## explanation_d
Correct. Whether exposure to a bacterium actually results in disease depends jointly on the organism's virulence (option B) and the host's immune status (option C) -- neither factor alone is sufficient to predict the outcome. This is why the same organism can cause severe disease in an immunocompromised patient and no disease at all in a healthy one, and why a highly virulent organism can still be resisted by a robust immune system. Remembering "virulence AND host immunity together" is the concept this question is built to test.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q06

## title
Concerning host microbe relationship, pathogenic bacteria are:

## question
Concerning host microbe relationship, pathogenic bacteria are:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Parasites

## explanation_a
Correct. A parasitic relationship is one in which the microbe benefits at the expense of the host, which is exactly the relationship pathogenic bacteria have with the humans or animals they infect -- they gain nutrients and a niche while causing harm. This is the defining host-microbe category for any organism whose presence causes disease. Recognizing "harm to host + benefit to microbe" as the parasitism signature is the key takeaway.

## answer_b
Commensals

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A commensal relationship benefits the microbe while leaving the host unaffected -- by definition, this excludes any organism that causes disease, since disease means the host IS affected (harmed). Pathogenic bacteria, by causing harm, cannot be classified as commensals even though some normally commensal organisms can become opportunistic pathogens under altered conditions. The presence of harm is what rules this option out.

## answer_c
Mutualistic

## explanation_c
Incorrect. A mutualistic relationship benefits both the microbe and the host, which is the opposite of what happens in infection, where the host suffers damage. Mutualism describes examples like gut flora that both survive on host nutrients and provide the host benefits such as vitamin synthesis. Pathogenic bacteria do not provide any such benefit to a host they are actively harming, so mutualism does not apply.

## answer_d
Saprophytes 'free living'

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Saprophytes are free-living organisms that obtain nutrients from dead or decaying organic matter in the environment, entirely independent of a living host relationship. Pathogenic bacteria, by definition, depend on and interact with a living host to cause disease, which is a fundamentally different lifestyle from free-living saprophytic existence. This option describes an entirely separate ecological category, not a type of host-microbe relationship at all.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q07

## title
In commensalism relationship:

## question
In commensalism relationship:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Host gets benefit, while the microbe doesn't

## explanation_a
Incorrect. This describes the relationship backwards -- commensalism is defined by the MICROBE benefiting, not the host, while the host is left unaffected rather than benefited. A relationship where only the host benefits and the microbe does not is not a standard symbiotic category tested in this framework. This option inverts which party gains the benefit.

## answer_b
Both host and microbe get benefit

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Both partners benefiting is the definition of mutualism, not commensalism -- commensalism specifically requires the host to be neither helped nor harmed, only the microbe to gain. Confusing these two categories is exactly what this distractor is designed to catch. The presence of host benefit is what disqualifies this option.

## answer_c
Microbe gets benefit while the host isn't harmed

## explanation_c
Correct. In commensalism the microbe gains a benefit -- typically nutrients or a habitat -- while the host experiences neither harm nor benefit, remaining essentially unaffected by the relationship. This is the relationship many components of normal human flora have with their host under ordinary conditions. The phrase "microbe benefits, host unaffected" is the precise definition to memorize.

## answer_d
Microbe gets benefit while the host is harmed

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A relationship where the microbe benefits while the host is harmed is parasitism, not commensalism -- this is the category pathogenic bacteria fall into. The presence of host harm is the discriminating feature that shifts this description out of commensalism and into parasitism. This option describes a different relationship entirely.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q08

## title
Exotoxin is:

## question
Exotoxin is:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Polysaccharide

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A polysaccharide is a carbohydrate polymer (as seen in capsules or O-antigen chains), not the chemical class an exotoxin belongs to. Exotoxins are defined by their protein nature, which is entirely different from a sugar-based structure. This option misassigns the chemical category.

## answer_b
Tri acyl glycerol

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A tri-acyl glycerol is a lipid (a fat molecule made of glycerol and three fatty acids), unrelated to the protein structure that defines an exotoxin. No classic bacterial exotoxin is built from this lipid class. This option is a plausible-sounding but chemically incorrect distractor.

## answer_c
LPS

## explanation_c
Incorrect. LPS (lipopolysaccharide) is the structural basis of endotoxin, not exotoxin -- this is precisely the contrast the question is testing. Confusing LPS with exotoxin conflates two categories that are kept firmly separate: a structural cell-wall component (endotoxin/LPS) versus an actively secreted protein (exotoxin). Selecting LPS here shows the opposite of the intended distinction.

## answer_d
Polypeptide 'protein'

## explanation_d
Correct. An exotoxin is, by definition, a polypeptide or protein actively secreted by living bacteria, which is what allows it to be specific in its target and mechanism (unlike the generic inflammatory trigger of endotoxin/LPS). Its protein nature is also why exotoxins, unlike LPS, can typically be converted into toxoids by formalin treatment. The word "polypeptide/protein" is the defining feature to associate with "exotoxin" on this exam.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q09

## title
Which of the following can be transformed to a toxoid using formalin:

## question
Which of the following can be transformed to a toxoid using formalin:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Lipid A layer of Gram-ve bacteria

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Lipid A is a structural component of the Gram-negative outer membrane (the toxic part of LPS/endotoxin), not a secreted protein exotoxin, so it cannot be formalin-toxoided the way a true exotoxin can. Toxoiding specifically works by chemically modifying a protein's toxic active site while sparing its overall antigenic shape, a process that does not apply to a lipid structure. This option misapplies toxoid technology to the wrong chemical class.

## answer_b
Techoic acid of gram +ve bacteria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Teichoic acid is a Gram-positive cell-wall structural component (a carbohydrate/polyol polymer), not a secreted protein exotoxin, so it is likewise not amenable to formalin toxoiding. Its pathogenic role (inducing chemotaxis and mediator release) is a structural effect, unrelated to the enzymatic/receptor-binding activity that toxoiding neutralizes in a true exotoxin. This option again misapplies the toxoid concept to a non-protein structure.

## answer_c
Diphtheria toxin "AB toxin"

## explanation_c
Correct. Diphtheria toxin is a classic AB-subunit exotoxin (a secreted protein), and treating it with formalin destroys its toxic activity while preserving enough of its structure for the immune system to still recognize it -- producing the diphtheria toxoid used in vaccination. This works specifically because diphtheria toxin is a genuine protein exotoxin, unlike lipid A or teichoic acid. The AB-toxin structure is the reason this option, and only this option, can be converted into a toxoid.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "All of the above" is wrong because two of the three listed items (lipid A and teichoic acid) are structural, non-protein cell-wall components that formalin toxoiding does not apply to -- only the diphtheria AB toxin, a genuine secreted protein exotoxin, can be converted this way. Selecting "all" would require every option to be toxoidable, which is not the case here. This option tests whether the reasoning was applied to each choice individually rather than assumed by default.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q10

## title
Concerning septic shock, which of the following is correct:

## question
Concerning septic shock, which of the following is correct:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
It's caused by an AB toxin

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Septic shock is not caused by an AB-subunit exotoxin; that mechanism (a binding B subunit delivering an enzymatically active A subunit) belongs to toxins like diphtheria toxin, not to the Gram-negative structural trigger responsible for septic shock. Confusing the exotoxin-driven shock mechanism with the LPS-driven one is the trap here. This option describes a different toxin category entirely.

## answer_b
Caused by a super Ag

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A superantigen (like TSST-1) is the trigger for toxic shock syndrome, a related but distinct clinical entity from septic shock. Septic shock specifically follows Gram-negative infection and is driven by a structural cell-wall component, not by a T-cell-activating superantigen. This option swaps in the mechanism for the "sibling" shock state covered elsewhere in this module.

## answer_c
Considered as Type 3 Hypersensitivity

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Septic shock is not classified as a Type 3 (immune-complex-mediated) hypersensitivity reaction; it is a direct innate-immune cytokine response to a bacterial structural component. Type 3 hypersensitivity involves antigen-antibody complex deposition causing tissue damage, a completely different immunologic process from the pattern-recognition-driven cytokine storm of septic shock. This option applies the wrong immunology framework.

## answer_d
Lipid A layer of G-ve bacteria is the cause behind this case

## explanation_d
Correct. Septic shock following Gram-negative bacterial infection is caused by lipopolysaccharide, specifically its lipid A component, which triggers a massive host cytokine response producing fever, hypotension and potential multi-organ dysfunction. This structural-component mechanism (rather than a secreted exotoxin, a superantigen, or a hypersensitivity reaction) is the defining cause examiners want recognized for Gram-negative septic shock. Remembering "lipid A of LPS = septic shock trigger" is the anchor fact here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q11

## title
Hemolysins are considered as:

## question
Hemolysins are considered as:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Endotoxins

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Endotoxin refers specifically to LPS/lipid A, a structural component of the Gram-negative outer membrane -- hemolysins are secreted exotoxin proteins with a completely different chemical identity and mechanism. Labeling hemolysin as an endotoxin conflates two unrelated toxin categories covered elsewhere in this module. The two terms are not interchangeable.

## answer_b
Structural toxins

## explanation_b
Incorrect. "Structural toxin" is not the standard classification for hemolysins, which are actively secreted exotoxin proteins, not fixed structural components of the bacterial cell envelope. This vaguely-worded option does not match the specific mechanistic category (membrane-disturbing/cytolytic) that defines how hemolysins actually work. It is a distractor without a precise, testable meaning in this framework.

## answer_c
Membrane disturbing "cytolytic toxins"

## explanation_c
Correct. Hemolysins are classified as membrane-disturbing (cytolytic) toxins because they damage host-cell membranes -- including red blood cells -- by directly disrupting membrane integrity, rather than by an enzymatic or receptor-binding route. This direct membrane-damage mechanism is what produces the visible hemolysis on blood agar associated with hemolysin-producing organisms. "Membrane-disturbing/cytolytic" is the precise mechanistic label to remember for hemolysins.

## answer_d
A-B toxins

## explanation_d
Incorrect. An A-B toxin has a distinct two-subunit structure (a binding B subunit and an enzymatically active A subunit), exemplified by diphtheria toxin -- this is a different mechanism from a hemolysin's direct membrane-disrupting action. Hemolysins do not require a separate binding subunit to deliver an enzymatic payload; they act by disturbing the membrane itself. This option misapplies the AB-toxin structural category to a cytolysin.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q12

## title
Concerning techoic acid of gram +ve bacteria:

## question
Concerning techoic acid of gram +ve bacteria:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Has cytolytic activity

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Teichoic acid does not act by directly lysing or disrupting cell membranes the way a cytolytic toxin (such as a hemolysin) does; its pathogenic effect works through a different route, provoking inflammation rather than causing direct membrane damage. Assigning it cytolytic activity confuses its mechanism with that of a completely different toxin class covered elsewhere in this module. This option describes the wrong type of pathogenic action.

## answer_b
Induces chemotaxis and release of mediators

## explanation_b
Correct. Teichoic acid, a Gram-positive cell-wall component, contributes to pathogenesis by inducing chemotaxis of inflammatory cells and triggering the release of inflammatory mediators -- functioning as a structural trigger for the host inflammatory response, broadly analogous to (but mechanistically distinct from) how LPS acts in Gram-negative bacteria. This chemotaxis/mediator-release role is the specific, testable fact that distinguishes teichoic acid's pathogenic contribution. Remembering "chemotaxis and mediator release" is the key phrase to associate with teichoic acid.

## answer_c
Endotoxin

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Endotoxin refers specifically to LPS/lipid A found in the Gram-negative outer membrane; teichoic acid is a distinct Gram-positive structural component and is not itself classified as an endotoxin, even though both can provoke inflammation. Using "endotoxin" as a catch-all label for any inflammatory bacterial structure is the error this option tests for. The two are analogous in effect but not identical in identity or Gram-stain origin.

## answer_d
It's considerd a super Antigen

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A superantigen (such as TSST-1) works by non-specifically cross-linking MHC class II and T-cell receptors to cause massive T-cell activation -- an entirely different immunologic mechanism from teichoic acid's chemotaxis/mediator-release effect on innate inflammatory cells. Calling teichoic acid a superantigen misapplies a T-cell-activation mechanism to a structural cell-wall trigger of innate immunity. These are two distinct pathogenic strategies covered separately in this module.
