# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q13

## title
Concerning TSST-1 of staph aureus bacteria, which of the following is correct:

## question
Concerning TSST-1 of staph aureus bacteria, which of the following is correct:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Considered as Super Antigen

## explanation_a
Correct. TSST-1 is classified as a superantigen: it non-specifically cross-links MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells with T-cell receptors, bypassing normal antigen-specific recognition and causing massive, widespread T-cell activation. This unrestricted activation triggers the cytokine storm (TNF-alpha, IL-1, IL-6) responsible for toxic shock syndrome. "Superantigen" is the precise mechanistic classification examiners expect for TSST-1.

## answer_b
Disturbs protein synthesis

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Disturbing protein synthesis is the mechanism of AB-subunit toxins like diphtheria toxin (which ADP-ribosylates elongation factor-2), not of a superantigen like TSST-1, which works entirely through immune T-cell activation rather than by entering a cell to block its ribosomal machinery. Confusing these two very different toxin mechanisms (protein-synthesis inhibition vs. superantigen-driven immune activation) is the trap here. TSST-1 never targets the host cell's translation machinery.

## answer_c
Causes septic shock

## explanation_c
Incorrect. TSST-1 causes toxic shock syndrome, a distinct clinical entity from septic shock, which is caused by lipid A of LPS during Gram-negative infection. While both conditions share overlapping features (fever, hypotension, cytokine storm), the underlying trigger and organism type differ -- TSST-1/S. aureus for toxic shock versus LPS/Gram-negative bacteria for septic shock. This option conflates the two related-but-distinct shock states covered in this module.

## answer_d
Released upon death of Staph. aureus

## explanation_d
Incorrect. TSST-1 is actively secreted by living, growing S. aureus, not released only upon bacterial death; toxic shock syndrome classically occurs during active infection/colonization (historically associated with tampon use) while the organism is proliferating and secreting toxin. This option misapplies a "released on lysis" pattern (more typical of endotoxin) to an actively secreted exotoxin. TSST-1's secretion during active growth is what allows toxic shock to develop while the organism is still clinically present and multiplying.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q14

## title
All of the following is common between toxic shock and septic shock except:

## question
All of the following is common between toxic shock and septic shock except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Overproduction of mediators and cytokines "TNF-alfa, IL-1, IL-6"

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true shared feature). Both toxic shock (superantigen-driven) and septic shock (LPS-driven) produce overproduction of the same cytokine mediators, including TNF-alpha, IL-1 and IL-6, which is why the two conditions look clinically similar despite having different triggers. This cytokine overlap is exactly the kind of feature the question wants recognized as SHARED, not as the exception. Because it is genuinely common to both, it is not the answer.

## answer_b
Fever, hypotension

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true shared feature). Fever and hypotension are hallmark clinical findings in both toxic shock and septic shock, reflecting the downstream effects of the shared cytokine storm regardless of which specific trigger (superantigen vs. lipid A) set it off. This clinical overlap is a genuinely shared feature, not the exception being tested. It is excluded as the correct answer for that reason.

## answer_c
Caused by by living bacteria

## explanation_c
Correct. Neither toxic shock nor septic shock requires ongoing infection with actively multiplying, living bacteria at the moment shock develops -- both are driven by toxin/mediator effects (a superantigen already secreted, or LPS already released) that can produce shock even independent of a large live bacterial burden at that instant. This is the one listed feature that is NOT actually shared/true in the way the others are, making it the exception the question asks for. Recognizing that both syndromes are mediator-driven states, not simply effects of live circulating bacteria, is the key insight here.

## answer_d
Mediated by bacterial toxins

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true shared feature). Both syndromes are mediated by bacterial toxins/components -- TSST-1 (a superantigen) for toxic shock and lipid A of LPS for septic shock -- even though the specific toxin differs between them. Being "toxin-mediated" in a broad sense is common to both, which is why this option is not the exception. It correctly describes a shared underlying principle across the two shock states.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q15

## title
One of the following sites is naturally sterile:

## question
One of the following sites is naturally sterile:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Lymph

## explanation_a
Incorrect (this option is actually the sterile one in a different sense) -- wait, note: lymph/lymphatic fluid within intact lymphatic vessels is indeed normally sterile in a healthy person, but among the choices given the module's key identifies the bronchi as the intended sterile answer. The skin and GIT are clearly non-sterile, colonized sites, which is the more important contrast the question is drawing. Focus on the module's tested pairing: GIT and skin are colonized; the bronchi are the designated sterile site in this bank's framework.

## answer_b
GIT

## explanation_b
Incorrect. The GI tract is one of the most heavily colonized sites in the human body, hosting a large and diverse population of resident normal flora that aid digestion and provide protective functions. It is the clearest example of a non-sterile site among the options given. This makes it an easy exclusion when identifying the naturally sterile site.

## answer_c
Skin

## explanation_c
Incorrect. The skin carries abundant resident (and transient) normal flora, particularly in moist areas such as skin folds, and is a classic example of a colonized rather than sterile body surface. Its constant environmental exposure and resident flora make it clearly non-sterile. This option is excluded for the same reason as the GI tract.

## answer_d
Bronchai

## explanation_d
Correct. The bronchi and lower respiratory tract are normally free of resident microbial flora, in contrast to the upper respiratory tract, GI tract and skin, all of which are colonized. This sterility is why any organism cultured in significant quantity from bronchial secretions should be treated as a potential pathogen rather than as expected normal flora. Remembering "bronchi/lower airway = sterile, upper airway = colonized" is the key distinction tested here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q16

## title
Concerning transient flora:

## question
Concerning transient flora:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
They are present in all humans

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Transient flora are, by definition, acquired variably from the environment or contact rather than being universally present in every human at all times; their presence depends on recent exposure, not on a fixed, universal colonization pattern. This option overstates the consistency of transient flora across all individuals. Universality is not the defining feature the question is testing.

## answer_b
They permanently colonize the host

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Permanent colonization is the defining feature of RESIDENT flora, which is the direct opposite of transient flora's temporary nature. This option describes the wrong flora category entirely, swapping the two terms the question is designed to distinguish. Transient flora specifically do NOT become permanently established.

## answer_c
They are temporary flora

## explanation_c
Correct. Transient flora are, by definition, temporary -- microorganisms that occupy a body surface for a limited period without becoming permanently established, unlike resident flora, which persist long-term. This temporary nature is why transient organisms can typically be removed by routine hygiene measures such as handwashing far more easily than resident flora. "Temporary" is the single word that captures the entire concept being tested.

## answer_d
They are present in all conditions

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Transient flora are not present under all conditions; their presence is variable and depends on recent environmental or contact exposure, which is the opposite of a flora population that is reliably present regardless of circumstance. This option again overstates the consistency/permanence of transient flora. It contradicts the very temporariness that defines this category.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q17

## title
All of the following are functions of normal flora except:

## question
All of the following are functions of normal flora except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Production of some vitamins

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true function). Normal flora genuinely contribute to vitamin production (such as vitamin K synthesis by certain gut bacteria), which is a real protective benefit they provide to the host. Because this is an actual, recognized function, it is excluded as the exception the question is looking for. It belongs with the other genuine benefits listed.

## answer_b
Part of the innate immunity

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true function). Normal flora act as part of innate immunity by competitively excluding potential pathogens from colonizing the same niche, among other protective mechanisms. This is a legitimate, well-recognized benefit of having an intact normal flora population. Being a true function rules this option out as the answer.

## answer_c
Strong stimulus for the infant's immune system

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true function). Normal flora provide a strong stimulus for the developing immune system of an infant, helping to mature and calibrate immune responses early in life. This developmental-immunology role is a genuine, documented benefit of early microbial colonization. As a true function, it is not the exception being tested.

## answer_d
Production of powerful toxins

## explanation_d
Correct. Producing powerful toxins against the host is NOT a function of normal flora -- normal flora are protective under ordinary conditions, and while some can become opportunistic pathogens if they translocate to an abnormal site or if host defenses fail, that is different from "producing powerful toxins" as a routine, intended function. This is the one listed item that does not belong with vitamin production, innate immunity support and infant immune stimulation. Recognizing that harmful toxin production is excluded from the normal, beneficial role of resident flora is the key point here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q18

## title
Which of the following virulence factors is not important in invasion?

## question
Which of the following virulence factors is not important in invasion?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Collagenase

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true invasion-promoting factor). Collagenase breaks down collagen in connective tissue, physically opening a path that helps bacteria spread through and invade host tissue. Because it genuinely promotes invasion, it is excluded as the answer to "which is NOT important."

## answer_b
Hyaluronidase

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true invasion-promoting factor). Hyaluronidase degrades hyaluronic acid in the extracellular matrix, another classic "spreading factor" that facilitates bacterial invasion through connective tissue. Like collagenase, this is a genuine invasion-promoting enzyme, so it does not fit the exception the question is asking for.

## answer_c
Invasins

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true invasion-promoting factor). Invasins trigger receptor-mediated uptake of bacteria into host cells, directly promoting cellular invasion. This is one of the clearest invasion-promoting mechanisms in the list, making it an easy exclusion as the "not important in invasion" answer.

## answer_d
Pili

## explanation_d
Correct. Ordinary pili function mainly in adhesion -- helping a bacterium attach to a host-cell surface -- rather than in the active tissue-degrading or cell-entry mechanisms that define invasion (as performed by collagenase, hyaluronidase and invasins). Attachment is a prerequisite step, but pili themselves do not break down tissue barriers or trigger cellular uptake. This distinction between "adhesion structure" and "invasion-promoting factor" is exactly what the question tests, and pili fall on the adhesion side.

## answer_e
flagella

## explanation_e
Incorrect (not the best answer, but also not an invasion-promoting factor in the tested sense) -- flagella provide motility, allowing a bacterium to move toward a favorable site, which is a distinct function from breaking down tissue or triggering cellular uptake. The printed key specifically designates pili (option D) as the answer among this list; flagella's motility role is likewise not classified as an invasion-promoting mechanism, but the single keyed answer here is D.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q19

## title
Which of the following isn't correct concerning phospholipase?

## question
Which of the following isn't correct concerning phospholipase?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Protein in nature

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true statement about phospholipase). Phospholipase is indeed a protein in nature, consistent with it being an enzyme that catalyses hydrolysis of membrane phospholipids. Since this statement is true, it is excluded as the answer to "which isn't correct."

## answer_b
Also called Hemolysin

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true statement about phospholipase). Phospholipase is also referred to as hemolysin in this context, reflecting its membrane-damaging, red-cell-lysing activity. Because this naming overlap is accurate, this option does not qualify as the false statement being sought.

## answer_c
Disrupt cell membrane

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true statement about phospholipase). Phospholipase genuinely disrupts the cell membrane by enzymatically hydrolysing membrane phospholipids, which is its defining mechanism of tissue damage. Being an accurate description of its action, this option is excluded as the answer.

## answer_d
Induce toxic shock

## explanation_d
Correct. Phospholipase does NOT induce toxic shock; toxic shock syndrome is specifically caused by a superantigen (TSST-1), which works through non-specific T-cell activation, an entirely different mechanism from phospholipase's enzymatic membrane-hydrolysis action. Because this statement is false, it is the one the question is asking you to identify as "not correct." Keeping enzymatic membrane damage (phospholipase) mechanistically separate from superantigen-driven toxic shock (TSST-1) is the key discrimination this question rewards.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q20

## title
This patient's condition is likely to be caused by which of the following?

## question
A 27-year-old man had an operation, and the wound becomes infected by a gram-ve organism. After 4 hours, he developed headache and muscle aches. His body temperature was 40 C, and his blood pressure was 80/50 mmHg. This patient's condition is likely to be caused by which of the following?

## vignette
A 27-year-old man had an operation, and the wound becomes infected by a gram-ve organism. After 4 hours, he developed headache and muscle aches. His body temperature was 40 C, and his blood pressure was 80/50 mmHg.

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Protein A

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Protein A is an antiphagocytic virulence factor of Staphylococcus aureus (a Gram-positive organism) that binds antibody Fc regions; it does not cause the febrile, hypotensive shock picture described here, and the vignette specifies a Gram-negative organism, which rules out this Gram-positive-associated factor. Both the organism type and the mechanism are mismatched with this option.

## answer_b
Capsule

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A bacterial capsule is an antiphagocytic structural feature that helps bacteria evade the immune system; it does not itself trigger the systemic cytokine storm responsible for fever and hypotension seen in this vignette. Capsules are found on both Gram-positive and Gram-negative organisms and are not the specific trigger for this shock presentation.

## answer_c
Toxin that has A and B subunits

## explanation_c
Incorrect. An AB-subunit toxin (such as diphtheria toxin) acts by delivering an enzymatically active subunit into host cells to disrupt a specific cellular process (like protein synthesis), which is a different mechanism from the systemic inflammatory cascade described here. This vignette's fever/hypotension pattern, occurring rapidly (within 4 hours) after Gram-negative wound infection, points to a structural cell-wall trigger rather than an AB-toxin mechanism.

## answer_d
Lipopolysaccharide

## explanation_d
Correct. This clinical picture -- a Gram-negative wound infection rapidly followed by fever and hypotension -- is classic for septic shock caused by lipopolysaccharide (specifically its lipid A component), which triggers an overwhelming host cytokine response. The Gram-negative organism specified in the vignette is the key clue pointing toward LPS rather than a Gram-positive-associated factor like protein A or capsule. Recognizing "Gram-negative infection + rapid fever/hypotension" as the signature presentation of LPS-mediated septic shock is the clinical reasoning this vignette is built to test.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q21

## title
Which of the following best describes injectisomes?

## question
Which of the following best describes injectisomes?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Type II secretion system

## explanation_a
Incorrect. An injectisome is the structural apparatus of a Type III secretion system, not Type II -- Type II secretion systems export folded proteins across the outer membrane by a different mechanism entirely. Mislabeling the secretion-system number is a common but important error to avoid. The needle-like injectisome architecture is specific to Type III.

## answer_b
Present usually in Gram +be bacteria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Injectisomes are found mostly in Gram-negative bacteria, not Gram-positive ones, since the apparatus is built to span the Gram-negative cell envelope's two membranes to reach directly into a host cell. This option reverses the Gram-stain association that is actually tested. Type III secretion is a hallmark virulence mechanism of many Gram-negative pathogens.

## answer_c
Help in bacterial invasion of cells

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true but less complete description). Injectisomes do contribute to bacterial invasion of cells, but this option describes a downstream effect rather than the defining structural/functional description the question asks for ("best describes"). The more precise and complete description is the direct-delivery mechanism captured in option D. Because a more specific and accurate answer is available, this general statement is not the best choice.

## answer_d
Deliver bacterial toxins directly to host cells

## explanation_d
Correct. An injectisome is the needle-like apparatus of a Type III secretion system that delivers bacterial effector proteins/toxins directly from the bacterial cytoplasm into the host-cell cytoplasm, bypassing the extracellular space entirely. This direct-delivery mechanism is what best and most specifically describes an injectisome's function, more precisely than the general "helps invasion" statement in option C. Remembering "direct toxin delivery into host cells" as the injectisome's defining feature is the key fact tested here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q22

## title
What is the type of symbiotic relation that benefits both microbe & host?

## question
What is the type of symbiotic relation that benefits both microbe & host?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Mutual relationship

## explanation_a
Correct. A mutualistic (mutual) relationship is the one in which both the microbe and the host benefit, exemplified by gut flora that gain nutrients from the host while providing benefits such as vitamin synthesis and protection against pathogens in return. This "both sides benefit" pattern is the specific hallmark of mutualism among the three symbiotic categories tested in this module. "Mutual = both benefit" is the phrase to lock in for this question.

## answer_b
Parasitic relationship

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A parasitic relationship benefits only the microbe at the expense of the host, which is the opposite of the "both benefit" relationship the question describes. This is the category pathogenic bacteria fall into, not the mutually beneficial one being asked about here.

## answer_c
Commensal relationship

## explanation_c
Incorrect. A commensal relationship benefits only the microbe while the host is neither helped nor harmed -- the host does not gain a benefit in commensalism, which disqualifies this option from matching a relationship where "both microbe and host" benefit. This is a close but distinct category from mutualism.

## answer_d
Autotrophic

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Autotrophic" describes an organism's mode of nutrition (synthesizing its own organic compounds, typically from CO2), not a type of host-microbe symbiotic relationship at all. This option is a category-error distractor, describing a nutritional classification rather than a relationship type.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q23

## title
Jumping genes in bacteria is the name used to describe:

## question
Jumping genes in bacteria is the name used to describe:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Conjugative plasmids in gram-ve bacteria

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A conjugative plasmid is a distinct genetic element capable of self-transfer via conjugation; it is not what "jumping genes" refers to, even though plasmids and transposons can sometimes interact (a transposon can insert into a plasmid). The conjugative plasmid itself does not "jump" between DNA locations the way a transposon does.

## answer_b
Genes responsible for sex pilus formations

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Genes responsible for sex-pilus formation are structural/functional genes for the conjugation apparatus, unrelated to the concept of a mobile DNA segment relocating within or between DNA molecules. This option describes a fixed set of genes with a specific function, not a class of mobile genetic element.

## answer_c
Integrated non lytic phage

## explanation_c
Incorrect. An integrated non-lytic (lysogenic) phage is a prophage, which becomes part of the bacterial chromosome through a different, phage-specific integration mechanism -- this is not the same phenomenon as a transposon "jumping" between locations. A prophage generally stays where it integrated, distinct from a transposon's repeated relocation ability.

## answer_d
Transposons

## explanation_d
Correct. Transposons are DNA segments capable of moving ("jumping") from one location to another within the genome, or between chromosome and plasmid, which is exactly the phenomenon "jumping genes" describes. This mobility can carry along neighboring genes, including resistance determinants, as the transposon relocates. "Jumping genes = transposons" is the direct definitional link this question tests.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q24

## title
Bacterial injectisomes contribute to bacterial pathogenicity by:

## question
Bacterial injectisomes contribute to bacterial pathogenicity by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Binding iron after being excreted into environment

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Binding iron released into the environment describes the function of siderophores, an iron-scavenging system unrelated to the direct-injection mechanism of an injectisome. This option confuses two entirely different bacterial virulence strategies.

## answer_b
Breaking down lecithin

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Breaking down lecithin is the action of lecithinase (a phospholipase), a tissue-damaging enzyme, not the function of an injectisome. Injectisomes deliver toxins by direct injection rather than by enzymatically degrading membrane lipids in the surrounding tissue.

## answer_c
Delivering bacterial toxins directly into host cells

## explanation_c
Correct. Injectisomes (Type III secretion systems) contribute to pathogenicity specifically by delivering bacterial toxins directly into host cells, bypassing extracellular diffusion entirely. This direct-delivery route is the defining mechanism by which this structure damages host cells and promotes disease. It is the same fact tested from a different angle in this module's earlier injectisome question.

## answer_d
Evading host defence mechanisms

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Evading host defence mechanisms describes antiphagocytic factors like capsule, protein A and protein M, a different virulence strategy from the direct toxin-delivery role of an injectisome. While an injectisome does aid overall bacterial success against the host, its specific mechanism as tested here is direct toxin delivery, not immune evasion.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q25

## title
Which of the following characterizes and differentiates bacterial phenotypic variation from genotypic variation

## question
Which of the following characterizes and differentiates bacterial phenotypic variation from genotypic variation

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Heritable

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Being heritable describes genotypic variation, not phenotypic variation -- a heritable, DNA-sequence-level change is passed to offspring, whereas phenotypic variation is a non-heritable expressed change. This option describes the wrong side of the phenotypic/genotypic contrast.

## answer_b
Not affected by change in surrounding environment

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Phenotypic variation is, in fact, typically triggered BY a change in the surrounding environment (such as conditions prompting spore formation), the opposite of being unaffected by environmental change. This option inverts the actual relationship between phenotypic variation and environmental influence.

## answer_c
Reversible

## explanation_c
Correct. Reversibility is the key feature that differentiates phenotypic variation from genotypic variation: a phenotypic change (such as a bacterium sporulating and later reverting to its vegetative form) can reverse without any underlying change to the DNA sequence, while a genotypic change (mutation) is a permanent, heritable alteration that does not spontaneously revert this way. "Reversible" is the precise differentiating word this question is testing.

## answer_d
Induced spontaneously due to error in replication

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Being induced spontaneously by a replication error describes the origin of a genotypic change (a mutation), not a phenotypic one -- phenotypic variation is a response to environmental conditions, not a replication error. This option describes the mechanism behind genotypic, not phenotypic, variation.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q26

## title
Concerning Transduction, Which of the following is correct

## question
Concerning Transduction, Which of the following is correct

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
A method of gene transfer between gram-ve bacteria only

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Transduction is not restricted to Gram-negative bacteria; it occurs in Gram-positive bacteria as well, as shown directly by this module's own staphylococcal (Gram-positive) beta-lactamase-plasmid transduction example. This option overstates a Gram-negative-only restriction that does not actually apply.

## answer_b
In generalized transduction, non transmissible plasmids of Gram+ve bacteria can be transferred

## explanation_b
Correct. In generalized transduction, a virulent (lytic) phage can accidentally package random host DNA -- including a plasmid that has no self-transfer machinery of its own, such as a Gram-positive (e.g., staphylococcal) beta-lactamase plasmid -- and deliver it into the next bacterium the phage infects. This is exactly how an otherwise non-transmissible plasmid can spread between bacteria despite lacking its own conjugative apparatus. Recognizing that generalized transduction is not restricted by Gram stain, and can move a "stuck" plasmid, is the key fact tested here.

## answer_c
Specialized transduction is mediated by virulent phage

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Specialized transduction is mediated by a lysogenic (temperate) phage, not a virulent (lytic) one -- this is the reverse pairing from what the option states. A virulent phage is the mediator of generalized transduction, while a lysogenic phage (one that can integrate as a prophage) mediates specialized transduction.

## answer_d
Lysogenic phage can transfer genes coded by plasmid

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A lysogenic phage transfers DNA that lies adjacent to its own prophage integration site on the bacterial chromosome (specialized transduction), not genes that happen to be coded by a separate plasmid; plasmid-borne genes moving via phage is specifically the generalized-transduction scenario (mediated by a virulent phage), not the lysogenic-phage-mediated specialized pathway. This option mismatches phage type with the DNA source it actually transfers.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q27

## title
Which of the followings is an example of phenotypic variation?

## question
Which of the followings is an example of phenotypic variation?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Mutation

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Mutation is a heritable, DNA-sequence-level change, which is the definition of genotypic variation, not phenotypic variation. This option describes the category the question is asking you to distinguish FROM, not an example of it.

## answer_b
Transformation

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Transformation is a mechanism of horizontal gene transfer (uptake of soluble DNA by a competent recipient), which can introduce new, heritable genetic material -- a genotypic-level event, not a reversible phenotypic change. This option describes a gene-transfer mechanism, not an example of phenotypic variation.

## answer_c
Conjugation

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Conjugation, like transformation, is a mechanism of horizontal gene transfer that can introduce new heritable DNA into a recipient cell, making it a genotypic-level process rather than a reversible expressed-characteristic change. It does not fit the definition of phenotypic variation.

## answer_d
Spore formation & vegetation

## explanation_d
Correct. Spore formation and its reversal (vegetation, i.e. returning to the vegetative form) is the classic example of phenotypic variation: a reversible change in the bacterium's expressed state driven by environmental conditions, without any underlying change to the DNA sequence. This example directly illustrates the reversibility that defines phenotypic variation, in contrast to the heritable, DNA-level changes represented by mutation, transformation and conjugation. "Spore formation & vegetation" is the go-to example to associate with phenotypic variation on this exam.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q28

## title
Which of the followings isn't coded by plasmids?

## question
Which of the followings isn't coded by plasmids?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Sex pili production

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true plasmid-coded trait). Sex pili are encoded by conjugative (F) plasmid genes, making sex-pilus production a genuinely plasmid-mediated trait. Because it is truly plasmid-coded, it is excluded as the answer to "which is NOT coded by plasmids."

## answer_b
Exotoxin production

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true plasmid-coded trait). Many bacterial exotoxin genes are carried on plasmids (as well as on transposons or via phage), making exotoxin production a legitimate example of a plasmid-mediated trait in numerous organisms. This genuine plasmid association excludes it from being the answer.

## answer_c
Endotoxin production

## explanation_c
Correct (the actual answer, mislabeled "c" is skipped in the printed source and "endotoxin production" is option D per the printed key). Endotoxin (LPS) production is an intrinsic, chromosomally encoded structural feature of the Gram-negative cell wall, not a trait carried on a plasmid the way sex pili or exotoxin genes can be. This is because LPS synthesis requires a large, fixed set of chromosomal genes that are not typically found on mobile plasmid DNA. Recognizing endotoxin as a chromosomally fixed structural trait, unlike several other virulence factors, is the key discriminator this question tests.

## answer_d
Ordinary pili production

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true plasmid-coded trait, listed as option "e" in the source but not the printed key answer). Ordinary (common) pilus production can also be plasmid-associated in some bacteria, similar to sex pili, distinguishing it from the chromosomally fixed nature of endotoxin production. This option is excluded because it, too, can be plasmid-mediated, unlike the correct answer.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q29

## title
B lactamase plasmid of staphylococci can be transferred by which of the following?

## question
B lactamase plasmid of staphylococci can be transferred by which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Conjugation

## explanation_a
Incorrect on its own (partially true only if paired). Conjugation is not the mechanism keyed as correct for a staphylococcal beta-lactamase plasmid in this source; the printed key instead pairs transformation with generalized transduction as the correct combined answer. Conjugation requires direct cell contact and a self-transmissible plasmid, which is a different scenario from the phage- or DNA-uptake-mediated routes tested here.

## answer_b
Specialized Transduction

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Specialized transduction is mediated by a lysogenic phage and transfers DNA adjacent to the prophage integration site, not a separate plasmid's genes -- this is the wrong transduction category for a staphylococcal beta-lactamase plasmid, which instead pairs with generalized transduction. Confusing generalized with specialized transduction is the trap this option sets.

## answer_c
Transformation

## explanation_c
Correct (jointly with generalized transduction, printed key: C&D). A staphylococcal beta-lactamase plasmid can be transferred by transformation, in which a competent recipient bacterium takes up free plasmid DNA released from a donor, given sufficient DNA homology. This is one of the two mechanisms the printed key credits for this Gram-positive plasmid's spread, alongside generalized transduction. Both routes matter because staphylococci, unlike many Gram-negative organisms, rely less on classic conjugation for this particular resistance plasmid's spread.

## answer_d
Generalized transduction

## explanation_d
Correct (jointly with transformation, printed key: C&D). A staphylococcal beta-lactamase plasmid can also be transferred by generalized transduction, in which a virulent (lytic) phage accidentally packages the plasmid along with other host DNA and delivers it to the next bacterium it infects. This mechanism does not require the plasmid to have its own conjugative transfer machinery. Together with transformation, this is the pair of mechanisms the printed answer key credits for this question.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q30

## title
Sex pili & helicase enzymes are used for which type of gene transfer?

## question
Sex pili & helicase enzymes are used for which type of gene transfer?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Transformation

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Transformation requires only a competent recipient and free DNA in the environment; it uses neither a sex pilus nor helicase as its mechanism of transfer. This option misapplies conjugation's machinery to a different gene-transfer mode.

## answer_b
Specialized transduction

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Specialized transduction is mediated by a lysogenic phage, not by sex pili or helicase; the transfer vehicle here is the phage particle itself, unrelated to the cell-contact machinery used in conjugation. This option again misattributes conjugation-specific tools to a phage-mediated process.

## answer_c
Generalized transduction

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Generalized transduction is mediated by a virulent (lytic) phage packaging host DNA, not by sex pili or helicase. As with specialized transduction, the transfer vehicle is a phage particle, not the direct cell-to-cell apparatus used in conjugation.

## answer_d
Conjugation

## explanation_d
Correct. Conjugation is the gene-transfer mechanism that specifically uses sex pili (to form the physical connection between donor and recipient cells) and helicase (to unwind the donor's DNA for transfer). This combination of structural and enzymatic machinery is unique to conjugation among the four gene-transfer mechanisms in this module. "Sex pili + helicase" is the signature pairing that identifies conjugation on this exam.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q31

## title
Which of the following is not needed in conjugation?

## question
Which of the following is not needed in conjugation?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Sex pili

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true requirement). Sex pili form the physical connection between donor and recipient cells and are a genuine requirement for conjugation to occur. Since it is truly needed, it is excluded as the answer to "which is NOT needed."

## answer_b
F plasmid

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true requirement). The F plasmid is the classic conjugative plasmid whose transfer is the whole point of conjugation, and its genes also encode the sex-pilus and transfer machinery; it is a genuine requirement. Being truly necessary rules this option out as the answer.

## answer_c
Helicase

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true requirement). Helicase unwinds the donor DNA so a single strand can be transferred through the conjugation channel, making it a genuinely required enzyme for the process. Its real necessity excludes it from being the correct "not needed" answer.

## answer_d
Competence

## explanation_d
Correct. Competence -- the physiological state a recipient bacterium must be in to take up free environmental DNA -- is a requirement specific to transformation, not to conjugation. Conjugation instead relies on direct cell-to-cell contact via sex pili plus an F plasmid and helicase, with no need for the recipient to be in any special "competent" state. Recognizing that "competence" belongs to transformation, and NOT to conjugation, is exactly the distinction this question tests.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q32

## title
Uptake by a recipient cell of soluble DNA released from a donor cell requires

## question
Uptake by a recipient cell of soluble DNA released from a donor cell requires

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Competence and DNA homology.

## explanation_a
Correct. Transformation -- uptake of free (soluble) DNA released from a donor cell -- requires the recipient to be in a physiologically "competent" state, plus sequence homology between the incoming DNA and the recipient's own genome for the DNA to be stably incorporated by recombination. Neither a phage, a sex pilus, nor a restriction endonuclease is the defining requirement here; competence and homology are the two specific, testable prerequisites. This pairing is the precise definition of what transformation needs to succeed.

## answer_b
Temperate phage

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A temperate phage is the vehicle for specialized transduction (via lysogeny), not for transformation, which involves no phage at all -- transformation is purely about a competent cell taking up naked DNA from its surroundings. This option misapplies a phage-mediated mechanism to a phage-independent process.

## answer_c
Lytic phage

## explanation_c
Incorrect. A lytic (virulent) phage is the vehicle for generalized transduction, not for transformation. As with the temperate-phage option, this confuses a phage-dependent gene-transfer route with the phage-independent uptake of soluble DNA described in the question.

## answer_d
Sex pilus

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A sex pilus is required for conjugation, which involves direct cell-to-cell contact between a donor and recipient -- transformation, by contrast, involves no donor-cell contact or structure at all, only free DNA in the environment and a competent recipient. This option misapplies conjugation's machinery to transformation.

## answer_e
Restriction endonuclease.

## explanation_e
Incorrect. A restriction endonuclease is an enzyme bacteria use to cut and defend against foreign (often phage) DNA; it is not what enables the recipient to take up soluble DNA in transformation. This option introduces an unrelated enzymatic defense mechanism rather than the actual competence/homology requirement.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q33

## title
Lysogenic conversion involves which of the following?

## question
Lysogenic conversion involves which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Transformation of a virulent phage to a lysogenic one

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A virulent phage converting into a lysogenic one describes a phage's own life-cycle choice, not the definition of lysogenic conversion, which is about the resulting change in the bacterial host's phenotype once a prophage is established. This option confuses the phage's life-cycle switch with the downstream phenotypic-conversion event the term actually names.

## answer_b
Change in the bacteria characters due to phage own genes

## explanation_b
Correct (jointly with option C, printed key: B&C). Lysogenic conversion is fundamentally a change in the bacterium's characteristics caused by genes carried by the integrated prophage itself, expressed once the phage has become part of the host chromosome. This phage-gene-driven phenotype change is one of the two defining components of lysogenic conversion credited by the printed key. Without new prophage genes being expressed, there would be no phenotypic change to call "lysogenic conversion."

## answer_c
Incorporation of a prophage into a bacterial chromosome

## explanation_c
Correct (jointly with option B, printed key: B&C). Lysogenic conversion also specifically requires incorporation of a prophage into the bacterial chromosome, which is the physical event that brings the phage's genes into the host genome in the first place. This structural integration event and the resulting phenotype change (option B) together make up the full definition credited by the printed key. Neither element alone fully captures the concept.

## answer_d
Uptake of free DNA from the environment

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Uptake of free DNA from the environment describes transformation, an entirely separate gene-transfer mechanism that involves no phage or prophage at all. This option confuses lysogenic conversion (a phage-driven phenotype change) with a completely different, phage-independent process.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q34

## title
The donor bacterium remains alive in which of the following?

## question
The donor bacterium remains alive in which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Transformation

## explanation_a
Incorrect (not the intended contrast). In transformation, DNA is released from a donor cell into the environment (often after the donor has already died or lysed) before a separate competent recipient takes it up; there is no live "donor" actively participating in the uptake event the way there is in conjugation. This scenario does not center on a donor's survival status the way the question intends.

## answer_b
Generalized Transduction

## explanation_b
Incorrect. In generalized transduction, the bacteriophage that packages and transfers host DNA typically lyses its bacterial host in the process of phage assembly and release, meaning the donor bacterium does not survive. This is a fundamentally different fate for the donor than in conjugation.

## answer_c
Specialized transduction

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Similarly, in specialized transduction the host bacterium carrying the prophage is eventually lysed when the phage enters its lytic cycle to package and release new phage particles carrying the adjacent host DNA. The donor's survival is not guaranteed here either, unlike in conjugation.

## answer_d
Conjugation

## explanation_d
Correct. In conjugation, DNA transfer occurs through a direct physical connection (the sex pilus) between two living cells, and the donor bacterium remains alive throughout and after the transfer process -- it is not destroyed or consumed by the act of conjugating. This is the one gene-transfer mechanism among the four in which donor survival is explicitly guaranteed, distinguishing it from the phage-mediated transduction routes, where the host is typically lysed. Remembering "conjugation = donor survives" is the specific, testable fact here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH45-Q35

## title
In Gram +ve bacteria, plasmids coding for B lactamase can be transferred by

## question
In Gram +ve bacteria, plasmids coding for B lactamase can be transferred by

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Conjugation & generalized transduction

## explanation_a
Incorrect. While generalized transduction is genuinely one correct mechanism for transferring this Gram-positive beta-lactamase plasmid, conjugation is not the printed key's paired mechanism; the key pairs generalized transduction with transformation instead. This option gets one half right but the other half wrong.

## answer_b
Conjugation & specialized transduction

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Specialized transduction is the wrong transduction category for this scenario (it transfers DNA adjacent to a prophage site, not a separate plasmid's genes), and conjugation is also not the key's paired mechanism here. Both halves of this option are inconsistent with the printed key.

## answer_c
Conjugation & transformation

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Conjugation is not credited as a mechanism for this Gram-positive beta-lactamase plasmid in the printed key, even though transformation correctly appears in this option. Since conjugation is not part of the keyed pair, this option is incomplete/incorrect.

## answer_d
Transformation & generalized transduction

## explanation_d
Correct. In Gram-positive bacteria, a plasmid coding for beta-lactamase can be transferred by transformation (a competent recipient taking up the free plasmid DNA) and by generalized transduction (a virulent phage accidentally packaging the plasmid and delivering it to a new host) -- exactly the pairing the printed key credits, matching the same fact tested for staphylococci earlier in this chapter block. Neither conjugation nor specialized transduction is the credited route for this specific plasmid-transfer scenario. This question restates, from a Gram-positive-specific angle, the same generalized-transduction-plus-transformation mechanism already tested for staphylococcal beta-lactamase plasmids.

## answer_e
Transformation & specialized transduction

## explanation_e
Incorrect. Specialized transduction is not the keyed transduction type here (generalized transduction is), even though transformation correctly appears in this option. The mismatch on the transduction type makes this option incorrect despite getting transformation right.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q01

## title
Which of the following is a differentiating character of Staphylococcus saprophyticus

## question
Which of the following is a differentiating character of Staphylococcus saprophyticus

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Bacitracin sensitivity

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Bacitracin sensitivity is a classic differentiating test for Streptococcus pyogenes (Group A strep, bacitracin-sensitive) versus other beta-hemolytic streptococci, not a staphylococcal species test. This option imports a test from the wrong genus into a staphylococcus-identification question.

## answer_b
Protein A that binds to the Fc portion of IgG

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Protein A binding the Fc portion of IgG is a virulence/immune-evasion feature of Staphylococcus aureus specifically, not a species-differentiating test for S. saprophyticus among the coagulase-negative staphylococci. This describes a pathogenicity mechanism, not a laboratory identification test.

## answer_c
Resistance to novobiocin

## explanation_c
Correct. Staphylococcus saprophyticus is specifically differentiated from other coagulase-negative staphylococci, such as S. epidermidis, by its resistance to the antibiotic novobiocin -- a simple disk-diffusion test used precisely for this purpose. This resistance is the defining laboratory feature examiners expect for S. saprophyticus identification, and it matters clinically because S. saprophyticus, unlike most other coagulase-negative staphylococci, is a genuine urinary pathogen.

## answer_d
Coagulase production

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Coagulase production is the test that identifies S. aureus specifically, separating it from the coagulase-negative staphylococci as a group (which includes both S. saprophyticus and S. epidermidis) -- it does not differentiate S. saprophyticus from other members of that coagulase-negative group.

## answer_e
Beta haemolysis on blood agar

## explanation_e
Incorrect. Beta-hemolysis on blood agar is a hemolytic-pattern test more relevant to streptococcal classification (e.g. distinguishing Group A strep) than to differentiating among coagulase-negative staphylococci, which are not typically classified by hemolysis pattern in this way.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q02

## title
Which one of the following Statements concerning Mycobacterium tuberculosis is correct

## question
Which one of the following Statements concerning Mycobacterium tuberculosis is correct

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
M.tuberculosis appears as violet rod in acid fact stained specimens

## explanation_a
Incorrect. In an acid-fast (Ziehl-Neelsen) stained specimen, an acid-fast organism such as M. tuberculosis appears RED (retaining the carbol-fuchsin dye), not violet -- violet is the color associated with Gram-positive organisms on Gram stain, a different staining method entirely. This option mixes up the color result of one stain with the method of another.

## answer_b
M.tuberculosis appears as a red rod in gram stained specimens

## explanation_b
Incorrect. M. tuberculosis is not reliably classified by Gram stain at all -- its thick, waxy, mycolic-acid-rich wall largely resists the Gram-staining process, so it does not consistently appear as a clear red (Gram-negative) rod on a standard Gram stain. Acid-fast staining, not Gram staining, is the correct method for this organism.

## answer_c
After being stained M.tuberculosis resists decolorization with alcohol but not acid

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This statement reverses the actual resistance pattern: M. tuberculosis, once stained, resists decolorization by ACID-ALCOHOL (both together, which is exactly what "acid-fast" means), not selectively by alcohol while remaining vulnerable to acid alone. The phrase "acid-fast" itself signals that acid (combined with alcohol) is precisely what the organism resists.

## answer_d
M.tuberculosis has a thick cell wall with large amount of mycolic acid

## explanation_d
Correct. M. tuberculosis has an unusually thick cell wall containing a large amount of mycolic acid, a waxy long-chain fatty acid that is responsible for the organism's acid-fastness -- its ability to resist decolorization by acid-alcohol after carbol-fuchsin staining. This structural fact is the root cause of why M. tuberculosis requires a specialized staining method (Ziehl-Neelsen) rather than routine Gram stain. Remembering "thick wall + mycolic acid = acid-fast" is the key chain of reasoning this question tests.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q03

## title
Which of the following is a major cause of hospital acquired infections?

## question
Which of the following is a major cause of hospital acquired infections?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Braucella

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Brucella is classically associated with zoonotic exposure (unpasteurized dairy, livestock contact) and causes brucellosis, an occupational/foodborne disease -- it is not among the leading causes of hospital-acquired infection.

## answer_b
Pseudomonas aeruginosa

## explanation_b
Correct. Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a major cause of hospital-acquired (nosocomial) infection, particularly in ventilated patients (ventilator-associated pneumonia), burn patients, and those who are immunocompromised, due to its ability to thrive in moist hospital environments and its intrinsic antibiotic resistance. This makes it one of the organisms most strongly linked to the hospital setting itself, among the options given. Recognizing Pseudomonas as a classic nosocomial pathogen is a foundational clinical-microbiology fact this question tests.

## answer_c
Legionella pneumophilia

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Legionella pneumophila can cause outbreaks linked to contaminated water systems (including some hospital water systems), but it is not the organism most classically highlighted as a general major cause of hospital-acquired infection compared to Pseudomonas aeruginosa in this list.

## answer_d
Mycoplasma

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Mycoplasma causes atypical, community-acquired pneumonia, particularly in younger patients and in close-contact settings (schools, military barracks); it is not classically identified as a major hospital-acquired infection organism.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q04

## title
Which of the following isn't considered a member of Enterobacteriaceae

## question
Which of the following isn't considered a member of Enterobacteriaceae

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Klebsiella

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true Enterobacteriaceae member). Klebsiella is a Gram-negative, facultatively anaerobic rod of the GI tract and a genuine member of the family Enterobacteriaceae, so it is excluded as the exception the question is asking for.

## answer_b
Proteus

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true Enterobacteriaceae member). Proteus is likewise a Gram-negative rod within Enterobacteriaceae, sharing the family's core biochemical profile, and is excluded as the answer for the same reason.

## answer_c
Salmonella typhi

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true Enterobacteriaceae member). Salmonella typhi is a classic member of Enterobacteriaceae, the family that also includes E. coli, Shigella, Klebsiella and Proteus -- it too is excluded as the exception.

## answer_d
H. pylori

## explanation_d
Correct. Helicobacter pylori is NOT a member of Enterobacteriaceae; it is a separate genus of curved/spiral Gram-negative organism that colonizes the gastric mucosa, a fundamentally different morphology and ecological niche from the straight rod-shaped, GI-tract Enterobacteriaceae members. This distinction -- H. pylori's unique morphology and habitat -- is exactly why it is excluded from the family despite superficially being "a Gram-negative GI-associated organism" like the others.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q05

## title
Which of the following syndromes is specifically due to superantigen production by Staph. aureus

## question
Which of the following syndromes is specifically due to superantigen production by Staph. aureus

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Endocarditis

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Endocarditis is caused by direct bacterial colonization and damage of a heart valve (by S. aureus or other organisms such as S. viridans), not specifically by a superantigen mechanism.

## answer_b
Impetigo

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Impetigo is a superficial skin infection typically caused by direct S. aureus or S. pyogenes tissue involvement (sometimes with exfoliative toxins in related conditions), not by the systemic superantigen mechanism that defines toxic shock.

## answer_c
Osteomyelitis

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Osteomyelitis is a direct bone infection, most commonly by S. aureus reaching bone via bacteremia or contiguous spread, again a tissue-invasion mechanism rather than a superantigen-driven syndrome.

## answer_d
Toxic shock syndrome

## explanation_d
Correct. Toxic shock syndrome is the syndrome specifically caused by superantigen production (TSST-1) by S. aureus: the superantigen non-specifically activates a massive number of T cells, producing the cytokine storm responsible for the syndrome's fever, hypotension and rash. This is the one listed syndrome that is defined by a superantigen mechanism rather than by direct tissue invasion or colonization.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q06

## title
Which of the following organisms reacts +ve to gram stain

## question
Which of the following organisms reacts +ve to gram stain

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Pasteurella multicoda

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Pasteurella multocida is a Gram-negative coccobacillus, so while it does react to Gram stain, it reacts negatively (pink/red), not positively -- the question specifically asks for a POSITIVE Gram reaction, which this option does not provide.

## answer_b
Chlymedia pneumoniae

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Chlamydia pneumoniae is an obligate intracellular organism that does not reliably Gram-stain at all, since it lacks the conventional peptidoglycan-rich cell wall structure that the Gram stain depends on for a clear, classifiable result.

## answer_c
M. tb

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Mycobacterium tuberculosis is not reliably classified by Gram stain; its thick, mycolic-acid-rich wall requires acid-fast staining instead, so it does not give a dependable positive (or negative) Gram-stain result.

## answer_d
Strept. viridans

## explanation_d
Correct. Streptococcus viridans is a Gram-positive coccus with a typical peptidoglycan-rich cell wall, so it reacts reliably and positively to the Gram stain, retaining the crystal violet dye and appearing purple/blue under the microscope -- unlike the other three organisms listed, which either stain negatively or do not Gram-stain reliably at all. This makes it the one organism here for which a positive Gram-stain reaction is both technically obtainable and diagnostically reliable. Remembering that a conventional peptidoglycan-rich wall is the prerequisite for a dependable Gram-stain result is the underlying principle this question tests.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q07

## title
Which of the following organisms can be stained by gram stain?

## question
Which of the following organisms can be stained by gram stain?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Mycobacteria

## explanation_a
Incorrect (not reliably Gram-stained). Mycobacteria have a thick, waxy, mycolic-acid-rich cell wall that resists standard Gram staining, requiring acid-fast (Ziehl-Neelsen) staining instead for reliable visualization.

## answer_b
Mycoplasma

## explanation_b
Incorrect (cannot be Gram-stained). Mycoplasma entirely lacks a cell wall, and since the Gram stain depends on differential retention of dye by a peptidoglycan-containing wall, an organism with no cell wall at all cannot be meaningfully Gram-stained.

## answer_c
Neisseria

## explanation_c
Correct. Neisseria species are Gram-negative diplococci with a conventional cell-wall structure that the Gram stain can reliably classify, correctly showing them as Gram-negative (pink/red) organisms -- unlike Mycobacteria, Mycoplasma and Chlamydia, all of which escape reliable Gram-stain classification for different structural reasons. Having an intact, typical peptidoglycan-containing wall is what makes a Gram result trustworthy in the first place. Neisseria meets that structural requirement, while the other three options each fail it in a different way (atypical wall, no wall, or obligate intracellular lifestyle).

## answer_d
Chlymedia

## explanation_d
Incorrect (does not reliably Gram-stain). Chlamydia is an obligate intracellular organism without a conventional free-living cell-wall structure available for Gram staining in the way other bacteria have, so it does not give a reliable Gram-stain result.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q08

## title
Which of the following is a spore forming gram positive bacilli?

## question
Which of the following is a spore forming gram positive bacilli?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Bacillus anthracis

## explanation_a
Correct. Bacillus anthracis is a spore-forming Gram-positive bacillus, one of the two classic genera (alongside Clostridium) capable of forming dormant, highly resistant endospores under adverse conditions. This spore-forming capability is what allows B. anthracis to persist in the environment for extended periods and is central to its transmission by spore inhalation.

## answer_b
C. diphtheria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Corynebacterium diphtheriae is a Gram-positive bacillus, but it is not a spore-forming organism -- endospore formation among medically important Gram-positive bacilli is specifically a feature of Bacillus and Clostridium, not Corynebacterium.

## answer_c
Actinomycis israelii

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Actinomyces israelii is a Gram-positive, filamentous, branching bacterium (associated with actinomycosis), but it is not classified among the spore-forming genera; its characteristic feature is filamentous branching growth, not endospore formation.

## answer_d
E. Coli

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Escherichia coli is a Gram-negative rod, not a Gram-positive bacillus, and does not form endospores at all -- this option fails on both the Gram-stain criterion and the spore-forming criterion.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q09

## title
Which of the following characterizes all members in the genus staphylococcus?

## question
Which of the following characterizes all members in the genus staphylococcus?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Gram positive cocci-catalase & coagulase positive

## explanation_a
Incorrect. While all staphylococci are catalase-positive, not all are coagulase-positive -- coagulase-positivity is specific to S. aureus, so requiring BOTH catalase and coagulase positivity would incorrectly exclude the coagulase-negative staphylococci (e.g. S. epidermidis, S. saprophyticus) from the genus.

## answer_b
Gram positive cocci-catalase negative

## explanation_b
Incorrect. This option has the catalase result backwards: staphylococci are catalase-POSITIVE, not negative -- catalase-negative is the streptococcal pattern, which is precisely what distinguishes Streptococcus from Staphylococcus.

## answer_c
Gram positive cocci-catalase positive

## explanation_c
Correct. Every member of the genus Staphylococcus is a Gram-positive coccus that is catalase-positive -- this combination is the genus-defining identification pattern, true of S. aureus, S. epidermidis, S. saprophyticus and all other staphylococci alike, and it is what separates the genus from Streptococcus (catalase-negative).

## answer_d
Gram positive cocci coagulase positive

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Coagulase-positivity is not true of all staphylococci -- it is a species-specific feature of S. aureus alone, so it cannot be the characteristic that defines "all members" of the genus.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q10

## title
Which of the following is incorrect about staph. aureus?

## question
Which of the following is incorrect about staph. aureus?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
G+ve cocci in clusters

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true statement about S. aureus). S. aureus is correctly described as Gram-positive cocci arranged in clusters, a classic microscopic appearance for this organism; being true, this statement is excluded as the "incorrect" answer.

## answer_b
Endotoxin is an important virulence factor

## explanation_b
Correct. It is incorrect to call endotoxin an important virulence factor of S. aureus, because S. aureus is a Gram-positive organism and endotoxin (lipopolysaccharide) is a structural component exclusive to the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria -- a Gram-positive organism simply cannot produce it. S. aureus instead relies on exotoxins, superantigens (e.g. TSST-1) and antiphagocytic surface proteins (e.g. protein A) for its virulence. This is the false statement the question is asking you to identify.

## answer_c
Catalase +ve

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true statement about S. aureus). S. aureus is genuinely catalase-positive, consistent with all members of the genus Staphylococcus; being true, this statement is excluded as the answer.

## answer_d
coagulase +ve

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true statement about S. aureus). S. aureus is genuinely coagulase-positive, the specific species-level test that distinguishes it from the coagulase-negative staphylococci; being true, this statement is excluded as the answer.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q11

## title
Which of the following determines Lancefield grouping of B hemolytic Streptococci?

## question
Which of the following determines Lancefield grouping of B hemolytic Streptococci?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
coagulase

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Coagulase is a staphylococcal identification test (specific to S. aureus), not a feature used to classify streptococci into Lancefield groups.

## answer_b
Catalase

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Catalase separates Staphylococcus (positive) from Streptococcus (negative) at the genus level, but it does not further subdivide streptococci into Lancefield groups.

## answer_c
Cell wall CHO Ag

## explanation_c
Correct. Lancefield grouping of beta-hemolytic streptococci is determined by the group-specific cell-wall carbohydrate (CHO) antigen -- this is the defining basis of the classification system (e.g. Group A strep = S. pyogenes, Group B strep = S. agalactiae), distinguishing it from hemolysis pattern, catalase, or coagulase testing.

## answer_d
hemolysin

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Hemolysin activity determines the hemolysis PATTERN (alpha, beta, or gamma) on blood agar, which is a separate classification axis from Lancefield grouping -- beta-hemolysis identifies a streptococcus as a candidate for Lancefield typing, but the group itself is determined by the carbohydrate antigen, not by the hemolysin.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q12

## title
Which of the following can be used for Classification of Beta Hemolytic Strept.

## question
Which of the following can be used for Classification of Beta Hemolytic Strept.

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
coagulase

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Coagulase is a staphylococcal test, not applicable to classifying beta-hemolytic streptococci.

## answer_b
Catalase

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Catalase distinguishes staphylococci from streptococci at the genus level; it does not further classify beta-hemolytic streptococci among themselves.

## answer_c
hemolysin

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Hemolysin activity is what produces the beta-hemolytic pattern itself (the starting point for this whole category of organism), but it is not the basis used to further classify or subdivide beta-hemolytic streptococci into groups.

## answer_d
Cell wall Carbohydrate Ag ( Lancfield Classification)

## explanation_d
Correct. The cell-wall carbohydrate antigen (Lancefield classification) is the system used to classify beta-hemolytic streptococci into groups (A, B, C, etc.), based on a group-specific carbohydrate in the cell wall -- this is the same underlying fact tested from a different angle in this cluster's companion question on what "determines" Lancefield grouping. This antigen-based scheme is independent of, and applied after, the initial hemolysis-pattern screen that identifies an isolate as beta-hemolytic in the first place. Clinically, the Lancefield group (e.g. Group A for S. pyogenes, Group B for S. agalactiae) is what then guides organism-specific management.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q13

## title
Which of the following is the most likely causative organism?

## question
70 years old man complaining of chest pain, cough with bloody sputum. Blood agar reveals alpha hemolytic colonies and Gram staining shows Gram +ve cocci. Which of the following is the most likely causative organism?

## vignette
70 years old man complaining of chest pain, cough with bloody sputum. Blood agar reveals alpha hemolytic colonies and Gram staining shows Gram +ve cocci.

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Strept. pneumoniae

## explanation_a
Correct. This presentation -- an older adult with chest pain, cough and bloody sputum, alpha-hemolytic Gram-positive cocci on blood agar -- is the classic picture of Streptococcus pneumoniae causing lobar pneumonia. Alpha-hemolysis narrows the field to S. pneumoniae or S. viridans, and the acute pneumonia presentation with bloody ("rusty") sputum is what specifically points to S. pneumoniae over the more indolent, dental-procedure-associated S. viridans.

## answer_b
Streptococcus viridans

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Streptococcus viridans is also alpha-hemolytic and Gram-positive, so it fits the lab findings, but its classic clinical association is subacute infective endocarditis following a dental procedure, not an acute pneumonia presentation with bloody sputum -- the clinical context here points away from viridans strep.

## answer_c
Strep.pyogenes

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Streptococcus pyogenes is beta-hemolytic, not alpha-hemolytic, which immediately rules it out given the blood-agar finding described in this vignette.

## answer_d
Neisseria gonorrhea

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Neisseria gonorrhoeae is a Gram-negative diplococcus, not a Gram-positive coccus, and is not associated with this alpha-hemolytic pneumonia presentation at all -- it fails the Gram-stain criterion outright.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q14

## title
Which of the following best describes the genus Clostridia?

## question
Which of the following best describes the genus Clostridia?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Grow best in presence in air

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Clostridium species are obligate anaerobes, meaning they grow best in the ABSENCE of air (oxygen), not in its presence -- this option describes the opposite of their actual oxygen requirement.

## answer_b
Spore forming

## explanation_b
Correct. Spore formation is the best and most defining description of the genus Clostridia: like Bacillus, Clostridium species form dormant, highly resistant endospores, which is central to their environmental persistence and to diseases such as tetanus, botulism and gas gangrene (via germination of spores in a wound). This spore-forming capacity, combined with the genus's strict anaerobic growth requirement, is what examiners expect as the two hallmark features of Clostridia. Recognizing spore formation as the answer here reinforces the same endospore concept already tested for Bacillus anthracis elsewhere in this module.

## answer_c
Capsulated

## explanation_c
Incorrect. While some Clostridium species may have capsules in specific contexts, capsule formation is not the genus-defining feature the way spore formation is; it does not "best describe" the genus as a whole.

## answer_d
Motile

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Motility varies among Clostridium species and is not the defining characteristic of the genus; spore formation is the feature consistently and specifically associated with Clostridia as a genus.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q15

## title
Which of the following is common to all members of family Enterobacteriacere?

## question
Which of the following is common to all members of family Enterobacteriacere?

## vignette

## correct_answer
E

## answer_a
Oxidase +ve

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Enterobacteriaceae are, as a family, oxidase-NEGATIVE -- this is actually one of the features that distinguishes them from non-fermenting Gram-negative rods such as Pseudomonas (oxidase-positive), so this option has the family's trait backwards.

## answer_b
Ferment lactose

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Lactose fermentation varies within the family: E. coli and Klebsiella are lactose fermenters, but Salmonella and Shigella are not -- so this is not a trait shared by every member.

## answer_c
Catalase -ve

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true statement, but this is a positive question with only one correct answer among several plausible-sounding options; catalase-negative is not the credited trait here). Catalase reactivity within Enterobacteriaceae is not the unifying feature this question is testing; the printed key instead credits the oxygen-metabolism trait (facultative anaerobe) as the universal one.

## answer_d
all are True pathogens

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Not every member of Enterobacteriaceae is a true pathogen -- some (like certain E. coli strains) are normal GI flora, while others (like Salmonella typhi) are frank pathogens, so pathogenicity is not uniform across the family.

## answer_e
Facultative anaerobes

## explanation_e
Correct. Being facultative anaerobes -- able to grow with or without oxygen -- is the biochemical feature genuinely shared by every member of the family Enterobacteriaceae, unlike lactose fermentation, oxidase reaction, or pathogenicity, all of which vary by genus and species within the family. This universal metabolic flexibility is part of why Enterobacteriaceae are so successful colonizers of the human GI tract, an environment with variable oxygen tension. It is this shared trait, rather than any biochemical test result, that unifies the family at the taxonomic level tested here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q16

## title
Which of the following organism causes urinary tract infection with stones

## question
Which of the following organism causes urinary tract infection with stones

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Escherichia coli

## explanation_a
Incorrect. E. coli is the single most common overall cause of urinary tract infection, but it is urease-negative and therefore does not have the alkalinizing effect on urine that leads to struvite stone formation.

## answer_b
Klebsiella

## explanation_b
Incorrect. While some Klebsiella strains can produce urease to a lesser extent, Proteus is the organism classically and most strongly associated with urease-driven struvite stone formation in this comparison, making it the better answer among these choices.

## answer_c
Salmonella

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Salmonella is not a classic urinary tract pathogen at all, and it is not associated with urease-driven stone formation; it is primarily an enteric pathogen.

## answer_d
Proteus

## explanation_d
Correct. Proteus is the organism classically associated with urinary tract infection complicated by stones, because it produces the enzyme urease, which hydrolyzes urea into ammonia and raises urinary pH, promoting precipitation of struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) crystals that can grow into large staghorn calculi over time. This urease-driven mechanism is unique to Proteus among the four options listed, none of which share this same alkalinizing effect on urine. Recognizing urease production as the causal link between Proteus and stone formation is the key reasoning step this question rewards.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q17

## title
The causative organism is:

## question
A 33 years old male developed bloody diarrhea 1 week after being treated with an antibiotic course. He was diagnosed with pseudomembranous colitis. The causative organism is:

## vignette
A 33 years old male developed bloody diarrhea 1 week after being treated with an antibiotic course. He was diagnosed with pseudomembranous colitis.

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Yersinia enterocolitica

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Yersinia enterocolitica causes diarrhea by direct foodborne/fecal-oral infection, typically without a recent-antibiotic-course trigger, and is not the organism associated with pseudomembranous colitis.

## answer_b
Shigella dysentaria

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Shigella dysenteriae causes bacillary dysentery through direct invasive infection of the colon, unrelated to antibiotic-induced flora disruption, and does not cause pseudomembranous colitis.

## answer_c
Salmonella typhi

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Salmonella typhi causes typhoid fever through a different pathogenic mechanism (systemic invasion via Peyer's patches), not through antibiotic-associated colonic flora disruption, and is not linked to pseudomembranous colitis.

## answer_d
Clostridium difficile

## explanation_d
Correct. Clostridium (Clostridioides) difficile is the classic cause of pseudomembranous colitis, precipitated specifically by a recent course of antibiotics that disrupts normal colonic flora and allows C. difficile to overgrow and produce toxins that damage the colonic mucosa, producing bloody diarrhea and the characteristic pseudomembranes. The antibiotic-course history in this vignette is the specific clue pointing to C. difficile over the other, non-antibiotic-associated enteric pathogens listed.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q18

## title
The causative organism is most likely:

## question
A 28 years old male developed symptoms of chest pain and dry cough. He reported having a shower in the gym after training. The causative organism is most likely:

## vignette
A 28 years old male developed symptoms of chest pain and dry cough. He reported having a shower in the gym after training.

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Strept. pneumoniae

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Streptococcus pneumoniae causes typical, community-acquired pneumonia, usually without a specific aerosolized-water exposure history like the gym-shower detail given here.

## answer_b
Klebsiella pneumonia

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Klebsiella pneumoniae classically causes pneumonia in alcoholics or hospitalized/debilitated patients, not in a young, otherwise healthy person after a shower exposure.

## answer_c
Mycoplasma pneumonia

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Mycoplasma pneumoniae causes atypical pneumonia typically spread by respiratory droplets in close-contact settings (schools, dormitories), not specifically via aerosolized water exposure like a shower.

## answer_d
Legionella pneumophilia

## explanation_d
Correct. Legionella pneumophila causes atypical pneumonia classically linked to inhaling aerosolized water contaminated with the organism, such as from a gym shower, cooling tower, or air-conditioning system -- the specific water-exposure history in this vignette (shower after training) is the deliberate clue pointing to Legionella over the other pneumonia-causing organisms listed. Unlike typical bacterial pneumonias, Legionella will not be identified on a routine sputum Gram stain or standard culture, and requires a specific urinary antigen test or specialized culture media. Recognizing the water-exposure history as diagnostically decisive, rather than relying on Gram-stain or culture findings, is the clinical-reasoning point this vignette tests.
