# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q19

## title
The organism is most likely:

## question
A 37 year old is hospitalized for ventilatory support. After 2 days he developed severe cough with greenish sputum. Gram stain shows Gram-ve bacilli & culture grows oxidase +ve bacilli with green discolouration of the agar plate. The organism is most likely:

## vignette
A 37 year old is hospitalized for ventilatory support. After 2 days he developed severe cough with greenish sputum. Gram stain shows Gram-ve bacilli & culture grows oxidase +ve bacilli with green discolouration of the agar plate.

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Sttrept. pneumonae

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Streptococcus pneumoniae is a Gram-POSITIVE coccus, not a Gram-negative bacillus, so it does not match the Gram-stain finding described in this vignette at all.

## answer_b
Pseudomonas aeruginosa

## explanation_b
Correct. This vignette's combination of findings -- a ventilated patient, greenish sputum, Gram-negative bacilli, oxidase-positive on culture, and green discoloration of the agar plate (pyocyanin pigment) -- is the classic laboratory and clinical signature of Pseudomonas aeruginosa causing ventilator-associated pneumonia. Each individual finding (oxidase-positive, green pigment, ventilator association) independently points toward Pseudomonas, making this the best-supported answer.

## answer_c
Mycoplasma pneumonia

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Mycoplasma pneumoniae lacks a cell wall and does not Gram-stain as Gram-negative bacilli, and it does not produce a green pigment on culture, so it does not match either described finding.

## answer_d
Legionella pneumophilia

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Legionella pneumophila is a Gram-negative bacillus but does not produce the distinctive green pyocyanin pigment on standard culture media the way Pseudomonas does, and it typically requires special culture media to grow at all, unlike the routine growth described here.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q20

## title
Which of the following is a difference between E. coli and P. aeruginosa

## question
Which of the following is a difference between E. coli and P. aeruginosa

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Sugar fermentation

## explanation_a
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial difference). E. coli is a lactose/sugar fermenter, whereas Pseudomonas aeruginosa is a non-fermenter -- this is a genuine difference, but since two other genuine differences are also listed, this option alone is incomplete as "the" answer when a more complete choice is available.

## answer_b
Oxidase enzyme

## explanation_b
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial difference). E. coli is oxidase-negative, while Pseudomonas aeruginosa is oxidase-positive -- a real and clinically useful difference, but again only one of several correct differences listed.

## answer_c
Oxygen Requirements

## explanation_c
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial difference). E. coli is a facultative anaerobe, while Pseudomonas aeruginosa is an obligate aerobe -- a genuine difference in oxygen requirement, but likewise only one piece of the full answer.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Correct. Sugar fermentation, oxidase enzyme reaction, and oxygen requirement are ALL genuine differences between E. coli (ferments sugars, oxidase-negative, facultative anaerobe) and Pseudomonas aeruginosa (non-fermenter, oxidase-positive, obligate aerobe) -- since every individual option is a true distinguishing feature, "all of the above" is the most complete and correct answer.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q21

## title
The causative organism is most likely:

## question
A 22 years old male presented to the ER after 1 week of a street cat bite. He was diagnosed with osteomyelitis. The causative organism is most likely:

## vignette
A 22 years old male presented to the ER after 1 week of a street cat bite. He was diagnosed with osteomyelitis.

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Gram +ve bacilli with it's spores in cat's mouth

## explanation_a
Incorrect. There is no clinically significant Gram-positive, spore-forming bacillus that is a classic component of cat oral flora causing bite-associated osteomyelitis; this option invents a mechanism (spores in the cat's mouth) that does not match the actual causative organism.

## answer_b
Gram +ve cocci colonizing his skin

## explanation_b
Incorrect. While Gram-positive cocci from the patient's OWN skin flora (e.g. Staphylococcus) can cause wound infections generally, the specific, classic organism for a cat-bite infection comes from the cat's oral flora, not the patient's skin -- this option misattributes the source of the organism.

## answer_c
Gram -ve coccobacilli, flora in cat's saliva

## explanation_c
Correct. Pasteurella multocida, a Gram-negative coccobacillus that is part of normal cat (and dog) oral/saliva flora, is the classic causative organism of osteomyelitis or soft-tissue infection following a cat bite. The cat-bite history and resulting osteomyelitis are the specific clues pointing to this Gram-negative coccobacillus from the animal's own oral flora, distinguishing it from ordinary human skin-flora organisms.

## answer_d
Atypical cell wall devoid bacteria

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Atypical cell wall devoid bacteria" describes something like Mycoplasma, which has no established association with cat-bite wound infections or osteomyelitis; this option does not match the classic causative organism for this scenario.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q22

## title
Which of the following organisms is transmitted via spores inhalation?

## question
Which of the following organisms is transmitted via spores inhalation?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Bacillus anthracis

## explanation_a
Correct. Bacillus anthracis is classically transmitted to humans via inhalation of its highly resistant endospores, causing inhalational anthrax -- this spore-inhalation route is the specific, well-recognized transmission mechanism for this organism, distinguishing it from the vector-borne and fecal-oral routes of the other organisms listed. The spores' extreme environmental resistance is what allows them to persist in soil or contaminated animal products long enough to later be inhaled. This spore-inhalation transmission route is also the basis for concern about B. anthracis as a potential bioterrorism agent via deliberately aerosolized spores.

## answer_b
Yersinia pestis

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Yersinia pestis (plague) is transmitted mainly via the bite of an infected flea (a vector-borne route), or by respiratory droplets in pneumonic plague, not classically by inhalation of environmentally persistent spores the way B. anthracis is.

## answer_c
Yersinia Enterocolitica

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Yersinia enterocolitica is transmitted primarily through the fecal-oral route via contaminated food (a classic association being undercooked pork), not by spore inhalation.

## answer_d
Francisella tularensis

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Francisella tularensis (tularemia) is transmitted through tick or deer-fly bites, direct contact with infected animals, or occasionally inhalation of contaminated aerosols in specific settings -- but it is not a spore-forming organism, so "spore inhalation" specifically does not describe its transmission mechanism the way it does for B. anthracis.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q23

## title
Which of the following organisms is a common cause of endocarditis following tooth extraction and is part of the normal oral flora?

## question
Which of the following organisms is a common cause of endocarditis following tooth extraction and is part of the normal oral flora?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Strept. viridans

## explanation_a
Correct. Streptococcus viridans is part of the normal oral flora, and following a dental procedure such as tooth extraction, transient bacteremia can allow it to seed a susceptible heart valve, making it the classic cause of subacute infective endocarditis in this specific clinical setting. Its association with a previously damaged or abnormal valve, and its typically indolent (subacute) course, are why endocarditis prophylaxis is considered for certain high-risk dental patients. This organism-plus-exposure pairing (oral flora plus dental procedure) is the specific fact this question is built to test.

## answer_b
Streptococcus pyogenes

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Streptococcus pyogenes is associated with pharyngitis, skin/soft-tissue infections and rheumatic fever, not with being a normal oral-flora organism causing dental-procedure-triggered endocarditis.

## answer_c
Streptococcus pneumoniae

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Streptococcus pneumoniae causes pneumonia, meningitis and otitis media, and while it can colonize the nasopharynx, it is not the organism classically associated with post-dental-procedure endocarditis.

## answer_d
Staphylococcus aureus

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Staphylococcus aureus can cause endocarditis, but its classic association is with ACUTE endocarditis (often in IV drug users or via a skin/vascular-catheter source), not with the subacute, dental-procedure-triggered presentation caused by normal oral flora, which is the pattern this question describes.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH6-Q24

## title
Toxic shock syndrome toxin (TSST-1) is primarily produced by:

## question
Toxic shock syndrome toxin (TSST-1) is primarily produced by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Staphylococcus aureus

## explanation_a
Correct. TSST-1 (toxic shock syndrome toxin-1) is primarily produced by Staphylococcus aureus, the superantigen responsible for staphylococcal toxic shock syndrome -- this is the same organism-toxin pairing tested from a different angle earlier in this chapter (which syndrome is due to S. aureus superantigen production).

## answer_b
Streptococcus pyogenes

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Streptococcus pyogenes can produce its own distinct superantigens (streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins) capable of causing a similar streptococcal toxic shock syndrome, but TSST-1 specifically is a S. aureus product, not a S. pyogenes one.

## answer_c
Streptococcus pneumoniae

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Streptococcus pneumoniae is not associated with superantigen/toxic-shock-toxin production; its pathogenicity relies on different mechanisms such as capsule-mediated antiphagocytic protection.

## answer_d
Strept. viridans

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Streptococcus viridans, normal oral flora associated with dental-procedure endocarditis, has no established role in superantigen or toxic-shock-toxin production.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q01

## title
Alteration of 30s subunit leads to resistance of which of the following:

## question
Alteration of 30s subunit leads to resistance of which of the following:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Aminoglycoside

## explanation_a
Correct. Alteration of the ribosomal 30S subunit is the specific target-site change that confers resistance to aminoglycosides, since these drugs work by binding the 30S subunit to disrupt protein synthesis -- if the binding site changes, the drug can no longer act. This target-alteration mechanism is the key link tested here between a specific ribosomal component and a specific drug class. Remembering "30S = aminoglycoside target" is the key fact this question is built around.

## answer_b
Cephalosporins

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Cephalosporins target bacterial cell-wall synthesis (penicillin-binding proteins), not the ribosomal 30S subunit, so a 30S alteration would not confer resistance to this drug class.

## answer_c
Quinolones

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Quinolones target DNA gyrase/topoisomerase, not the ribosomal 30S subunit, so resistance to quinolones would instead come from alteration of DNA gyrase, a separate mechanism tested elsewhere in this module.

## answer_d
Sulfa

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Sulfa drugs (sulfonamides) act on folate synthesis enzymes, not the ribosome, so resistance to sulfa drugs comes from a different mechanism (such as an alternative metabolic pathway), not from 30S subunit alteration.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q02

## title
Change in the permeability of the drug is mechanism of resistance for which of the following drugs?

## question
Change in the permeability of the drug is mechanism of resistance for which of the following drugs?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Penicillin & Chloramphenicol

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Penicillin resistance is more classically linked to beta-lactamase enzymatic inactivation, and chloramphenicol resistance to enzymatic modification (acetylation), rather than to altered drug permeability as the primary mechanism tested for this pairing in this module.

## answer_b
Tetracycline & amikacin

## explanation_b
Correct. Altered membrane permeability -- reducing how much drug enters the bacterial cell -- is the resistance mechanism specifically linked to tetracycline and amikacin in this module's framework, lowering the effective intracellular drug concentration below what is needed to be effective. This membrane-level change protects the organism without altering the drug's target or metabolism at all. Recognizing tetracycline and amikacin as the pair linked to permeability change (rather than to target alteration or enzymatic inactivation) is the specific fact tested here.

## answer_c
Streptomycin & Quinolones

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Streptomycin resistance is more classically linked to ribosomal target alteration, and quinolone resistance to DNA gyrase alteration, rather than to altered permeability as the primary tested mechanism for this pairing.

## answer_d
Rifampicin & sulfonamides

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Rifampicin resistance is more classically linked to alteration of its RNA polymerase target, and sulfonamide resistance to an alternative metabolic pathway, rather than to altered permeability as the tested mechanism for this pairing.

inf

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q03

## title
Development of Alternative metabolic pathway is the mechanism of resistance for which of the following drugs

## question
Development of Alternative metabolic pathway is the mechanism of resistance for which of the following drugs

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Chloramphenicol

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Chloramphenicol resistance is more classically linked to enzymatic drug modification (acetylation) rather than to bypassing a metabolic pathway.

## answer_b
Tetracycline

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Tetracycline resistance is linked to altered permeability (as tested elsewhere in this module) or efflux, not to developing an alternative metabolic pathway.

## answer_c
Quinolones

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Quinolone resistance is linked to alteration of DNA gyrase, not to bypassing a metabolic pathway.

## answer_d
sulfonamides

## explanation_d
Correct. Sulfonamides work by inhibiting an enzyme in the bacterial folate-synthesis pathway; resistant organisms can develop an alternative metabolic route (such as using preformed folate) that bypasses the drug-blocked step entirely, conferring resistance specifically to sulfonamides among the drugs listed. This metabolic-bypass strategy leaves the drug's original target enzyme intact and simply irrelevant to the organism's continued folate supply. It is a fundamentally different resistance strategy from target alteration, permeability change, or enzymatic drug inactivation.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q04

## title
Which of the following is an example for acquired genetic resistances

## question
Which of the following is an example for acquired genetic resistances

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Vancomycin resistance by E.coli

## explanation_a
Incorrect. E. coli's resistance to vancomycin is intrinsic (it is a Gram-negative organism whose outer membrane vancomycin, a large glycopeptide, cannot penetrate), not a newly acquired genetic trait.

## answer_b
Ampicillin resistance by klebsiella

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Klebsiella's resistance to ampicillin is typically intrinsic in this comparison (a naturally occurring chromosomal beta-lactamase), rather than the specific example of NEWLY acquired resistance the question highlights via S. aureus.

## answer_c
Vancomycin resistance by klebsiella

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Similar to vancomycin resistance in E. coli, Klebsiella's resistance to vancomycin reflects an inherent (Gram-negative outer-membrane) barrier rather than a newly acquired resistance gene.

## answer_d
Penicillin resistance by staphylococcus aureus

## explanation_d
Correct. Penicillin resistance in Staphylococcus aureus is the classic example of ACQUIRED genetic resistance: S. aureus was originally penicillin-susceptible, and resistance emerged after strains acquired a beta-lactamase-encoding gene (often via a plasmid), actively gaining a new resistance mechanism rather than having always possessed it.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q05

## title
Mycoplasma resistance to Cell wall antibiotics is an example for:

## question
Mycoplasma resistance to Cell wall antibiotics is an example for:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Non genetic resistance

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Mycoplasma's resistance IS genetically based (it is an inherent property of its genome that it never encodes a cell wall), so "non-genetic" resistance does not correctly describe this mechanism.

## answer_b
inherit genetic resistance

## explanation_b
Correct. Mycoplasma's resistance to cell-wall-active antibiotics (such as penicillins) is inherent (intrinsic) genetic resistance: Mycoplasma naturally lacks a cell wall entirely, so a drug that targets cell-wall synthesis has no target to act on -- this is a built-in, inherited trait of the organism, not something newly acquired. Because Mycoplasma species have never possessed a cell wall throughout their evolutionary history, this resistance is universal across the genus rather than variable by strain. This inherent quality is what distinguishes it from acquired resistance, which depends on a specific strain gaining a new gene.

## answer_c
aquired genetic resistance

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Acquired genetic resistance describes a NEWLY gained resistance trait (such as S. aureus acquiring a beta-lactamase gene), which is different from Mycoplasma's resistance, which is an inherent, always-present trait rather than something gained.

## answer_d
mutations

## explanation_d
Incorrect. While the absence of a cell wall in Mycoplasma is ultimately a stable genomic trait, describing this resistance simply as "mutations" misses the more precise and specific classification the question is testing: inherent/intrinsic genetic resistance, as distinguished from acquired resistance.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q06

## title
Which of the following is true about superinfections

## question
Which of the following is true about superinfections

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Caused by infection with more than one type of bacteria

## explanation_a
Incorrect. While a superinfection does involve a different organism than the one originally being treated, the defining feature is not simply "more than one type of bacteria" being present -- it is specifically the eradication of susceptible flora permitting a resistant organism to overgrow.

## answer_b
Usually happens with narrow spectrum Antibiotics

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Superinfection is more classically associated with BROAD-spectrum antibiotics, which eliminate a wider range of normal flora (creating more opportunity for a resistant organism to fill the ecological niche), not with narrow-spectrum agents.

## answer_c
Lead to overgrowth of resistant normal flora

## explanation_c
Correct. A superinfection develops when an antibiotic eradicates the susceptible normal flora at a body site, removing the competitive suppression that flora normally provides, which then allows an intrinsically resistant (or opportunistic) organism to overgrow unchecked. This antibiotic-driven ecological disruption, rather than exposure to a second organism per se, is the defining mechanism of superinfection.

## answer_d
Caused exclusivly by bacteria

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Superinfections are not caused exclusively by bacteria; they can also involve fungi (e.g. Candida overgrowth) after normal bacterial flora is suppressed by antibiotics, so this option is too restrictive.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q07

## title
Which of the following can carry genes for Bacterial antibiotic resistance

## question
Which of the following can carry genes for Bacterial antibiotic resistance

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Temperate phage.

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A temperate phage is the vehicle for specialized transduction and can transfer genes located near its prophage integration site, but it is not the element specifically named in this module as the classic carrier of antibiotic-resistance genes.

## answer_b
R-plasmid.

## explanation_b
Correct. The R-plasmid (resistance plasmid) is specifically identified as the mobile genetic element that carries genes for bacterial antibiotic resistance, and can be transferred between bacteria by conjugation or other plasmid-transfer mechanisms. Because it is a plasmid rather than a chromosomal element, an R-plasmid can move horizontally between different bacterial cells, spreading resistance genes across a population far faster than mutation alone could. This mobility is what makes R-plasmids clinically significant drivers of resistance outbreaks.

## answer_c
lytic phage.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. A lytic phage mediates generalized transduction, potentially packaging random host DNA, but it is not the element this module specifically names as the classic resistance-gene carrier.

## answer_d
virulance plasmid.

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A virulence plasmid carries genes for virulence factors (such as toxins or adhesins), not specifically antibiotic-resistance genes -- this is a different category of mobile genetic element from the R-plasmid.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q08

## title
B lactamase enzyme cause antibiotic resistance by

## question
B lactamase enzyme cause antibiotic resistance by

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Modifying antibiotic permiability

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Modifying membrane permeability is a separate resistance mechanism (as tested for tetracycline/amikacin elsewhere in this module), not how beta-lactamase itself works.

## answer_b
Pumbing Antibiotic out of bacterial cell

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Actively pumping the antibiotic out of the cell describes an efflux pump mechanism, a distinct strategy from beta-lactamase's enzymatic action.

## answer_c
Prevent B lactam entry to bacterial cell

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Preventing drug entry describes a permeability/porin-based mechanism, not the direct enzymatic action that defines beta-lactamase.

## answer_d
Inactivation of B lactam

## explanation_d
Correct. Beta-lactamase causes resistance by directly inactivating the beta-lactam antibiotic -- it enzymatically hydrolyzes the beta-lactam ring structure that is essential for the drug's activity, destroying the drug's ability to bind and inhibit penicillin-binding proteins before it can ever reach its target. This direct enzymatic destruction of the drug molecule is a fundamentally different strategy from permeability changes, efflux pumps, or target-site alteration, all of which leave the drug intact but unable to act. Beta-lactamase production is one of the most clinically significant resistance mechanisms against penicillins and cephalosporins worldwide.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q09

## title
All of the following are examples for plasmid mediated resistance except:

## question
All of the following are examples for plasmid mediated resistance except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Alteration of 30s subunit

## explanation_a
Correct. In this module's framework, alteration of the 30S ribosomal subunit is classified as a chromosome-mutation-mediated resistance mechanism, not a plasmid-mediated one, making it the exception among these four options. This chromosomal classification matters because it means 30S-subunit resistance typically spreads by vertical inheritance (parent to daughter cell) rather than by horizontal plasmid transfer between different bacteria. The other three options here are instead grouped as plasmid-mediated in this module's framework.

## answer_b
Enzyme inactivating active drug

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true plasmid-mediated example). Enzyme inactivation of the active drug (e.g. beta-lactamase) is a classic plasmid-mediated resistance mechanism, since resistance genes encoding such enzymes are frequently carried on plasmids like the R-plasmid.

## answer_c
Active efflux pump

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true plasmid-mediated example). Active efflux pumps are grouped as plasmid-mediated resistance in this module's framework, consistent with efflux-pump genes often being plasmid-borne.

## answer_d
Modification of porins permeability

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true plasmid-mediated example). Modification of porin permeability is likewise grouped as plasmid-mediated resistance here, fitting the pattern of plasmid-carried resistance genes altering membrane transport proteins.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q10

## title
All of the following are examples for chromosome mutation mediated resistance except:

## question
All of the following are examples for chromosome mutation mediated resistance except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Alteration of 30s subunit

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true chromosome-mutation-mediated example). Alteration of the 30S subunit is grouped as chromosome-mutation-mediated in this module's framework, consistent with the companion question on plasmid-mediated resistance.

## answer_b
Active efflux pumb

## explanation_b
Correct. Active efflux pumps are grouped as a PLASMID-mediated resistance mechanism in this module's framework (as tested in the companion question on plasmid-mediated resistance examples), making this the exception among the four chromosome-mutation-mediated options listed here. Because efflux-pump genes are frequently plasmid-borne, resistance via this mechanism can spread horizontally between bacteria far more readily than a chromosomal mutation could. This classification is the mirror image of the companion question testing plasmid-mediated resistance examples.

## answer_c
Development of alternative metabolic pathway

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true chromosome-mutation-mediated example). Development of an alternative metabolic pathway (e.g. for sulfonamide resistance) is grouped as chromosome-mutation-mediated in this module's framework.

## answer_d
Alteration of DNA gyrase

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true chromosome-mutation-mediated example). Alteration of DNA gyrase, the target of quinolones, is a classic chromosome-mutation-mediated resistance mechanism.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q11

## title
The duration of antibiotic treatment is reviewed after

## question
The duration of antibiotic treatment is reviewed after

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
24 hours

## explanation_a
Incorrect. 24 hours is generally too early for a meaningful review of clinical response or culture/susceptibility results in most infections; the module's tested checkpoint is later than this.

## answer_b
72hours

## explanation_b
Correct. Antibiotic treatment duration and appropriateness are reviewed after 72 hours, a stewardship checkpoint that allows enough time for clinical response and culture/susceptibility data to become available, guiding a decision to continue, narrow, or change the regimen. Reviewing at this specific interval balances giving the antibiotic enough time to show a clinical effect against not continuing an ineffective or overly broad regimen for too long. This 72-hour checkpoint is a core antimicrobial-stewardship practice aimed at reducing unnecessary antibiotic exposure and the resistance-selection pressure that comes with it.

## answer_c
5 days

## explanation_c
Incorrect. 5 days is not the specific review checkpoint tested in this module; the correct interval is 72 hours (3 days).

## answer_d
1 week

## explanation_d
Incorrect. 1 week is not the specific review checkpoint tested in this module; reviewing this late would delay appropriate de-escalation or adjustment of therapy compared to the 72-hour standard.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH7-Q12

## title
selection of AB resistant mutants is enhanced by the following except:

## question
selection of AB resistant mutants is enhanced by the following except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Wrong choice of an antibiotics

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true resistance-enhancing factor). Choosing the wrong antibiotic for the causative organism exposes bacteria to a drug it may not be fully susceptible to, favoring survival and selection of less-susceptible or resistant subpopulations.

## answer_b
Interrupted course of antibiotics

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true resistance-enhancing factor). An interrupted antibiotic course leaves surviving, partially exposed bacteria (including any partially resistant subpopulation) to recover and proliferate, enhancing resistance selection.

## answer_c
High dose of antibiotics

## explanation_c
Correct. An adequately HIGH dose of antibiotics is the one option here that does NOT enhance selection of resistant mutants -- in fact, sufficient dosing helps ensure more complete eradication of susceptible organisms before resistant subpopulations can emerge and dominate. This is the opposite effect from under-dosing, which does promote resistance selection by exposing bacteria to sub-lethal drug concentrations.

## answer_d
Use of an antibiotic that is not indicated

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true resistance-enhancing factor). Using an antibiotic that is not indicated for the situation exposes bacteria unnecessarily to selective drug pressure without therapeutic benefit, favoring the emergence of resistant strains without appropriate justification.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q01

## title
Which statement describes best the morphology of Molds

## question
Which statement describes best the morphology of Molds

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Filamentous fungi

## explanation_a
Correct. Molds are correctly described as filamentous fungi, growing as branching, thread-like hyphae rather than as single cells. This filamentous growth form is the defining structural feature that separates molds from yeasts (unicellular, budding) among the basic fungal morphologies. Recognizing "filamentous" as the answer for "mold morphology" is the direct fact this question tests.

## answer_b
Dimorhic fungi

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Dimorphic describes a fungus capable of switching between a mold form and a yeast form depending on temperature/environment -- this describes a capacity some fungi have, not the basic morphology "mold" itself.

## answer_c
Yeast cells

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Yeast cells are unicellular and reproduce by budding, which is the opposite morphological category from a filamentous mold.

## answer_d
Capsulated fungi

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Capsulated fungi" describes a fungus with a polysaccharide capsule (such as Cryptococcus), an accessory virulence structure, not the basic growth-form morphology (mold vs yeast) the question is asking about.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q02

## title
Which of the following characterizes fungi of medical importance?

## question
Which of the following characterizes fungi of medical importance?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Cholesterol is the dominant cell membrane sterol

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Fungi use ERGOSTEROL, not cholesterol, as their dominant membrane sterol -- this sterol difference is in fact the basis for selective toxicity of several antifungal drug classes (polyenes, azoles) that target ergosterol specifically.

## answer_b
Cell wall is composed of glucan and mannan

## explanation_b
Correct. A characterizing feature of medically important fungi is that their cell wall is composed of glucan and mannan, polysaccharide/glycoprotein components distinct from a bacterial cell wall. This composition, together with chitin (tested elsewhere in this module), defines the fungal wall's structure. Recognizing glucan/mannan as true fungal wall components, in contrast to the false options here, is the point of this question.

## answer_c
They are obligate anaerobic organisms

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Fungi of medical importance are generally aerobic or facultatively anaerobic, not obligate anaerobes -- most require oxygen for normal growth and metabolism.

## answer_d
Peptidoglycan is the main component is cell wall

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Peptidoglycan is the main structural component of BACTERIAL cell walls, not fungal ones; the fungal wall is instead built primarily from chitin, glucan and mannan.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q03

## title
Which of the following isn't a morphological feature of Fungi

## question
Which of the following isn't a morphological feature of Fungi

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Diamorphic

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true fungal morphological feature). Dimorphism -- the ability to switch between mold and yeast forms -- is a genuine morphological feature seen in certain medically important fungi, so it is excluded as the answer to "isn't a feature."

## answer_b
Non sepetate hyphae

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true fungal morphological feature). Non-septate (coenocytic) hyphae are a real morphological variant seen in certain fungal groups (e.g. Zygomycetes), distinguishing them from septate-hyphae fungi -- a genuine fungal feature.

## answer_c
Unicellular yeast cells

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true fungal morphological feature). Unicellular yeast cells are one of the two basic fungal growth forms (alongside filamentous molds), a genuine fungal morphology.

## answer_d
Spore forming bacilli

## explanation_d
Correct. "Spore-forming bacilli" describes a BACTERIAL morphology (a rod-shaped, endospore-forming bacterium such as Bacillus or Clostridium), not a fungal one -- fungi can produce fungal spores, but "spore-forming bacilli" specifically names a bacterial shape/structure that has no place among true fungal morphological features like dimorphism, hyphal type, or yeast form. This is the deliberately out-of-category option the question is built to catch.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q04

## title
What is the best description of dimorphic fungi?

## question
What is the best description of dimorphic fungi?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Have both septate & non septate hyphae

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Having both septate and non-septate hyphae describes a variation in hyphal structure, not the mold-versus-yeast temperature-dependent switch that specifically defines dimorphism.

## answer_b
Can live in both aerobic & anaerobic conditions

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Ability to live in both aerobic and anaerobic conditions describes an oxygen-tolerance property, unrelated to the defining morphological switch of dimorphic fungi.

## answer_c
Cause cutaneous & systemic infections

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true consequence, not the best description). Dimorphic fungi CAN cause both cutaneous and systemic infections, but this is a downstream clinical CONSEQUENCE of their mold/yeast switch, not the defining description of dimorphism itself -- a more precise, mechanistic option is available.

## answer_d
Form hyphae in nature & yeast in vivo

## explanation_d
Correct. Dimorphic fungi are best described as forming hyphae (the mold form) in nature and converting to a yeast form in vivo (inside the human body) -- this temperature-dependent switch (25C environmental mold form vs 37C body-temperature yeast form) is the precise, defining feature of dimorphism, and it is what underlies their capacity to cause both cutaneous and systemic disease described in option C. Options A, B and C each miss this defining temperature-linked switch by describing either an unrelated hyphal-structure detail or the switch's downstream clinical consequence instead of the mechanism itself.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q05

## title
Fungal spores are diffrent from bacterial spores in:

## question
Fungal spores are diffrent from bacterial spores in:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Fungal spores are produced by filamentous fungi only

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Fungal spores are not restricted to filamentous fungi only; various fungal forms, including some yeasts, can also produce spores as part of their reproductive biology.

## answer_b
Fungal spores are essetial part of fungal life cycle

## explanation_b
Correct. Fungal spores differ fundamentally from bacterial spores in being an essential, routine part of the normal fungal life cycle (used for reproduction and dispersal), rather than being produced only as a stress-survival response the way bacterial endospores are. This life-cycle role, rather than any structural detail, is the key distinguishing fact tested here. Bacterial spores (endospores) exist specifically to help the organism survive adverse conditions, a fundamentally different biological purpose.

## answer_c
Fungal spores are produced when surrounding conditions are bad

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Being produced specifically "when surrounding conditions are bad" describes the trigger for BACTERIAL endospore formation, not fungal spores, which form as a routine part of the life cycle regardless of whether conditions are adverse.

## answer_d
Fungal spores can be absent in some fungal species

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This option does not correctly characterize the fungal-vs-bacterial spore distinction the question asks about; it describes variability across fungal species rather than the defining functional difference (life-cycle role vs survival response).

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q06

## title
A fungus that grows as a mold at 25C (room temperature) and as a yeast at 37C (human body temperature) is described as

## question
A fungus that grows as a mold at 25C (room temperature) and as a yeast at 37C (human body temperature) is described as

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Anaerobic

## explanation_a
Incorrect. "Anaerobic" describes an oxygen requirement (growth without oxygen), unrelated to the temperature-dependent mold/yeast morphology switch described in the question.

## answer_b
Saprophytic

## explanation_b
Incorrect. "Saprophytic" describes an organism that lives on dead/decaying organic matter, a nutritional/ecological category unrelated to the mold-vs-yeast temperature switch.

## answer_c
Dimorphic

## explanation_c
Correct. A fungus growing as a mold at room temperature (25C, in nature) and converting to a yeast form at body temperature (37C, in vivo) is, by definition, dimorphic -- this is the exact temperature-dependent switch that defines dimorphism, tested here directly from the definition rather than from its clinical consequence. None of the other options (anaerobic, saprophytic, dermatophytic) describe a temperature-dependent morphological switch at all.

## answer_d
Dermatophytic

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Dermatophytic" describes fungi (dermatophytes) that specifically infect keratinized tissue (skin, hair, nails), a clinical/ecological classification unrelated to the mold-vs-yeast temperature switch being described.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q07

## title
Which of the following statements regarding fungal cell walls is correct?

## question
Which of the following statements regarding fungal cell walls is correct?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Fungal cell walls primarily contain peptidoglycan.

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Peptidoglycan is the main structural component of BACTERIAL cell walls, not fungal ones -- fungi lack peptidoglycan entirely.

## answer_b
Fungal cell walls are mostly composed of Chitin.

## explanation_b
Correct. Fungal cell walls are mostly composed of chitin, a structural polysaccharide distinct from bacterial peptidoglycan, giving the fungal wall its own characteristic mechanical properties. This chitin-based composition is the specific, testable fact that distinguishes the fungal wall from all the other (incorrect) descriptions offered here.

## answer_c
Fungal cell walls are similar in structure to bacterial cell walls.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Fungal cell walls are chemically and structurally distinct from bacterial cell walls -- fungi use chitin/glucan/mannan, while bacteria use peptidoglycan, so they are not similar in structure despite both being "cell walls."

## answer_d
Fungal cell walls lack any polysaccharides.

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Fungal cell walls do contain polysaccharides (chitin, glucan, mannan) -- this option incorrectly denies their presence entirely.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q08

## title
Which of the following is the main component of most fungal cell walls?

## question
Which of the following is the main component of most fungal cell walls?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Cellulose

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Cellulose is the main structural polysaccharide of PLANT cell walls, not fungal ones -- fungi use chitin instead.

## answer_b
Peptidoglycan

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Peptidoglycan is a bacterial, not fungal, cell-wall component.

## answer_c
Lipopolysaccharide

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Lipopolysaccharide (LPS) is a component specific to the Gram-negative bacterial OUTER MEMBRANE, entirely unrelated to fungal cell-wall structure.

## answer_d
Chitin

## explanation_d
Correct. Chitin is the main component of most fungal cell walls, providing structural rigidity in a manner analogous to (but chemically distinct from) peptidoglycan in bacteria or cellulose in plants -- this is the same fact tested from a slightly different angle in this cluster's companion question on fungal cell wall statements. Because human cells have neither chitin nor a cell wall at all, drugs targeting fungal wall synthesis achieve a high degree of selective toxicity.

## title
The macroscopic aggergation (visible mass) of fungal hyphae is known as the:

## question
The macroscopic aggergation (visible mass) of fungal hyphae is known as the:

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Mycelium

## explanation_a
Correct. When individual fungal hyphae aggregate into a visible macroscopic mass, the resulting structure is called the mycelium -- the term specifically describing this aggregated collection of hyphae, distinguishing it from a single hypha, a yeast form, or fungal spores. Recognizing this specific term is useful clinically, since colony morphology on culture (mycelial vs yeast-like growth) is one of the first clues a microbiologist uses to classify an unknown fungal isolate.

## answer_b
Yeast

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Yeast describes the unicellular, budding fungal growth form, a completely different structure from an aggregated mass of filamentous hyphae.

## answer_c
Hyphal body

## explanation_c
Incorrect. "Hyphal body" is not the standard term for this macroscopic hyphal aggregation; the correct, established term is mycelium.

## answer_d
Spores

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Spores are reproductive/dispersal structures produced by fungi, distinct from the mycelium, which is the vegetative mass of hyphae itself.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q10

## title
Which of the following techniques is commonly used to visualize fungal elements in skin scrapings or nail clippings by dissolving keratin?

## question
Which of the following techniques is commonly used to visualize fungal elements in skin scrapings or nail clippings by dissolving keratin?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
India ink preparation

## explanation_a
Incorrect. India ink preparation is used to visualize the polysaccharide capsule of organisms such as Cryptococcus neoformans (by showing a clear halo against a dark background), not to dissolve keratin in skin/nail specimens.

## answer_b
KOH (potassium hydroxide) preparation

## explanation_b
Correct. KOH (potassium hydroxide) preparation is the technique commonly used to visualize fungal elements in skin scrapings or nail clippings; the KOH dissolves the surrounding host keratin, clearing it away so fungal hyphae and spores become visible under the microscope without needing a fungal-specific stain. This makes KOH prep a fast, inexpensive first-line test that can often confirm a dermatophyte or yeast infection at the bedside before culture results are available.

## answer_c
Gram staining

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Gram staining is a bacterial staining method based on cell-wall peptidoglycan content and is not the technique used for dissolving keratin to reveal fungal elements.

## answer_d
Periodic acid-Schiff (PAS) stain

## explanation_d
Incorrect. PAS (periodic acid-Schiff) stain does highlight fungal cell-wall polysaccharides in tissue sections, but it works by staining the fungal wall itself rather than by dissolving host keratin the way KOH preparation specifically does for skin/nail scrapings.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q11

## title
polyenes primarily acts by

## question
polyenes primarily acts by

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Blocking fungal microtubule assembly

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Blocking microtubule assembly is a mechanism associated with a different antifungal class (e.g. griseofulvin), not polyenes.

## answer_b
Inhibiting nucleic acid synthesis

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Inhibiting nucleic acid synthesis is the mechanism of a different antifungal class (e.g. flucytosine), not polyenes.

## answer_c
Binding to ergosterol and forming pores in fungal cell membranes

## explanation_c
Correct. Polyenes (such as amphotericin B) primarily act by binding ergosterol in the fungal cell membrane and forming pores, which disrupts membrane integrity and causes leakage of cell contents, killing the fungal cell. This ergosterol-targeting mechanism is what gives polyenes their selective toxicity against fungi (whose membranes contain ergosterol) over human cells (which use cholesterol instead).

## answer_d
Inhibiting Beta-glucan synthesis

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Inhibiting beta-glucan synthesis is the mechanism of echinocandins, a different antifungal class that targets the fungal cell WALL, not the polyene mechanism, which targets the cell MEMBRANE.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q12

## title
Echinocandins (e.g., caspofungin) act by inhibiting:

## question
Echinocandins (e.g., caspofungin) act by inhibiting:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Ergosterol synthesis

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Inhibiting ergosterol SYNTHESIS is the mechanism of azole antifungals, not echinocandins; polyenes, by contrast, BIND already-formed ergosterol rather than blocking its synthesis -- neither matches the echinocandin mechanism.

## answer_b
Microtubule formation

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Inhibiting microtubule formation is associated with a different antifungal class (e.g. griseofulvin), not echinocandins.

## answer_c
Beta-glucan synthase

## explanation_c
Correct. Echinocandins, such as caspofungin, act by inhibiting beta-glucan synthase, the enzyme responsible for producing beta-glucan, a key structural component of the fungal cell WALL. This wall-targeting mechanism is fundamentally different from the membrane-targeting mechanism of polyenes, giving echinocandins a distinct spectrum and side-effect profile.

## answer_d
Capsule synthesis

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Inhibiting capsule synthesis would target an organism like Cryptococcus's polysaccharide capsule specifically, not the general fungal cell-wall beta-glucan that echinocandins actually target.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q13

## title
What is the structural component that is found in all viruses?

## question
What is the structural component that is found in all viruses?

## vignette

## correct_answer
E

## answer_a
Envelope

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Not all viruses have an envelope; many important human pathogens (e.g. poliovirus, rhinovirus) are non-enveloped ("naked") viruses that rely on the capsid alone for protection.

## answer_b
DNA

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A virus carries either DNA or RNA as its genome, never both -- so DNA specifically is not found in every virus (RNA viruses lack it entirely).

## answer_c
Spikes

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Spikes (surface projections, often glycoproteins) are found on many enveloped viruses but are not a universal viral feature, since non-enveloped viruses lack this structure.

## answer_d
RNA

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Similarly, RNA is not found in every virus -- DNA viruses use DNA as their genome instead, so RNA specifically is not universal.

## answer_e
Capsid

## explanation_e
Correct. The capsid -- the protein coat surrounding the viral genome -- is the one structural component found in ALL viruses, whether or not they also have an envelope, and regardless of whether their genome is DNA or RNA. This universality is why the capsid, more than any other structure, defines what makes a virus particle a virus particle. Remembering "capsid = universal, envelope = optional" is the key distinction this question tests.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q14

## title
Viroid can be defined as:

## question
Viroid can be defined as:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Host DNA enclosed in capsid

## explanation_a
Incorrect. "Host DNA enclosed in capsid" does not describe a viroid at all -- a viroid is RNA, not host DNA, and specifically lacks any protein coat/capsid.

## answer_b
Infectious protein particles

## explanation_b
Incorrect. "Infectious protein particles" describes a PRION, an entirely different infectious agent composed purely of misfolded protein with no nucleic acid at all -- not a viroid, which is nucleic-acid-based.

## answer_c
Circular RNA without envelope or coat

## explanation_c
Correct. A viroid is defined as a small, circular RNA molecule that lacks both an envelope and a protein coat (capsid) -- this absence of any protective coat, combined with its circular RNA nature, is the precise definition that distinguishes a viroid from a virus (which has at least a capsid), a prion (protein, no nucleic acid), or a satellite/defective virus (needs a helper virus). Because a viroid has no protein coat of any kind, it also cannot be neutralized by antibodies directed against a capsid or envelope protein, a further contrast with true viruses.

## answer_d
Virus particles that can't replicate without a helper virus

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Virus particles that can't replicate without a helper virus" describes a defective or satellite virus, a different infectious entity from a viroid, which is defined by its lack of coat/envelope rather than by a helper-virus dependency.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q15

## title
Latent Viral infection is:

## question
Latent Viral infection is:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Virus is continuously detected with mild or no clinical symptoms

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Continuous detection of virus with mild/no symptoms describes a CHRONIC infection (ongoing, low-level productive replication), a distinct persistence pattern from latency, which is defined by the virus being occult (hidden, non-productive), not continuously detectable.

## answer_b
Virus presents in an occult form inside the infected cell.

## explanation_b
Correct. Latent viral infection is defined as the virus presenting in an occult (hidden, non-productive) form inside the infected cell -- the virus is present but not actively producing new infectious particles, only to potentially reactivate later under certain triggers. This occult, non-productive state is the precise defining feature that distinguishes latent from chronic or slow infection.

## answer_c
Viral infections with prolonged incubation period

## explanation_c
Incorrect. A prolonged incubation period describes a SLOW infection (e.g., as seen in some prion or slow-virus diseases), a different persistence pattern from latency, which is about the virus's occult cellular state, not the length of the initial incubation period.

## answer_d
Virus Causing disease away from site of entry

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Virus causing disease away from site of entry" describes a systemic spread pattern, not the defining feature of latent infection specifically, which is about the virus's hidden, non-productive state within a cell rather than about disease location relative to entry.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q16

## title
Viruses are characterized by all the following except:

## question
Viruses are characterized by all the following except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Having either DNA or RNA

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true general viral property). Every virus has either DNA or RNA as its genome (never both), a genuinely universal viral characteristic, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Non susceptible to antibiotics

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true general viral property). Viruses are indeed non-susceptible to antibiotics, since antibiotics target bacterial-specific structures/processes (cell wall synthesis, bacterial ribosomes, etc.) that viruses do not possess or use.

## answer_c
Obligate intracellular

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true general viral property). All viruses are obligate intracellular organisms, requiring a host cell's machinery to replicate -- a genuinely universal viral feature.

## answer_d
Destroyed by phenol

## explanation_d
Correct. Being "destroyed by phenol" is NOT a universal viral property -- non-enveloped (naked) viruses, which lack a lipid envelope for phenol/detergents to disrupt, are notably more resistant to phenol than enveloped viruses are. This resistance variation by viral structure type is exactly why this statement cannot be listed as a universal characteristic of "viruses" in general, making it the exception the question asks for.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q17

## title
RBCs can be used for detection of viral growth on tissue culture by

## question
RBCs can be used for detection of viral growth on tissue culture by

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Virally infected cells adsorb RBCs

## explanation_a
Correct. Hemadsorption -- the technique this question describes -- works because virally infected culture cells express viral hemagglutinin proteins on their surface, causing added red blood cells to adhere (adsorb) directly onto those infected cells. This visible adherence signals that viral replication and protein expression have occurred, even before an obvious cytopathic effect might be seen. Recognizing "infected cells adsorb RBCs" as the defining mechanism of hemadsorption is the fact this question tests.

## answer_b
Virus kills RBCs

## explanation_b
Incorrect. RBC-based viral detection does not typically work by the virus directly killing red blood cells; hemadsorption relies on RBCs adhering to infected cells' surfaces, not on RBC destruction.

## answer_c
Virus interferes with replication of another virus inside RBCs

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Viral replication does not occur "inside RBCs" as a general detection mechanism; RBCs are mature cells without the machinery most viruses need to replicate, and this does not describe hemadsorption.

## answer_d
Virus causes rounding of RBCs

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Rounding of RBCs" is not the recognized mechanism of RBC-based viral detection; hemadsorption specifically involves RBCs adhering to infected cells' surfaces, not a shape change in the RBCs themselves.

## answer_e
Virus inhibits agglutination of RBCs

## explanation_e
Incorrect. This option describes the opposite of what actually happens in hemagglutination-related phenomena; many viruses instead CAUSE agglutination of RBCs (hemagglutination) via their surface proteins, rather than inhibiting it, in a related but distinct detection technique from hemadsorption.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q18

## title
Interference reaction to detect viral growth on tissue culture can be defined as :

## question
Interference reaction to detect viral growth on tissue culture can be defined as :

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Virus stops replication of another virus

## explanation_a
Incomplete/imprecise. "Virus stops replication of another virus" is close to the general idea of interference but is less mechanistically precise than the specific, standard definition involving the cytopathic-effect (CPE) blocking phenomenon.

## answer_b
Addition of specefic antiviral serum

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Adding specific antiviral serum describes a neutralization/serologic technique, not the interference phenomenon, which occurs between two viruses in culture, not between a virus and an antibody preparation.

## answer_c
Non cytopathic viruses inhibit CPE of cytopathic virus

## explanation_c
This is the standard definition of viral interference used for tissue-culture detection: a non-cytopathic virus present in the culture inhibits the cytopathic effect (CPE) that a second, cytopathic (CPE-producing) virus would otherwise cause, revealing the first virus's presence indirectly by the CPE's absence. This phenomenon is thought to arise because the non-cytopathic virus can trigger host antiviral defenses (such as interferon production) that also blunt the second virus's ability to damage the cell. NOTE: this question's own printed answer key marks it with no valid letter ("XXX" -- an unrecoverable ANSWER-KEY GAP), so correct_answer here is set to C based on independent virology-textbook verification of the standard definition, NOT transcribed from a printed key letter; see author_notes and the concept's uncertainty field for the gap documentation.

## answer_d
Overgrowth of cells on tissue culture forming discrete focci

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Overgrowth of cells forming discrete foci describes a transformation/focus-formation assay (relevant to detecting oncogenic viral transformation), not the interference phenomenon.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q19

## title
The viral coat is composed of which of the following?

## question
The viral coat is composed of which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Single protein unit

## explanation_a
Incorrect. The viral coat (capsid) is not a single protein unit; it is built from many repeating protein subunits assembled together, not one monolithic protein.

## answer_b
Lipoprotein

## explanation_b
Incorrect. "Lipoprotein" describes the composition of the viral ENVELOPE (when present), not the protein-only capsid/coat, which contains no lipid component.

## answer_c
Capsomers

## explanation_c
Correct. The viral coat (capsid) is composed of capsomers -- the repeating protein subunits that self-assemble to form the capsid structure surrounding the viral genome. This is the same capsomere fact already established as a live concept in this lane's prior blocks, now reused for this specific "viral coat composition" framing.

## answer_d
peptidoglycan

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Peptidoglycan is a bacterial cell-wall component, entirely unrelated to viral structure -- viruses have no peptidoglycan of any kind.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q20

## title
What is the viroin of enveloped virus ?

## question
What is the viroin of enveloped virus ?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Nucleocapsid

## explanation_a
Incorrect (incomplete). "Nucleocapsid" alone describes the genome-plus-capsid complex, but for an ENVELOPED virus the complete virion also includes the surrounding envelope, which this option omits.

## answer_b
core + envelope

## explanation_b
Incorrect (imprecise terminology). "Core" is a vaguer, less precise term than "nucleocapsid" for describing the genome-plus-capsid complex; the module's credited answer uses the more specific and standard term.

## answer_c
Coat + envelope

## explanation_c
Incorrect (imprecise terminology). "Coat" is likewise a less precise term than "nucleocapsid" for the genome-plus-capsid unit, so this option does not match the credited, more specific phrasing.

## answer_d
Nucleocapsid + envelope

## explanation_d
Correct. The complete virus particle (virion) of an enveloped virus is composed of the nucleocapsid (the viral genome plus its surrounding capsid) together with the envelope that surrounds the whole structure. "Nucleocapsid + envelope" is the precise, complete and correctly worded description of an enveloped virion.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q21

## title
Which of the following isn't a function of viral capsid?

## question
Which of the following isn't a function of viral capsid?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Attachment of virus to host cell receptors

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true capsid function). The capsid, or capsid proteins in some viruses, can mediate attachment to specific host-cell receptors, a genuine capsid function, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Protection of viral genome

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true capsid function). Protecting the viral genome from the environment and host defenses is a core, defining capsid function.

## answer_c
Antigenicity of virus

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true capsid function). The capsid contributes significantly to a virus's antigenicity, since capsid proteins are frequently the target of host antibody responses -- a genuine capsid function.

## answer_d
Sensitivity to 70% alcohol

## explanation_d
Correct. Sensitivity to 70% alcohol is NOT a function of the viral capsid -- alcohol sensitivity is instead a property associated with the lipid ENVELOPE (which alcohol disrupts), and non-enveloped viruses (protected only by a capsid, no envelope) are notably more resistant to alcohol-based disinfection than enveloped ones. This makes alcohol sensitivity the one listed option that is not a true capsid function.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q22

## title
Which of the following isn't true about viral envelope ?

## question
Which of the following isn't true about viral envelope ?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
It is lipoprotein in nature

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true statement about the envelope). The viral envelope is genuinely lipoprotein in nature, combining a lipid bilayer with embedded viral glycoproteins.

## answer_b
lipid part is acquired from host cell nuclear or cell membrane during budding

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true statement about the envelope). The envelope's lipid component is indeed acquired from the host cell's nuclear or plasma membrane during the budding process, as the virus exits the cell.

## answer_c
protein part is coded by host genome

## explanation_c
Correct. It is NOT true that the envelope's protein part is coded by the host genome -- the envelope glycoproteins are coded by the VIRAL genome, even though the surrounding lipid bilayer itself is host-derived. This is the deliberate false statement the question asks you to identify: mixing up which envelope component (lipid vs protein) comes from which genome (host vs virus) is the classic trap here.

## answer_d
sensitive to detergents

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true statement about the envelope). The viral envelope is genuinely sensitive to detergents, which disrupt its lipid bilayer -- consistent with why enveloped viruses are generally easier to inactivate with soap/detergent than non-enveloped ones.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q23

## title
Which of the following viruses produce "Negri" inclusion bodies?

## question
Which of the following viruses produce "Negri" inclusion bodies?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Measles virus

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Measles virus is associated with its own distinctive cellular changes (e.g. multinucleated giant cells/Warthin-Finkeldey cells), not with Negri bodies specifically.

## answer_b
Herpes viruses

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Herpes viruses produce their own characteristic intranuclear (Cowdry type A) inclusion bodies, a different named finding from Negri bodies.

## answer_c
Rabies virus

## explanation_c
Correct. Rabies virus is specifically associated with producing "Negri" inclusion bodies -- eosinophilic cytoplasmic inclusions found in infected neurons, classically in the hippocampus, and historically a key diagnostic finding for rabies on light microscopy of brain tissue. Their absence on a brain biopsy does not rule out rabies, since not every infected neuron necessarily contains a visible inclusion, but their presence is highly specific.

## answer_d
Pox virus

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Pox viruses are associated with their own named inclusion bodies (Guarnieri bodies), a different eponymous finding from Negri bodies.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q24

## title
inclusion bodies can be all of the following except:

## question
inclusion bodies can be all of the following except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Are aggregates of virions

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true property of inclusion bodies). Inclusion bodies can genuinely be aggregates of virions, so this is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
aggregates oqf viral proteins

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true property of inclusion bodies). Inclusion bodies can also be aggregates of viral proteins alone (without complete virions), a genuine form these structures can take.

## answer_c
Can only be detected using EM

## explanation_c
Correct. Inclusion bodies are NOT detectable only by electron microscopy (EM) -- many classic inclusion bodies (such as the Negri bodies of rabies) are visible by routine LIGHT microscopy with appropriate staining, making "can only be detected using EM" a false, overly restrictive statement and thus the exception the question asks for. Relying on this false restriction could lead a laboratory to under-invest in routine light-microscopic screening for inclusion-body-forming viral infections.

## answer_d
Degenerative changes in Virally infected cell

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true property of inclusion bodies). Inclusion bodies can also represent degenerative changes within a virally infected cell, another genuine form they can take.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q25

## title
Viral growth on tissue culture can be recognized by:

## question
Viral growth on tissue culture can be recognized by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Cell lysis

## explanation_a
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial method). Cell lysis (cytopathic effect) is a genuine way to recognize viral growth, but it is only one of several valid methods, so it alone is not the most complete answer.

## answer_b
Interference phenomenon

## explanation_b
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial method). The interference phenomenon (a non-cytopathic virus blocking a cytopathic virus's CPE) is another genuine detection method, but again only one piece of the complete picture.

## answer_c
Haemadsorption

## explanation_c
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial method). Hemadsorption (infected cells adsorbing RBCs) is a third genuine detection method, but likewise incomplete on its own.

## answer_d
All of the above

## explanation_d
Correct. Cell lysis, the interference phenomenon, and hemadsorption are ALL valid, complementary ways to recognize viral growth on tissue culture -- since each is independently true, "all of the above" is the most complete and correct answer, tying together the detection methods covered individually elsewhere in this module. A laboratory technician monitoring a tissue culture for viral growth would typically watch for any one of these three signs, since not every virus produces all three equally well.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q26

## title
Mechanism of cell transformation by oncogenic viruses include all of the following except:

## question
Mechanism of cell transformation by oncogenic viruses include all of the following except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Insertion of viral oncogene

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true transformation mechanism). Insertion of a viral oncogene into the host genome is a genuine, well-established mechanism of oncogenic transformation, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Mutation of regulatory gene

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true transformation mechanism). Mutation of a host regulatory (cell-cycle-control) gene is another genuine transformation mechanism.

## answer_c
Production of viral protein that inactivates cell suppressor gene

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true transformation mechanism). Production of a viral protein that inactivates a cell tumor-suppressor gene (such as p53 or Rb inactivation by certain viral oncoproteins) is a well-documented transformation mechanism.

## answer_d
production of viral coded toxin

## explanation_d
Correct. Production of a viral-coded toxin is NOT a mechanism of oncogenic cell transformation -- transformation works through genetic/molecular manipulation of cell-cycle control (oncogene insertion, regulatory gene mutation, suppressor-gene inactivation), not through direct cytotoxic damage from a toxin, which is a mechanism associated instead with certain toxin-producing bacteria. Confusing bacterial and viral pathogenic strategies in this way is precisely the kind of cross-category error this question is designed to catch.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q27

## title
Virus can persist inside human body even after recovery due to ?

## question
Virus can persist inside human body even after recovery due to ?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Chronic infection

## explanation_a
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial mechanism). Chronic infection (ongoing, low-level productive viral replication) is one genuine way a virus can persist after apparent recovery, but it is not the only one.

## answer_b
Latent infection

## explanation_b
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial mechanism). Latent infection (occult, non-productive persistence with potential reactivation) is another genuine persistence mechanism, but again only one piece of the full picture.

## answer_c
slow infection

## explanation_c
Incorrect on its own (a true but partial mechanism). Slow infection (long silent incubation before progressive disease) is a third genuine persistence mechanism.

## answer_d
all of the above

## explanation_d
Correct. Chronic, latent and slow infection are all genuine, distinct mechanisms by which a virus can persist in the human body even after apparent recovery from acute illness -- since each is independently valid, "all of the above" is the most complete and correct answer. A clinician evaluating a patient with recurrent or delayed disease after an apparently resolved viral illness should consider all three persistence mechanisms as possible explanations.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q28

## title
Mechanism of "Cell tropism" include all of the following except

## question
Mechanism of "Cell tropism" include all of the following except

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
presence of Specific virus receptor on the cell

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true tropism determinant). Presence of a specific receptor the virus can bind is a core, genuine determinant of cell tropism, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Presence of proteolytic enzyme required by the virus

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true tropism determinant). Availability of a proteolytic enzyme the virus needs (e.g. for envelope protein cleavage/activation) is another genuine tropism determinant.

## answer_c
presence favourable Temperature & pH of the cell

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true tropism determinant). Favorable temperature and pH conditions in a given cell/tissue is a genuine environmental determinant of whether a virus can successfully replicate there.

## answer_d
Absence of immunity against virus in those sites

## explanation_d
Correct. Absence of immunity against the virus in a particular site is NOT a mechanism of cell tropism -- tropism is fundamentally about whether the virus CAN infect a given cell type at all (via receptor compatibility, required enzymes, and favorable conditions), a property independent of whether the local immune system happens to be suppressing the virus there. This is the deliberate distractor mixing up tropism (infectivity potential) with immune evasion (a separate biological concept).

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q29

## title
Entry and penetration of virus through host cell membrane occurs by:

## question
Entry and penetration of virus through host cell membrane occurs by:

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Interaction between the virus & specific host cell receptors

## explanation_a
Incorrect (describes attachment, not entry). Interaction between the virus and specific host receptors describes the ATTACHMENT step, which precedes and enables entry, but is not itself the entry/penetration mechanism the question asks about.

## answer_b
Endocytosis or fusion of the virus envelope with host cell membrane

## explanation_b
Correct. Entry and penetration of the virus through the host cell membrane occurs by endocytosis (the cell engulfing the virus) or by fusion of the viral envelope with the host cell membrane (for enveloped viruses) -- these are the two recognized mechanisms by which a virus actually crosses into the host cell after attachment has already occurred. Which of the two a given virus uses depends largely on whether it is enveloped (favoring fusion) or non-enveloped (relying more heavily on endocytosis).

## answer_c
Budding

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Budding describes how a newly formed enveloped virus EXITS the host cell (a release mechanism), the opposite direction of movement from entry/penetration.

## answer_d
Exocytosis

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Exocytosis similarly describes an outward-release process, not viral entry into the cell.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q30

## title
The viral envelope is primarily derived from which of the following host cell structures?

## question
The viral envelope is primarily derived from which of the following host cell structures?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Host cell DNA

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Host cell DNA is genetic material, not a membranous structure, and is not the source of the viral envelope's lipid bilayer.

## answer_b
Host cell plasma membrane

## explanation_b
Correct. The viral envelope is primarily derived from the host cell's plasma membrane (or, for some viruses, the nuclear membrane), acquired as the virus buds through that membrane on its way out of the cell -- this is the same host-membrane-derivation fact tested from a different angle in this cluster's companion question on envelope statements. This host-membrane origin is also why an enveloped virus's antigenic surface can sometimes incorporate host-cell proteins alongside its own virus-coded glycoproteins.

## answer_c
Host cell ribosomes

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Host cell ribosomes are involved in protein synthesis, not in forming the lipid-based viral envelope.

## answer_d
Host cell lysosomes

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Host cell lysosomes are membrane-bound digestive organelles, not the structure from which the viral envelope's lipid bilayer is typically derived during budding.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q31

## title
Which viral component is crucial for the integration of retroviral DNA into the host genome?

## question
Which viral component is crucial for the integration of retroviral DNA into the host genome?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Reverse transcriptase

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Reverse transcriptase synthesizes the DNA copy from the retroviral RNA genome, an earlier step in the retroviral life cycle that precedes and enables integration, but it is not the enzyme that performs the integration itself.

## answer_b
Integrase

## explanation_b
Correct. Integrase is the retroviral enzyme crucial for integrating the reverse-transcribed viral DNA into the host cell genome, creating the stable "provirus" -- this is a distinct, later step from the DNA synthesis performed by reverse transcriptase, and is a validated target of antiretroviral integrase-inhibitor drugs. Without successful integration, the retroviral genome cannot establish the stable, heritable infection state that allows it to persist and be copied every time the host cell divides.

## answer_c
Protease

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Protease cleaves viral polyproteins into their functional individual proteins during virion maturation, a later step in the life cycle unrelated to genome integration.

## answer_d
RNA polymerase

## explanation_d
Incorrect. RNA polymerase is not the enzyme responsible for retroviral DNA integration; retroviruses instead rely on their own reverse transcriptase (to make DNA from RNA) and integrase (to insert that DNA into the host genome).

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q32

## title
Which of the following components is found in all viruses?

## question
Which of the following components is found in all viruses?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Mitochondria

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Mitochondria are host-cell organelles for aerobic respiration; viruses possess no organelles of their own and rely entirely on the host cell's machinery.

## answer_b
Ribosomes

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Ribosomes are likewise a host-cell (or bacterial) structure for protein synthesis; viruses have no ribosomes of their own and must hijack the host's ribosomes to translate viral proteins.

## answer_c
Nucleo-capsid

## explanation_c
Correct. The nucleocapsid (the viral genome packaged within its capsid) is the structural component found in ALL viruses -- whether or not an envelope is also present, every virus has, at minimum, a genome enclosed in a capsid, forming the nucleocapsid. This is precisely why viruses are sometimes described as "obligate intracellular parasites" that borrow the host's organelles and ribosomes rather than carrying any of their own.

## answer_d
Cell wall

## explanation_d
Incorrect. A cell wall is a structure of bacteria, fungi and plants; viruses have no cell wall of any kind.
