# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q33

## title
Systemic viral infection is characterized by all the following except:

## question
Systemic viral infection is characterized by all the following except:

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Usually produces long lasting immunity

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true feature of systemic infection). Systemic viral infections often do produce long-lasting immunity after recovery, a genuine feature, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Associated with viremia

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true feature of systemic infection). Viremia (virus circulating in the bloodstream) is a defining feature of systemic infection, since it is how the virus reaches distant organs.

## answer_c
affects Multibole organs

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true feature of systemic infection). Affecting multiple organs is a genuine, defining feature of systemic infection, distinguishing it from a localized infection confined to the entry site.

## answer_d
Has a short incubation period (IP)

## explanation_d
Correct. A systemic viral infection does NOT typically have a short incubation period -- because the virus must first replicate locally, enter the bloodstream, and then spread to distant organs before causing disease, systemic infections generally have a LONGER incubation period than localized infections, making "short incubation period" the exception among these otherwise true features. This longer incubation window is clinically important, since it explains the delay often seen between exposure to a systemically spreading virus and the eventual appearance of symptoms.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q34

## title
Which of the following isn't true about localized viral infection ?

## question
Which of the following isn't true about localized viral infection ?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Short incubation period

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true feature of localized infection). A short incubation period is a genuine feature of localized infection, since the virus causes disease quickly at its entry site without needing to spread systemically first.

## answer_b
Multiplication at entry site

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true feature of localized infection). Multiplication at the entry site (rather than spreading to distant organs) is the defining feature of localized infection.

## answer_c
No long lasting immunity

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true feature of localized infection). Localized infections often do not produce the same degree of long-lasting immunity as systemic infections, a genuine contrasting feature.

## answer_d
Severe viremia

## explanation_d
Correct. It is NOT true that localized viral infection involves severe viremia -- by definition, a localized infection stays confined to and replicates at its entry site without significant spread into the bloodstream, so severe viremia (a hallmark instead of SYSTEMIC infection) is the false statement here, and thus the exception the question asks you to identify. This distinction matters clinically, since a localized infection is generally easier to contain and treat locally than one that has already reached the bloodstream.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q35

## title
All of the following is true regarding -ve sense ssRNA virus except

## question
All of the following is true regarding -ve sense ssRNA virus except

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
It must be transcribed by RNA polymerase

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true statement). A negative-sense ssRNA genome genuinely must be transcribed by an RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (carried within the virion) before it can be translated, so this is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
It cann't act as mRNA

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true statement). A -ve sense ssRNA genome genuinely cannot act as mRNA directly -- its sequence is complementary to mRNA, so it must first be transcribed into a +ve sense (mRNA-equivalent) copy before host ribosomes can translate it.

## answer_c
-ve sense ssRNA strand is infectious

## explanation_c
Correct. It is NOT true that a -ve sense ssRNA strand is infectious on its own -- unlike a +ve sense ssRNA genome (which CAN act directly as mRNA and IS infectious by itself), a -ve sense genome requires its own accompanying RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (packaged within the virion) to first make a usable, translatable copy; the naked genome alone, without this polymerase, cannot initiate infection. This is the false statement the question asks you to identify.

## answer_d
genome is Single Stranded.

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true statement). By definition, a -ve sense ssRNA virus genome is single-stranded, so this is excluded as the exception.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q36

## title
Which of the following is the correct sequence of virus replication cycle?

## question
Which of the following is the correct sequence of virus replication cycle?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Attachment penetration, replication, assembly, uncoating, release.

## explanation_a
Incorrect. This sequence places replication and assembly before uncoating, which is biologically impossible -- the genome must first be freed from its capsid (uncoating) before it can be replicated or before new virions can be assembled.

## answer_b
Attachment penetration, replication, uncoating, assembly, release

## explanation_b
Incorrect. This sequence places uncoating after replication, again impossible -- the genome cannot be replicated while still enclosed in an intact capsid; uncoating must occur first.

## answer_c
Attachment, penetration, uncoating, replication, assembly, release

## explanation_c
Correct. The correct sequence of the virus replication cycle is: attachment (to the host cell), penetration (entry into the cell), uncoating (release of the genome from the capsid), replication (of the genome and synthesis of viral components), assembly (of new virions), and release (from the host cell). This logical order reflects that each step depends on the one before it -- the genome must be freed (uncoated) before it can be copied (replicated), and components must exist before they can be put together (assembled).

## answer_d
Attachment, uncoating, penetration, assembly, replication, release

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This sequence places uncoating before penetration and assembly before replication, both biologically impossible -- uncoating occurs only after the virus has entered the cell (penetration), and assembly requires replicated components to already exist.

## answer_e
Uncoating, attachment, penetration, replication, assembly, release

## explanation_e
Incorrect. Beginning with "uncoating" before "attachment" is impossible -- a virus cannot uncoat its genome before it has even attached to and entered a host cell.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q37

## title
All of the following contribute in attachment of virus to the host cell except

## question
All of the following contribute in attachment of virus to the host cell except

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Lysozymes

## explanation_a
Correct. Lysozymes are host antimicrobial enzymes that degrade bacterial peptidoglycan; since viruses have no peptidoglycan cell wall for lysozyme to act on, lysozymes play no role whatsoever in viral attachment, making this the exception among the four options. This distractor is a common trap precisely because lysozyme is a well-known antimicrobial enzyme in other contexts, tempting a test-taker to assume it must play some antiviral role here too.

## answer_b
Capsid

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true contributor). The capsid (or specific capsid proteins, in non-enveloped viruses) can mediate attachment to host cells, a genuine contributing factor.

## answer_c
Glycoproteins on envelope

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true contributor). Glycoproteins on the viral envelope are classic mediators of attachment for enveloped viruses, binding specific host-cell receptors.

## answer_d
Specefic cell receptors

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true contributor). Specific host-cell receptors are the binding partners viral surface proteins (capsid or envelope glycoproteins) attach to -- a genuine and essential contributor to the attachment process.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q38

## title
Which of the following is not among the indications of reporting sharp injury incidents in healthcare?

## question
Which of the following is not among the indications of reporting sharp injury incidents in healthcare?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Administeration of post exposure prophylaxis

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true indication). Enabling administration of post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP) is a genuine, essential reason to report a sharps injury promptly, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Allow follow up for the exposed HCP.

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true indication). Allowing follow-up testing/monitoring for the exposed healthcare provider is a genuine reason for reporting.

## answer_c
assessment of Type and severity of exposure

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true indication). Assessment of the type and severity of exposure is a genuine, necessary step triggered by reporting, used to guide the appropriate response.

## answer_d
Preventing exposed HCP from work

## explanation_d
Correct. Preventing the exposed healthcare provider from working is NOT an indication for reporting a sharps injury -- reporting exists to trigger risk assessment, PEP, and follow-up, not to automatically remove the worker from duty. This is the deliberately incorrect option, since work restriction is not a standard, default consequence of reporting an exposure.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q39

## title
Which of the following procedures has the lowest risk of acquiring infection by the patient during healthcare delivery?

## question
Which of the following procedures has the lowest risk of acquiring infection by the patient during healthcare delivery?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Abdominal examination

## explanation_a
Correct. Abdominal examination is a non-invasive procedure that does not breach the patient's skin or mucosal barriers, making it the lowest-infection-risk activity among these options -- there is no direct route for organisms to enter the body during a simple physical exam. This is why infection-control resources are concentrated on invasive procedures rather than on routine, non-invasive patient examinations.

## answer_b
Intravenous injection

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Intravenous injection breaches the skin barrier, creating a route for organisms to enter the bloodstream, and carries meaningfully higher infection risk than a non-invasive exam.

## answer_c
Placement of urinary catheters

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Placement of urinary catheters breaches the urethral/mucosal barrier and is a well-recognized risk factor for catheter-associated urinary tract infection, carrying substantially higher risk than a non-invasive exam.

## answer_d
Surgical procedures

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Surgical procedures are the most invasive option listed, breaching multiple tissue layers and carrying the highest infection risk among these four options.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q40

## title
Protective environment in hospitals is recommended for

## question
Protective environment in hospitals is recommended for

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Allogenic Hematopoetic stem cell transplantation patient

## explanation_a
Correct. Protective environment precautions -- a positive-pressure room designed to shield a highly vulnerable patient from organisms in the surrounding air -- are specifically recommended for allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) patients, who are profoundly immunocompromised during the post-transplant period and at extreme risk from environmental fungal/bacterial exposure (notably Aspergillus). Providing this protection is a key part of post-transplant supportive care until the patient's own immune system has sufficiently engrafted and recovered.

## answer_b
Diabetic patient

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A diabetic patient, while at somewhat increased infection risk in general, does not have the level of profound immunocompromise that specifically warrants protective environment precautions.

## answer_c
Kidney transplant patients

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Kidney transplant patients are immunosuppressed but not to the same degree requiring protective environment precautions the way allogeneic HSCT patients are, in this module's framework.

## answer_d
patients who went through surgical operations

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Patients who underwent surgical operations are not, by that fact alone, candidates for protective environment precautions; post-surgical infection risk is managed through standard surgical-site infection prevention, not positive-pressure isolation.

## answer_e
TB patients

## explanation_e
Incorrect. TB patients require AIRBORNE precautions (negative pressure), the opposite pressure direction from protective environment precautions -- this option deliberately tests whether the two isolation categories and their patient populations are being confused.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q41

## title
What is the type of gloves that should be worn for cleaning contaminated instruments, handling waste, cleaning contaminated surfaces?

## question
What is the type of gloves that should be worn for cleaning contaminated instruments, handling waste, cleaning contaminated surfaces?

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Surgical gloves.

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Surgical gloves are sterile and designed for direct patient procedures requiring a sterile field, not for the puncture/chemical-resistance needs of cleaning contaminated instruments or waste.

## answer_b
Clean latex gloves

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Clean (non-sterile) latex gloves are suited to lower-risk clinical tasks like phlebotomy, but lack the durability needed for handling contaminated instruments or waste, which can involve sharp edges or harsh cleaning chemicals.

## answer_c
Heavy duty gloves.

## explanation_c
Correct. Heavy-duty gloves are the appropriate PPE for cleaning contaminated instruments, handling waste, and cleaning contaminated surfaces, since these tasks require greater puncture and chemical resistance than routine clinical gloves provide. Using a lighter-duty glove for these tasks would risk both chemical exposure and accidental puncture from sharp instrument edges during cleaning.

## answer_d
Transparent gloves

## explanation_d
Incorrect. "Transparent gloves" is not a standard PPE category matched to this task; the correct answer specifically calls for heavy-duty gloves due to their durability.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q42

## title
Which of the following isn't included in aseptic techniques ?

## question
Which of the following isn't included in aseptic techniques ?

## vignette

## correct_answer
E

## answer_a
Hand hygiene with antiseptic soap

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true aseptic-technique component). Hand hygiene with antiseptic soap is a core component of aseptic technique, reducing organism transfer to the sterile field, so it is excluded as the exception.

## answer_b
Patient skin antisepsis

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true aseptic-technique component). Patient skin antisepsis before a procedure is a genuine aseptic-technique component, reducing organism load at the procedure site.

## answer_c
No touch technique

## explanation_c
Incorrect (a true aseptic-technique component). No-touch technique (avoiding contamination of sterile equipment/surfaces by touch) is a genuine aseptic-technique component.

## answer_d
Wearing sterile gloves

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true aseptic-technique component). Wearing sterile gloves is a core, defining component of maintaining a sterile field during a procedure.

## answer_e
Wearing high efficiency mask

## explanation_e
Correct. Wearing a high-efficiency (N95) mask is NOT a component of aseptic technique -- that mask type belongs to airborne-precaution PPE, protecting the WEARER from inhaling infectious airborne particles, a fundamentally different infection-control goal from maintaining a sterile field to protect the PATIENT during a procedure. Conflating the two categories could lead a team to either omit a needed respiratory mask during an airborne-risk procedure or to wrongly assume a mask alone maintains a sterile field.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q43

## title
Which of the following about endoscopes is true?

## question
Which of the following about endoscopes is true?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Comes in contact with sterile tissues.

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Endoscopes typically contact mucous membranes (e.g. GI or respiratory tract lining), not normally sterile tissue -- this is precisely why they are classified as semi-critical rather than critical items.

## answer_b
Belong to high risk items

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Endoscopes are classified as semi-critical items, not "high risk" (critical) items -- critical items are those contacting sterile tissue or the bloodstream, a different, higher-risk category.

## answer_c
Belong to same category of vascular catheter and intrauterine devices.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Vascular catheters and intrauterine devices are critical items (entering sterile tissue or the bloodstream, requiring full sterilization); endoscopes belong to a different, semi-critical category with a lower reprocessing requirement.

## answer_d
Are disinfected after cleaning

## explanation_d
Correct. Endoscopes are correctly disinfected (high-level disinfection) after cleaning, reflecting their semi-critical classification -- they contact mucous membranes but not normally sterile tissue, so full sterilization (required for critical items) is not the standard requirement. Over-sterilizing a semi-critical item like an endoscope would be an unnecessary use of resources, while under-processing it below high-level disinfection would risk transmitting infection between patients.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q44

## title
Air borne isolation precautions doesn't include which of the following

## question
Air borne isolation precautions doesn't include which of the following

## vignette

## correct_answer
C

## answer_a
Vaccination for HCW

## explanation_a
Incorrect (a true component, where applicable). Vaccination for the healthcare worker, where a vaccine exists for the airborne pathogen in question (e.g. measles), is a genuine component of airborne-precaution practice.

## answer_b
High efficiency mask (N95) for Health care worker

## explanation_b
Incorrect (a true component). A high-efficiency (N95) mask for the healthcare worker is a core, defining PPE component of airborne precautions.

## answer_c
Positive air flow ventilation

## explanation_c
Correct. Airborne isolation precautions do NOT include positive (+ve) air flow ventilation -- they specifically require NEGATIVE-pressure ventilation, which keeps the patient's infectious airborne particles contained within the room rather than allowing them to escape. Positive-pressure ventilation is instead the requirement for protective environment precautions, the opposite isolation category, making this the exception among the listed options.

## answer_d
Surgical mask for patient

## explanation_d
Incorrect (a true component, in some protocols). A surgical mask on the patient (source control) can be part of airborne-precaution practice, particularly during transport, so this is a genuine (if secondary) component rather than the exception.

## answer_e
Isolation in single room

## explanation_e
Incorrect (a true component). Isolation in a single room is a core, defining component of airborne precautions.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q45

## title
Which type of isolation should be applied to a person suffering from lung tuberculosis?

## question
Which type of isolation should be applied to a person suffering from lung tuberculosis?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Airborne isolation.

## explanation_a
Correct. Pulmonary (lung) tuberculosis requires airborne isolation, since Mycobacterium tuberculosis is spread via small, long-suspended airborne particles that require negative-pressure isolation and N95 respiratory protection for staff, rather than the larger droplets or direct-contact routes covered by other isolation categories. This is the same airborne-precaution category applied to measles, another disease spread by small, long-suspended particles rather than larger droplets.

## answer_b
Droplet isolation.

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Droplet isolation is used for pathogens spread by larger respiratory droplets that do not remain suspended in air for long distances (e.g. influenza, pertussis) -- a different transmission profile from TB.

## answer_c
Standard precautions.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Standard precautions alone are insufficient for an airborne pathogen like pulmonary TB; a dedicated airborne isolation category with negative pressure and N95 protection is specifically required.

## answer_d
Contact isolation

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Contact isolation is used for pathogens spread by direct or indirect physical contact (e.g. multidrug-resistant organisms on skin/surfaces), not for an airborne respiratory pathogen like TB.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q46

## title
What is the appropriate post exposure prophylaxis for an unvaccinated health care workers exposed to hepatitis B virus?

## question
What is the appropriate post exposure prophylaxis for an unvaccinated health care workers exposed to hepatitis B virus?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Initiate 1st dose of vaccine

## explanation_a
Incorrect on its own (a true but incomplete component). Starting the hepatitis B vaccine series is a genuine part of correct PEP, but the vaccine alone is not sufficient, since it provides only delayed, active immunity -- HBIg is also needed to cover the gap.

## answer_b
Give 1 dose of HB Ig

## explanation_b
Incorrect on its own (a true but incomplete component). Giving HBIg alone provides immediate passive protection, but without also starting the vaccine series, the worker is left without durable, active immunity going forward.

## answer_c
Give antiviral chemotherapy

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Antiviral chemotherapy is not the standard post-exposure prophylaxis approach for hepatitis B exposure in an unvaccinated healthcare worker; the standard approach combines vaccine and HBIg instead.

## answer_d
Initiate 1 dose of vaccine + 1 dose of HB Ig

## explanation_d
Correct. For an unvaccinated healthcare worker exposed to HBV, the appropriate post-exposure prophylaxis is to initiate 1 dose of hepatitis B vaccine PLUS 1 dose of hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIg) together -- combining immediate passive protection (HBIg) with the start of active, long-term immunization (vaccine series), which neither component alone can provide. Delaying either component, or omitting one, leaves the exposed worker with a meaningful window of vulnerability to developing hepatitis B infection.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q47

## title
Proper disposal of used needles include which of the following?

## question
Proper disposal of used needles include which of the following?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Always breaking or bending the needle before disposal

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Breaking or bending a needle before disposal is an unsafe practice that increases the risk of an accidental needlestick injury during the manipulation, and is specifically discouraged.

## answer_b
Carefully removing needle from syringe

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Manually removing the needle from the syringe before disposal introduces an unnecessary extra manipulation step that increases needlestick risk, and is not the recommended safe-disposal practice.

## answer_c
Carefully recapping needle

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Recapping a needle -- even "carefully" -- is a classic, well-documented cause of accidental needlestick injury (especially two-handed recapping) and is specifically discouraged in safe sharps practice.

## answer_d
Placing needle & attached syringe in puncture resistant container

## explanation_d
Correct. Proper disposal of a used needle means placing the needle together with its attached syringe directly into a puncture-resistant sharps container, without any additional manipulation (no recapping, no bending, no separating the needle from the syringe) that would increase the risk of an accidental needlestick injury. Following this simple rule consistently is one of the most effective and low-cost ways to prevent occupational bloodborne pathogen exposure among healthcare workers.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q48

## title
Which PPE should be worn during starting IV lines or phlebotomy?

## question
Which PPE should be worn during starting IV lines or phlebotomy?

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Sterile gown

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A sterile gown is reserved for procedures requiring a fully sterile field (e.g. surgery), which is a higher barrier requirement than routine IV access or phlebotomy needs.

## answer_b
High efficiency mask

## explanation_b
Incorrect. A high-efficiency (N95) mask is specific to airborne-precaution PPE, protecting against inhaled airborne particles -- irrelevant to the blood-exposure risk of IV line placement or phlebotomy.

## answer_c
Surgical gloves.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Surgical gloves are sterile and intended for procedures requiring a sterile field; routine venipuncture does not require this higher, sterile-technique standard.

## answer_d
Clean latex gloves

## explanation_d
Correct. Clean (non-sterile) latex gloves are the appropriate PPE for starting IV lines or phlebotomy -- these are routine, blood-contact procedures that need barrier protection against blood exposure, but do not require a fully sterile field, making clean gloves the proportionate PPE choice. Reserving sterile gloves and gowns for procedures that truly require a sterile field helps conserve these more resource-intensive supplies for when they are actually needed.

## answer_e
Sterile gown

## explanation_e
Incorrect (this option duplicates another listed choice, restating the sterile-gown distractor). As with option A, a sterile gown is not the appropriate PPE level for a routine procedure like IV access or phlebotomy.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q49

## title
Which of the following best describes the term "hospital acquired infection"?

## question
Which of the following best describes the term "hospital acquired infection"?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
An infection acquired in the community

## explanation_a
Incorrect. "Acquired in the community" describes a community-acquired infection, the opposite category from a hospital-acquired (nosocomial) infection, which by definition originates in the healthcare setting.

## answer_b
An infection acquired in a healthcare setting >48 hours post admission and was not incubating before

## explanation_b
Correct. Hospital-acquired infection is best defined as one acquired in a healthcare setting more than 48 hours after admission, and which was NOT already incubating (present but not yet symptomatic) at the time of admission. Both conditions -- the 48-hour threshold AND the "not incubating before" clause -- are required together for the correct definition, since most community-acquired infections have an incubation period shorter than 48 hours.

## answer_c
An infection acquired in a healthcare setting >24 hours post admission and was not incubating before

## explanation_c
Incorrect. This option uses the wrong time threshold (24 hours instead of the correct 48 hours) -- 24 hours is too short a window to reliably distinguish a hospital-acquired infection from one that was already incubating on admission.

## answer_d
An infection acquired in a healthcare setting 48 hours post admission and was incubating before

## explanation_d
Incorrect. This option incorrectly states the infection "was incubating before" admission, which is the OPPOSITE of the correct definition -- an infection already incubating before admission is, by definition, NOT hospital-acquired, regardless of when symptoms appear.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q50

## title
For which of the following diseases would you implement airborne precautions?

## question
For which of the following diseases would you implement airborne precautions?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Influenza

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Influenza is managed with DROPLET precautions (larger respiratory droplets, not small long-suspended airborne particles), not airborne precautions.

## answer_b
Measles

## explanation_b
Correct. Measles is one of the classic diseases requiring airborne precautions, since the measles virus spreads via small, long-suspended airborne particles capable of traveling and remaining infectious over greater distances and time than droplet-transmitted pathogens -- the same isolation category applied to pulmonary tuberculosis elsewhere in this module. Because measles is highly contagious via this airborne route, a suspected case should trigger prompt isolation even before laboratory confirmation is available.

## answer_c
Staphylococcus aureus

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Staphylococcus aureus infections (including MRSA) are typically managed with CONTACT precautions (direct/indirect physical contact transmission), not airborne precautions.

## answer_d
Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV)

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) is managed with CONTACT and/or DROPLET precautions, not airborne precautions, since it is not transmitted by small, long-suspended airborne particles the way measles or TB are.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q51

## title
Which of the following precautions should be taken for a patient with a highly immunocompromised condition

## question
Which of the following precautions should be taken for a patient with a highly immunocompromised condition

## vignette

## correct_answer
D

## answer_a
Contact precautions

## explanation_a
Incorrect. Contact precautions are designed to prevent transmission of an organism FROM the patient to others via contact, the opposite direction of protection needed for a highly immunocompromised patient who instead needs protecting FROM the environment.

## answer_b
Droplet precautions

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Droplet precautions likewise protect others from a patient's respiratory droplets, not the patient from environmental organisms.

## answer_c
Airborne precautions

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Airborne precautions (negative pressure) also protect others from the patient's airborne particles -- the opposite direction of protection and the opposite pressure requirement from what a highly immunocompromised patient needs.

## answer_d
Protective environment precautions

## explanation_d
Correct. Protective environment precautions -- using a POSITIVE-pressure room -- are specifically designed to shield a highly immunocompromised patient from potentially contaminated air/organisms in the surrounding environment, the opposite direction of protection (and opposite pressure) from contact, droplet, and airborne precautions, which all protect others FROM the patient rather than the patient from the environment. Placing a highly immunocompromised patient in the wrong precaution category (one aimed at protecting others rather than protecting the patient) would leave them dangerously exposed to ordinary environmental organisms.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q52

## title
Which of the following personal protective equipment (PPE) is essential for airborne precautions?

## question
Which of the following personal protective equipment (PPE) is essential for airborne precautions?

## vignette

## correct_answer
B

## answer_a
Sterile gown

## explanation_a
Incorrect. A sterile gown is relevant to sterile-field procedures, not to the specific respiratory-protection need of airborne precautions.

## answer_b
High efficiency mask

## explanation_b
Correct. A high-efficiency (N95) mask is the essential PPE for airborne precautions, filtering the small, long-suspended airborne particles (as with TB or measles) well enough to protect the wearer's respiratory tract -- this is the same requirement tested from a different angle in this cluster's companion question on what airborne precautions include. Without this specific respiratory protection, a healthcare worker caring for a patient on airborne precautions would be at meaningful risk of inhaling the same small, long-suspended infectious particles as the patient.

## answer_c
Surgical gloves.

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Surgical gloves address a barrier need for hand contact with blood/body fluids or a sterile field, not the airborne respiratory-protection need that defines this precaution category.

## answer_d
Clean latex gloves

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Clean latex gloves, similarly, address hand-contact barrier needs, not airborne respiratory protection.

## answer_e
Sterile gown

## explanation_e
Incorrect (this option duplicates another listed choice, restating the sterile-gown distractor). As with option A, a sterile gown does not address the airborne respiratory-protection requirement.

# Item
## id
QST-ASU-INF-CH8910-Q53

## title
In which type of precaution should the patient's room have POSITIVE pressure ventilation?

## question
In which type of precaution should the patient's room have POSITIVE pressure ventilation?

## vignette

## correct_answer
A

## answer_a
Protective environment precautions

## explanation_a
Correct. Protective environment precautions specifically require a room with POSITIVE-pressure ventilation, which pushes room air outward whenever the door opens, preventing potentially contaminated corridor air from reaching a highly vulnerable patient inside -- the deliberate opposite pressure direction from airborne precautions. Mixing up these two pressure directions in practice would defeat the purpose of either isolation category entirely, either exposing a vulnerable patient to outside organisms or allowing an airborne pathogen to escape into the corridor.

## answer_b
Droplet precautions

## explanation_b
Incorrect. Droplet precautions do not have a specific positive- or negative-pressure ventilation requirement in the way protective environment or airborne precautions do; droplet transmission is managed primarily through masking and spatial separation, not room air pressure.

## answer_c
Airborne precautions

## explanation_c
Incorrect. Airborne precautions specifically require NEGATIVE-pressure ventilation (the opposite of positive pressure), to contain the patient's infectious airborne particles within the room rather than let them escape.

## answer_d
Contact precautions

## explanation_d
Incorrect. Contact precautions do not have a specific positive- or negative-pressure ventilation requirement; contact transmission is managed through PPE (gowns, gloves) and environmental cleaning, not room air pressure.
